Introduction

1.      Let X be the Poincaré half-plane

X = \{z ∈ ℂ | Im(z) > 0\}.

The group SL(2, ℝ) acts on X by homographic transformations

az+b z ↦  ─────. cz+d

If Γ is a subgroup of SL(2, ℤ) defined by congruence conditions, the Riemann surface X/Γ is the complement of a finite set of points ("at infinity") in a compact Riemann surface. It is therefore an algebraic curve. Its points are in bijective correspondence with the isomorphism classes of elliptic curves equipped with a "level structure" of a suitable kind. It is known from this interpretation that it admits as a field of definition a subfield of a cyclotomic field. In this article, we study the structure at infinity and the reduction modulo p of X/Γ.

Let us concentrate on the case in which Γ is the subgroup of SL(2, ℤ) formed by the matrices congruent modulo n to the identity matrix. Suppose that n ≥ 3, in which case Γ acts on X without a fixed point.

For z ∈ X, let E\textunderscore{}z denote the quotient of ℂ by the lattice ℤ + ℤz. The addition law on ℂ descends to the quotient and equips E\textunderscore{}z with the structure of an elliptic curve (= an abelian variety of dimension one). The elliptic curves E\textunderscore{}z and E\textunderscore{}z′ are isomorphic if and only if z and z′ are conjugate under SL(2, ℤ). The kernel E(z)\textunderscore{}n of multiplication by n on E(z) is the image of (1/n)(ℤ + ℤz); we shall denote by α(z) the isomorphism

E(z)\textunderscore{}n  ≃  (ℤ/nℤ)\textasciicircum{}2 : (a+bz)/n ↦ (a,b).


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0004-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0004-A01.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0005}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 5}{D018-P0005-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 5; collected-paper page 498; original folio 147.}\par\smallskip
For the pairs (E(z),α(z)) and (E(z′),α(z′)) to be isomorphic, it is necessary and sufficient that z and z′ be conjugate under Γ.

For every elliptic curve E over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p not dividing n, the group E\textunderscore{}n is equipped with a nondegenerate alternating form e\textunderscore{}n with values in the nth roots of unity in k. A level-n structure on E is an isomorphism α:E\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2. If α is a level-n structure, we shall denote by ζ(α) the primitive nth root of unity

ζ(α) = e\textunderscore{}n(α\textasciicircum{}−1((1,0)), α\textasciicircum{}−1((0,1))).

Put k = ℂ, and set ζ\textunderscore{}n = exp(2πi/n). A pair (E,α) is isomorphic to a pair (E(z),α(z)) (z ∈ X) if and only if

ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n

(or ζ\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}−1, according to the sign convention used in the definition of e\textunderscore{}n).

2.      The foregoing generalizes to families of elliptic curves parametrized by a scheme S. One finds that X/Γ represents the following functor F\textunderscore{}n on schemes over ℂ: F\textunderscore{}n(T) is the set of isomorphism classes of algebraic families, parametrized by T, of elliptic curves E equipped with a level-n structure such that ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n.

The definition of F\textunderscore{}n(T) remains meaningful when T is merely assumed to be a scheme over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n, 1/n]. Igusa proved in [11] that the resulting functor F\textunderscore{}n is representable by a scheme M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]. Igusa's method is as follows.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0005-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0005-A01.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0006}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 6}{D018-P0006-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 6; collected-paper page 499; original folio 148.}\par\smallskip
a) If M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] exists, then M\textunderscore{}nm\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] exists. If E is an elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field, multiplication by m induces an isomorphism of E\textunderscore{}nm/nE\textunderscore{}nm with E\textunderscore{}n; a level-nm structure α:E\textunderscore{}nm ≃ (ℤ/nm)\textasciicircum{}2 thereby defines, by reduction modulo n, a level-n structure. This carries over to an arbitrary base scheme and defines a morphism of functors F\textunderscore{}nm → F\textunderscore{}n. This morphism is relatively representable. It makes M\textunderscore{}nm\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] an unramified Galois covering, with group Ker(SL(2, ℤ/nm) → SL(2, ℤ/n)), of

M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}nm]

(⊗ denotes an extension of scalars).

b) If M\textunderscore{}nm\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] exists, M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] exists. One obtains M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/nm] by passing to the quotient.

c) M\textunderscore{}3\textasciicircum{}∘[1/3] and M\textunderscore{}4\textasciicircum{}∘[1/4] exist. Explicitly, one has

M\textunderscore{}3\textasciicircum{}∘[1/3] = Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}3,1/3,μ,(μ\textasciicircum{}3−1)\textasciicircum{}−1]),

with universal elliptic curve the plane cubic with equation

X\textasciicircum{}3 + Y\textasciicircum{}3 + Z\textasciicircum{}3 = 3μ XYZ.

The zero section is (1,−1,0), and the basis of the group of points of order 3 is ((−1,0,1), (−1,ζ\textunderscore{}3,0)).

At level 4, one has

M\textunderscore{}4\textasciicircum{}∘[1/4] = Spec(ℤ[i,1/2,σ,(σ(σ\textasciicircum{}4−1))\textasciicircum{}−1]),

where i = ζ\textunderscore{}4, with universal elliptic curve the plane cubic whose nonhomogeneous equation is

y\textasciicircum{}2 = x(x−1)(x−λ),  where

λ = (1/4)(σ + 1/σ)\textasciicircum{}2.

The zero section is the point at infinity, and the basis (r,s) of the group of points


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0006-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0006-A01.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0007}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 7}{D018-P0007-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 7; collected-paper page 500; original folio 149.}\par\smallskip
of order 4 is given by

r = (1/2(σ + 1/σ), i(σ²+1)(σ−1)²/(4·σ²))

s = (−i/2(σ − 1/σ) + 1, −(σ²−1)(σ+1)²/(4·σ²)).

The formulas above for M\textunderscore{}4\textasciicircum{}∘[1/4] are copied from Shioda [31]. Igusa used not M\textunderscore{}4\textasciicircum{}∘[1/4], but the moduli scheme of elliptic curves equipped with a structure intermediate between a level-4 structure and a level-2 structure.

d) M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] is obtained by gluing M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/3n] and M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/4n].

3.      The Riemann surface X/Γ is noncompact. Geometrically, this fact translates as follows: if E\textunderscore{}η is an elliptic curve equipped with a level-n structure over ℂ((T)), it may happen that the minimal model of E\textunderscore{}η over ℂ[[T]] has bad reduction. In that case, the special fiber E'\textunderscore{}o of the Néron model E' of E\textunderscore{}η over ℂ[[T]] is isomorphic to ℂ*×ℤ/kn for a suitable k. Let E\textunderscore{}o be the subgroup of E'\textunderscore{}o that is the union of the components of E'\textunderscore{}o whose order in π\textunderscore{}o(E'\textunderscore{}o) divides n. This subgroup is isomorphic to ℂ*×ℤ/n. It has the following advantage over E'\textunderscore{}o: after extending scalars from ℂ((T)) to ℂ((T\textasciicircum{}\{1/ℓ\})), the number of connected components of E'\textunderscore{}o is multiplied by ℓ, while E\textunderscore{}o remains unchanged.

By specialization, the alternating form e\textunderscore{}n defines an alternating form, again denoted e\textunderscore{}n, on (E\textunderscore{}o)\textunderscore{}n. By specialization, the level-n structure α defines

α\textunderscore{}o : (E\textunderscore{}o)\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)²,

and

ζ(α\textunderscore{}o) = e\textunderscore{}n(α\textasciicircum{}−1((1,0)), α\textasciicircum{}−1((0,1))) = ζ\textunderscore{}n.

This discussion suggests that the points at infinity of X/Γ correspond


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0007-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0007-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0008}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 8}{D018-P0008-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 8; collected-paper page 501; original folio 150.}\par\smallskip
to the isomorphism classes of systems (E\textunderscore{}o,e\textunderscore{}n,α\textunderscore{}n) as above. This is indeed the case.

In the text, we refine this modular interpretation of the set of points at infinity of X/Γ into a modular interpretation of the projective curve X/Γ̄, the compactification of X/Γ. Here is the principle.

For E\textunderscore{}η over ℂ((T)) as above, the special fiber Ē'\textunderscore{}o of the minimal model Ē' of E\textunderscore{}η over ℂ[[T]] is a cycle of kn projective lines:

[cycle of kn projective lines: see authority asset P0008-A01]

Let Ē be obtained from Ē' by contracting to a point the irreducible components of Ē'\textunderscore{}o whose order does not divide n. The special fiber Ē\textunderscore{}o of Ē is a cycle of n projective lines meeting transversely, and is a compactification of E\textunderscore{}o (E\textunderscore{}o identifies with the smooth locus Ē\textunderscore{}o\textasciicircum{}reg of Ē\textunderscore{}o). By specialization, + and α define a group law on the smooth locus Ē\textunderscore{}o\textasciicircum{}reg of Ē\textunderscore{}o, an action of Ē\textunderscore{}o\textasciicircum{}reg on Ē\textunderscore{}o, and an isomorphism

α\textunderscore{}o : (Ē\textunderscore{}o\textasciicircum{}reg)\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)².

Let T be a scheme of finite type over ℂ. Inspired by the remarks above, we define a generalized elliptic curve over T, equipped with a level-n structure α such that ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n, as consisting of:

a) a proper flat morphism p : E → T;

b) a commutative group-scheme structure on the open subset E\textasciicircum{}reg of E where p is smooth; addition is assumed to extend to an action

+ : E\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}T E → E;


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0008-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0008-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0009}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 9}{D018-P0009-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 9; collected-paper page 502; original folio 151.}\par\smallskip
c) an isomorphism of group schemes

(E\textasciicircum{}reg)\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)²\textunderscore{}T.

The geometric fibers of p are assumed either to be elliptic curves equipped with a level-n structure such that ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n, or to be isomorphic to one of the systems (Ē\textunderscore{}o,α\textunderscore{}o) constructed above.

We prove that the curve X/Γ̄ represents the following functor F̄\textunderscore{}n on schemes of finite type over ℂ. F̄\textunderscore{}n(T) is the set of isomorphism classes of generalized elliptic curves E over T, equipped with a level-n structure α such that ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n.

To make the definition of generalized elliptic curves viable, we make intensive use of Grothendieck techniques (base-change theorems (EGA III) and duality ([10]) in coherent-sheaf cohomology). These techniques provide a systematic means of reducing relative statements concerning schemes over a base scheme S to the case in which S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field.

To prove that the functor F̄\textunderscore{}n is representable, we use the general criterion of M. Artin [3]. Verifying the hypotheses of this criterion presents the following delicate point. Datum b) in the definition of a generalized elliptic curve is a morphism of schemes over T

+ : E\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}T E → E,

whose source E\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}T E is not necessarily proper over T. This forces us to prove the effective prorepresentability of F\textunderscore{}n by an indirect route, since Grothendieck's existence theorem is not directly applicable.

Chapters II and III and §1 of IV contain the tools required to overcome these difficulties. Above, we worked over ℂ. In the text, we work over ℤ or ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n], as appropriate, and obtain a curve M\textunderscore{}n[1/n], projective and smooth over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n], solving a moduli problem, and such that X/Γ̄ is obtained from it by extension of scalars to ℂ.

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0009-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0009-A02.}
\end{flushright}

\hypertarget{D018-P0010}{}

4.      To speak of the "reduction mod p of X/Γ̄" presupposes the choice of a model M\textunderscore{}n of X/Γ̄ over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]. The definition of M\textunderscore{}n given in the text is equivalent to the following: M\textunderscore{}n is the normalization of the j-line (the projective line over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n], to which M\textunderscore{}n maps by the "modular invariant of the universal elliptic curve" map) in the function field of M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n]. Also let M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘ be the affine curve whose coordinate ring is the normalization of ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][j] in the coordinate ring of M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘. It is shown in the text that

M\textunderscore{}n[1/n] = M\textunderscore{}n ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]     and

M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] = M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n].

We prove the following results:

a) The modular curve M\textunderscore{}n is projective over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n], and smooth away from a finite set of points of M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘, namely the supersingular points whose characteristic divides n.

b) There exists a finite family of points f\textunderscore{}i of M\textunderscore{}n with values in ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] (= sections of the scheme morphism M\textunderscore{}n → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n])), such that

b1) the sections f\textunderscore{}i are disjoint (= the points f\textunderscore{}i are incongruent modulo every prime ideal of ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]);

b2) M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘ is the complement in M\textunderscore{}n of the union of the "sections at infinity" f\textunderscore{}i.

c) Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}} be a prime ideal of ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] dividing the prime factor p of n. We determine the set of irreducible components of the reduction of M\textunderscore{}n modulo \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}}. To describe the result, we suppose that n is the product of two relatively prime integers ≥ 3, n' and n''. Under this hypothesis, M\textunderscore{}n[1/n'] is the normalization in M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] of the scheme M\textunderscore{}\{n'\}[1/n'], for which we know a modular interpretation. The pullbacks to M\textunderscore{}\{n'\}\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n'] and M\textunderscore{}\{n''\}\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n''] of the


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0010-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0010-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0011}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 11}{D018-P0011-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 11; collected-paper page 504; original folio 153.}\par\smallskip
universal elliptic curves over M\textunderscore{}\{n'\}\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n'] and M\textunderscore{}\{n''\}\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n''] glue together and furnish a universal elliptic curve E over M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘. The level structure

α : E\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)²

over M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] extends to a morphism of group schemes over M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘,

α\textasciicircum{}−1 : (ℤ/n)² → E\textunderscore{}n.

The p-primary component of α\textasciicircum{}−1 is a morphism

β(p) : (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)² → E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}.

The supersingular points of characteristic p of M\textunderscore{}\ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}} (p|n) are those at which β(p) is zero. The irreducible components of the reduction of M\textunderscore{}n modulo \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}} are in bijective correspondence with the cyclic subgroups of order p\textasciicircum{}k of (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)² (the points of ℙ¹(ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)): at the nonsupersingular points of the component indexed by A ⊂ (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)², the kernel of β(p) is A. We prove that these irreducible components are unibranch, and that any two of them meet at every supersingular point.

The irreducible components of the reduction of M\textunderscore{}n modulo p had been determined by Pjateckii-Shapiro (see his article in this volume). Unfortunately, he works with PGL(2) rather than GL(2), so that his results are more tangled than ours.

Under the same hypothesis on n as above, one of us (P. Deligne) recently proved that these irreducible components are in fact smooth, and that M\textunderscore{}n is a regular scheme.

To study M\textunderscore{}n, the first step is to cover by open subsets having a modular interpretation the open subset M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h of M\textunderscore{}n obtained by removing the supersingular points whose characteristic divides n. This is achieved by studying the moduli scheme classifying elliptic curves equipped with an isomorphism


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0011-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0011-A02.}

\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0012}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 12}{D018-P0012-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 12; collected-paper page 505; original folio 154.}\par\smallskip
α : E\textunderscore{}n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n.

A modular interpretation makes it possible

a) to determine the set of points of M\textunderscore{}n over an algebraically closed field;

b) to prove the smoothness of M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]. It remains, and this is the most delicate point, to study the supersingular points.

5.      It is no doubt possible to study the structure of M\textunderscore{}n at infinity by rigid- analytic methods, using the theory of the Tate curve, without first giving the points at infinity a modular description. Such an approach may even be the only practicable one for studying the boundary of higher-dimensional moduli schemes. It has often served us as a heuristic guide (see below). However, to make it precise, it requires rigid-analytic preliminaries that we wished to avoid.

This explains why the Tate curve appears only in the final chapter, Chapter VII. We use it to deduce properties at finite distance of the M\textunderscore{}n, and of their reduction modulo p, from results on the Fourier-series expansions of modular forms. In particular, we prove the following results.

− The graded algebra of modular forms on X/Γ whose expansions at all cusps have coefficients in ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] is a finitely generated algebra over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n].

− If the prime ideal \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}} of ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] does not divide n, and if at one cusp the Fourier-series expansion of a modular form φ on X/Γ has coefficients in ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] and is \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}}-integral, then the expansion of φ at the other cusps has the same properties.

Fragmentary indications are also given for the case where \ensuremath{\mathfrak{p}} divides n.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0012-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0012-A02.}
\end{flushright}

\hypertarget{D018-P0013}{}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 13; collected-paper page 506; original folio 155.}\par\smallskip
6.      Our heuristic use of the Tate curve rests on the following principles.

a) With the aid of the Tate curve, one can guess or prove what happens in a neighborhood of infinity.

b) Outside the supersingular points, the situation is everywhere the same, and in particular the same as in a neighborhood of infinity.

To these principles is added the following one.

c) Geometrically, i.e. after extension of scalars (or of the residue field) to \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p, the situation is the same at the various supersingular points of characteristic p.

The local (formal) theory of M\textunderscore{}n in a neighborhood of the geometric points corresponding to an elliptic curve E over \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is governed by the p-divisible group

D\textunderscore{}p(E) = ⋃ E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}n\},

that is, essentially, by the completed formal group of E at the origin. This follows from the Serre–Tate theorem, according to which E and this p-divisible group have the same deformation theory. Principles b) and c) follow from it, since this p-divisible group depends, up to isomorphism, only on whether E is ordinary or supersingular.

7.      Let H be an indefinite quaternion algebra, of discriminant D, over ℚ, and let Δ be a congruence subgroup of the group of units of H. The group Δ is a discrete subgroup of SL(2,ℝ) with compact quotient. The complete curve X/Δ parametrizes abelian varieties A of dimension 2, equipped with a homomorphism from an order of H into End(A) ("false elliptic curves," in Serre's terminology). Our methods apply to the study of X/Δ and of its reduction modulo p, provided that p is prime to D.

The following remarks even make it possible to deduce, from theorems for M\textunderscore{}n, theorems on the local structure of X/Δ.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0013-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0013-A01.}
\end{flushright}

\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 14}{D018-P0014-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 14; collected-paper page 507; original folio 156.}\par\smallskip
d) Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} be an order of H, with \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ\textunderscore{}p ≃ GL(2,ℤ\textunderscore{}p), and let e be a nontrivial idempotent of \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ\textunderscore{}p. If A is an abelian scheme of dimension 2 over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p, equipped with μ : \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} → End(A), the completion \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ\textunderscore{}p acts on the p-divisible group D\textunderscore{}p(A) = ⋃ A\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}n\}, and

D\textunderscore{}p A) = e·D\textunderscore{}p(A) ⊕ (1−e)D\textunderscore{}p(A).

The abelian scheme A, equipped with μ, and the p-divisible group eD\textunderscore{}p(A), have the same deformation theory.

e) the p-divisible group eD\textunderscore{}p(A) is isomorphic to D\textunderscore{}p(E) for an elliptic curve E that is either ordinary or supersingular.

The reduction modulo p of X/Δ was studied by Morita. He also obtains results in the much more difficult case where p|D.

8.      In the literature on moduli schemes, the language of Weil's Foundations is sometimes used. According as one uses this language or Grothendieck's, emphasis is placed on one of the following two views of what a scheme X over a number field K is.

a) It is a scheme X, equipped with a morphism X → Spec(K). This is the view that predominates in our notation.

b) It is a scheme X̄ over the algebraic closure K̄ of K, which is defined over K. This view shows through in the proof technique that consists in reducing a statement concerning an S-scheme X to a statement concerning its geometric fibers X\textunderscore{}s̄, obtained by extension of scalars from S to an algebraically closed field k(s̄).

The "restriction of scalars à la Grothendieck" from K to ℚ takes on a very different appearance according to the view adopted.

a) To the K-scheme X one associates the ℚ-scheme defined by X and the composite morphism X → Spec(K) → Spec(ℚ). It is not distinguished from X in the notation. A scheme is first of all a ℚ-scheme; if it is a K-scheme, it is equipped with a ℚ-morphism X → Spec(K).

b) Let I be the set of embeddings of K into ℚ̄. For σ ∈ I, set


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0014-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0014-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0015}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 15}{D018-P0015-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 15; collected-paper page 508; original folio 157.}\par\smallskip
X̄\textunderscore{}σ = X ⊗\textunderscore{}\{K,σ\} ℚ̄. We have X ⊗\textunderscore{}ℚ ℚ̄ = ∑\textunderscore{}\{σ∈I\} X̄\textunderscore{}σ. The ℚ-scheme deduced, as in a), from a K-scheme appears as the disjoint sum of the conjugates X̄\textunderscore{}σ of X̄, this sum being moreover defined over ℚ. The ℚ-morphism X → Spec(K) is interpreted as the map from this sum to the index set I = Spec(K) ⊗\textunderscore{}ℚ ℚ̄.

For M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n], the situation is as follows: the scheme M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] represents the functor of isomorphism classes of elliptic curves E/T, equipped with an isomorphism

α : E\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)².

Such a pair (E,α) defines (by the theory of the alternating form e\textunderscore{}n) a primitive n-th root of unity ζ(α) over T, hence a morphism of schemes

(E,α) ↦ ζ(α) : M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]).

Viewed as a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]-scheme, M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] classifies the (E,α)/T, with T a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]-scheme, such that ζ(α) = ζ\textunderscore{}n.

We have

M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ℂ = X/Γ ;

whereas, if one puts X\textasciicircum{}± = ℂ − ℝ,

M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℂ = X\textasciicircum{}± × GL(2,ℤ/n) / GL(2,ℤ)

is the sum of φ(n) copies of X/Γ.

9.      An elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k has one or more nontrivial automorphisms. This rules out representability of the functor that assigns to a scheme T the set of isomorphism classes of elliptic curves E over T: it is not even a sheaf. This can be remedied in two ways.

a) Systematically impose additional level structures that eliminate these automorphisms. This is what we have done in this introduction.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0015-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0015-A01.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0016}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 16}{D018-P0016-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 16; collected-paper page 509; original folio 158.}\par\smallskip
b) Use the language of algebraic stacks, for which we refer to [8] and [17]. This is what we do in the text, for the following reasons.

1. Imposing an additional level structure modifies the behavior at infinity of the objects studied (M\textunderscore{}\{n̄m\} ramifies over M\textunderscore{}n along infinity).

2. The calculations and proofs become simpler when one does not have to concern oneself with troublesome additional structures.

In Chapter VI, from our results on modular stacks we deduce results on the coarse moduli schemes (= coarse modular schemes) usually considered.

10.     We refer to the table of contents and to the introductions of the various chapters for a more precise description of their contents.

We thank N. Katz for the help he has given us.

Notations

The work is divided into chapters; each chapter is divided into sections. Internal references within a single chapter do not mention the number of that chapter.

We shall freely use the definitions and notation of the theory of algebraic stacks as set out in [8] §4. However, contrary to the terminology of loc. cit., a morphism of categories fibered in groupoids

φ : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}1 → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}2

will be called representable if, for every morphism x : X → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}2 from a scheme X to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}2, the fiber product \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}1 ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}2\} X is an algebraic space.

A group scheme G over a scheme S is sometimes called an S-group. The subgroup scheme that is the kernel of multiplication by n in G is denoted by G\textunderscore{}n.

Let X be an S-scheme. If T is an S-scheme, we denote by X\textunderscore{}T the fiber product X ×\textunderscore{}S T. If S = Spec(A) and T = Spec(B), X\textunderscore{}B, or X ⊗\textunderscore{}A B, have the same meaning. For n ∈ ℕ, we set X[1/n] = X ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ[1/n].

We set ζ\textunderscore{}n = exp(2πi/n).

For the principles governing our notation for modular stacks, we refer to §0 of Chapter III.

\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0017-A01.}
\end{flushright}

\hypertarget{D018-P0018}{}

I Preliminaries

This chapter gathers well-known results of which we shall have occasion to make use. The reader is invited to consult it only as needed.

1. Curve schemes.

1.0.     In this section, a curve scheme over a scheme S means a proper and flat morphism of finite presentation, of relative dimension at most 1.

1.1.     If C/S is a curve scheme over S, the Euler–Poincaré characteristic χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}s\}) of the fibers is locally constant (EGA III, 7.9.). The arithmetic genus g\textunderscore{}s of C\textunderscore{}s is defined by

1 − g\textunderscore{}s = χ(C\textunderscore{}s,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}s\}).

If the Euler–Poincaré characteristic of the fibers is constant with value 1 − g, C|S is called a curve scheme of genus g, or simply a curve of genus g over S.

1.2.     A morphism p : X → S is said to be Cohen–Macaulay if it is flat and of finite presentation and its (geometric) fibers are Cohen–Macaulay schemes.

A Cohen–Macaulay curve scheme over S is a curve scheme p : C → S that is Cohen–Macaulay and whose geometric fibers are purely of dimension one. Likewise, when we speak of smooth, reduced, or integral curves, "purely of dimension one" will be understood.

For S = Spec(A), we shall speak interchangeably of curves over S and curves over A.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0018-A01.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0018-A01.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0019}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 19}{D018-P0019-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 19; collected-paper page 512; original folio 161.}\par\smallskip
2. Duality in the cohomology of coherent sheaves.

2.1.     We propose to recall what Grothendieck duality theory provides for a morphism p : X → S when

a) p is Cohen–Macaulay, purely of relative dimension d; and

b) S is Noetherian.

In the applications we have in view, we could always reduce to the case in which p is moreover quasi-projective.

The relative dualizing complex Rp\textasciicircum{}!\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S [10] has only one nonzero cohomology sheaf, that of degree −d; it is the sheaf of regular differentials

(2.1.1.)        ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} = ℋ\textasciicircum{}\{−d\}(Rp\textasciicircum{}!\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S)

([10] V. 9.7.). This sheaf is flat over S. Its formation commutes with every base change S' → S and with étale localization on X (see [10] or [34]).

2.2.     If p is moreover proper, one defines a "trace" morphism

(2.2.1)         Tr : R\textasciicircum{}d p\textunderscore{}*(ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S

whose formation is compatible with every base change. The duality theorem asserts that, for every bounded complex of coherent sheaves K, the arrow deduced from Tr,

(2.2.2.)        Rp\textunderscore{}* R Hom\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}X\}(K,ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\})[−d] ≃ R Hom\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S\}(Rp\textunderscore{}*K,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S),

is an isomorphism.

If K is reduced to a locally free sheaf F placed in degree 0 and the sheaves R\textasciicircum{}i p\textunderscore{}*(F) are locally free, (2.2.2.) becomes


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0019-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0019-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0020}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 20}{D018-P0020-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 20; collected-paper page 513; original folio 162.}\par\smallskip
(2.2.3.)        R\textasciicircum{}\{d−j\}p\textunderscore{}*(F\textasciicircum{}∨ ⊗ ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}) ≃ R\textasciicircum{}j p\textunderscore{}*(F)\textasciicircum{}∨

(where ∨ denotes the dual of an \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}X-module, respectively of an \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S-module).

ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} is an invertible sheaf if and only if p is Gorenstein [10].

For S not necessarily Noetherian, one defines ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} and Tr by passage to the limit; for K a bounded complex of locally free sheaves, (2.2.2.) remains valid and is proved by passage to the limit.

When S is the spectrum of a field k, ω\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} will also be denoted ω\textunderscore{}\{X/k\}, or simply ω\textunderscore{}X.

2.3.     Let X be a reduced curve over an algebraically closed field k, and let π : X̃ → X be the normalization of X. The sheaf ω\textunderscore{}X is identified with the following subsheaf of the direct image under π of the sheaf of meromorphic differentials on X. The sections of ω\textunderscore{}X over U are the meromorphic differentials ω on π\textasciicircum{}\{−1\}(U) such that, for every P ∈ U and every f ∈ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{X,P\},

∑\textunderscore{}\{R↦P\} Res\textunderscore{}R(f·ω) = 0.

If the only singularities of X are ordinary double points P\textunderscore{}i, and π\textasciicircum{}\{−1\}(P\textunderscore{}i) = \{P'\textunderscore{}i,P''\textunderscore{}i\}, the regular differentials on X are identified with the meromorphic differential forms ω on X̃ that are regular away from P'\textunderscore{}i,P''\textunderscore{}i, have at worst a simple pole at P'\textunderscore{}i and P''\textunderscore{}i, and satisfy

Res\textunderscore{}\{P'\textunderscore{}i\}(ω) + Res\textunderscore{}\{P''\textunderscore{}i\}(ω) = 0

([27], Chapter IV).

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0020-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0020-A02.}
\end{flushright}

3. Review of the Picard functor.

3.1.     Let p : X → S be a proper flat morphism of finite presentation. The relative Picard functor of X over S, denoted Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}, is the fppf sheaf associated with the presheaf

(3.1.1)         S' ↦ Pic(X ×\textunderscore{}S S') = the group of isomorphism classes of invertible sheaves on X ×\textunderscore{}S S'.

The canonical morphism Pic(X)/Pic(S) → Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}(S) is injective if p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}X = \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S. It is bijective if, in addition, p has a section (TDTE V.2.4.).

3.2.     One says that p is cohomologically flat in dimension 0 (EGA III, 7.), or simply cohomologically flat, if the formation of p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}X commutes with every base change S' → S. A fundamental theorem of M. Artin asserts that, if p is proper, flat of finite presentation, and cohomologically flat, then Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} is representable by an algebraic space locally of finite presentation over S ([2] Thm. 7.3). If, in addition, p : X → S is a curve scheme, Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} is smooth over S.

If Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} is representable by an algebraic space, Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} denotes the subfunctor in groups of Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\} that is the neutral component of Pic\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}. If p : X → S is a curve scheme, it is representable by an algebraic space.

3.3.     Let C be a proper curve over a field k. If ℒ is an invertible sheaf on C, its degree deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ) is defined by the Riemann–Roch formula

(3.3.1)         χ(ℒ) = deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ) + χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C).

Then (cf. [15])

(3.3.2)         deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ ℒ\textunderscore{}2) = deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ\textunderscore{}1) + deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ\textunderscore{}1).


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0021-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0021-A02.}
\end{flushright}

(3.3.3)     If the reduced irreducible components of C are the subschemes D\textunderscore{}i, and if D\textunderscore{}i has multiplicity n\textunderscore{}i (= the length of the local ring of C at the generic point of D\textunderscore{}i), then

deg\textunderscore{}C(ℒ) = ∑ n\textunderscore{}i deg\textunderscore{}\{D\textunderscore{}i\}(ℒ), where one has set

(3.3.4)         deg\textunderscore{}\{D\textunderscore{}i\}(ℒ) = deg\textunderscore{}\{D\textunderscore{}i\}(ℒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{D\textunderscore{}i\}).

3.4.     Let p : C → S be a curve over S and let ℒ be an invertible sheaf on C. The degree of ℒ on the fibers C\textunderscore{}s (s ∈ S) of C is locally constant and defines a morphism of abelian sheaves (for the fppf topology):

(3.4.1)         deg : Pic\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} → ℤ.

The kernel of (3.4.1) is denoted Pic\textasciicircum{}\{[o]\}\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}. (See 3.7 for the relation between Pic\textasciicircum{}o and Pic\textasciicircum{}\{[o]\} in a special case.)

3.5.     Let C be a reduced curve over an algebraically closed field k. The diagram of the irreducible components of C is the following unoriented graph Γ(C), in which two kinds of vertices are distinguished.

(3.5.1)     The set Γ\textasciicircum{}o = 'Γ\textasciicircum{}o ⊔ "Γ\textasciicircum{}o of vertices of Γ(C) is the sum of the set 'Γ\textasciicircum{}o of irreducible components of C and the set "Γ\textasciicircum{}o of singular points of C.

(3.5.2)     For p ∈ C(k), call the points of the normalization C̃ of C mapping to p the branches of C at p. The set Γ\textasciicircum{}1 of edges of Γ(C) is the set of pairs (p,b) formed by a singular point p and a branch b of C at p.

(3.5 3)     An edge (p,b) joins p ∈ "Γ\textasciicircum{}o to the irreducible component of C, identified with an irreducible component of C̃, that contains b.

Examples 3.5.4.     Denote the points of 'Γ\textasciicircum{}o by ○ and those of "Γ\textasciicircum{}o by ⊗. The following curves have the following diagrams:

curve                                       diagram

If C is purely of dimension one, but not necessarily reduced, set Γ(C) = Γ(C\textunderscore{}red).

3.6.     Let Aut(Γ(C)) denote the group of automorphisms of the graph Γ(C) that carry 'Γ\textasciicircum{}o(C) into 'Γ\textasciicircum{}o(C) and "Γ\textasciicircum{}o(C) into "Γ\textasciicircum{}o(C).

In the case where Γ(C) is a cycle with 2n vertices and 2n edges:

we shall say that an element of Aut(Γ(C)) is a rotation, or that it preserves the orientation of Γ(C), if it induces the identity on the first integral cohomology group H\textasciicircum{}1(Γ(C),ℤ). The distinguished subgroup Aut\textasciicircum{}+(Γ(C)) of Aut(Γ(C)) formed by the rotations is cyclic of order n, and Aut(Γ(C)) is the dihedral group that is the semidirect product of a "reflection" by Aut\textasciicircum{}+(Γ(C)) ≃ ℤ/nℤ.

3.7. Let k be an algebraically closed field, and let C be a proper reduced curve over k whose singularities are formally isomorphic to that of the union of the coordinate axes in an affine space \ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}n.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0023-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0023-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0024}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 24}{D018-P0024-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 24; collected-paper page 517; original folio 166.}\par\smallskip
The Picard scheme Pic\textunderscore{}\{C/k\} decomposes as follows. Let (D\textunderscore{}i)\textunderscore{}\{i∈I\} be the irreducible components of C, let D̃\textunderscore{}i be the normalization of D\textunderscore{}i, and let C̃ = \ensuremath{\amalg} D̃\textunderscore{}i be the normalization of C.

a) The "total degree" morphism

deg\textunderscore{}I = (deg\textunderscore{}\{D\textunderscore{}i\})\textunderscore{}\{i∈I\} : Pic\textunderscore{}\{C/k\} → ℤ\textasciicircum{}I

is surjective, with kernel Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/k\}.

b) The morphism

Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/k\} → Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C̃/k\} = ∏ Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{D̃\textunderscore{}i/k\}

is surjective, with image the largest quotient of Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/k\} that is an abelian scheme. Its kernel is a torus, canonically isomorphic to

H\textasciicircum{}1(Γ(C),ℤ) ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m.

If D is proper and purely of dimension one over k, there exists a unique curve C as above, equipped with a radicial morphism C → D; the map

Pic\textunderscore{}\{D/k\} → Pic\textunderscore{}\{C/k\}

is surjective with connected unipotent kernel.

4. Non-smoothness subscheme.

Let f : X → S be a flat morphism of finite presentation, purely of relative dimension d.

It follows from the Jacobian criterion for smoothness that the subscheme X\textasciicircum{}sing of X defined by the Jacobian ideal, i.e. by the d-th determinantal ideal of Ω\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}\{X/S\}

(Bourbaki; Commutative Algebra, Chapter 7, §4, Exercise 10) has as its underlying topological space the set of points of X at which f is not smooth. We shall call it the non-smoothness subscheme of X [SGA 7, VI]. The locus on which f is smooth is denoted X\textasciicircum{}reg

5. Review of deformation theory.

5.1.     Let Λ be a complete Noetherian local ring with residue field k. Two particularly useful cases are the following.

a) Λ = k.

b) k is perfect of characteristic p > 0 and Λ is the ring of Witt vectors W(k) (the unique complete discrete valuation ring with residue field k and maximal ideal (p)).

Let C\textunderscore{}Λ denote the category of local Artinian Λ-algebras with residue field k, and let Ĉ\textunderscore{}Λ denote the category of complete Noetherian local Λ-algebras with residue field k. A covariant functor F from C\textunderscore{}Λ to (Sets) is said to be pro-representable if there exist R ∈ Ob Ĉ\textunderscore{}Λ and an isomorphism of functors on C\textunderscore{}Λ

(5.1.1)        ξ̂ : Hom(R,A) ⟶̃ F(A).

The algebra R is then uniquely determined.

A covariant functor F from Ĉ\textunderscore{}Λ to (Sets) induces by restriction a functor F|C\textunderscore{}Λ : C\textunderscore{}Λ → (Sets). If F|C\textunderscore{}Λ is prorepresentable, it is said to be effectively prorepresentable if there exists ξ ∈ F(R) (with R as above) that defines (5.1.1).

5.2.     Consider systems (M,B,α) of the following type

b) B is a complete flat Noetherian local M-algebra, with residue field k, equipped with an isomorphism

α : B ⊗\textunderscore{}M k ≃ k[[X,Y]]/(XY).

Such a system (Λ[[t]], A, α\textunderscore{}\{o/0\}) is provided by the Λ[[t]]-algebra

A = Λ[[t,X,Y]]/(XY−t).

It is proved in [8] §1 that this last system is versal. For every (B,M,α), there exists a homomorphism Λ[[t]] → M such that (B,α) is deduced from (A,α\textunderscore{}\{o/0\}) by extension of scalars:

B = A ⊗\textunderscore{}\{Λ[[t]]\} M.

One easily deduces the following statement:

Proposition 5.3.     Let p : C → S be a scheme of curves over a Noetherian scheme S. Let s ∈ S and x ∈ C\textunderscore{}s(s). Suppose that C\textunderscore{}s has at x an ordinary double point with rational tangent directions. Then the completion Ĉ\textunderscore{}x of C at x is Ŝ\textunderscore{}s-isomorphic to Ŝ\textunderscore{}s[[X,Y]]/(XY−t) for a suitable t ∈ Γ(Ŝ\textunderscore{}s,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}).

Better still:

Theorem 5.3.     Let p : C → S be a scheme of curves, let s ∈ S, and let x ∈ C\textunderscore{}s(s) be such that C\textunderscore{}s has an ordinary double point at x. Then, locally for the étale topology (near x and s), C is S-isomorphic to

X = S[u,v]/(uv−t) ⊂ \ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}2\textunderscore{}S

for a suitable t. If the tangent directions of C\textunderscore{}s at x are rational over k(s), the henselization C\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{(x)\} of C at x is S\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{(s)\}-isomorphic to the henselization of X at the

This theorem follows at once from the special case in which S is assumed to be of finite type over ℤ. It follows from 5.2 and R. Elkik's theory of algebraization of modules of isolated singularities.

6. Division points of elliptic curves.

Let E be an elliptic curve (an abelian variety of dimension one) over an algebraically closed field k.

For every integer n ≥ 1, the group scheme E\textunderscore{}n, the kernel of multiplication by n, is known to be the spectrum of a Hopf algebra of dimension n\textasciicircum{}2 over k. If n is invertible in k, then E\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2.

If k has characteristic p > 0, the kernel of the Frobenius morphism F : E → E\textasciicircum{}(p) has rank p. It is isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}p or to α\textunderscore{}p. If it is isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}p, E is said to be ordinary, and E\textunderscore{}n ≃ ℤ/n × μ\textunderscore{}n. Otherwise E is said to be supersingular.

In the exact sequence

0 → Ker(F) → E\textunderscore{}p → E\textunderscore{}p/Ker(F) → 0,

the two extreme terms are Cartier dual. If E is supersingular, E\textunderscore{}p is therefore isomorphic to α\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}2\}, the unique nontrivial extension killed by p of α\textunderscore{}p by α\textunderscore{}p.

The supersingular elliptic curves over k are all isogenous and form a finite number of isomorphism classes (cf. [32]).

7. Review of commutative algebra.

We shall often use the following results from commutative algebra; for their proofs we refer to EGA.

(7.1)     Let f : X → Y be a morphism of finite type between Noetherian schemes. If Y is Cohen–Macaulay and f is flat, then X is Cohen–Macaulay if and only if the fibers of f are. For example, if Y is Cohen–Macaulay and f is quasi-finite and flat, then X is Cohen–Macaulay.

If Y is regular, X is Cohen–Macaulay, and at the point x ∈ X with image y ∈ Y one has

(7.1 1)        dim(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{X,x\}) = dim(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{Y,y\}) + dim(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{X,x\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{Y,y\}\} k(y)),

then f is flat at x. Condition (7.1.1) is satisfied if f is quasi-finite and dominant and X is irreducible.

EGA IV 6.3.5 and EGA IV 6.1.5.

(7.2)     Let Y be Noetherian of dimension two. For Y to be normal, it is necessary and sufficient that Y be Cohen–Macaulay and regular except at points of codimension two.

EGA IV. 5.8.6. (Serre's normality criterion).

(7.3)     Let Y be Noetherian of dimension one and generically reduced. For Y to be reduced, it is necessary and sufficient that Y be Cohen–Macaulay.

EGA IV. 5.8.5.

(7.4)     Let f : X → Y be a morphism of flat S-schemes of finite presentation. For f to satisfy one of the following conditions: to be flat, smooth, étale, an open immersion


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0028-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0028-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0029}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 29}{D018-P0029-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 29; collected-paper page 522; original folio 171.}\par\smallskip
an isomorphism, or a flat relative complete intersection, it is necessary and sufficient that for every geometric point s̄ of S, f\textunderscore{}s̄ : X\textunderscore{}s̄ → Y\textunderscore{}s̄ satisfy the stated condition.

For "flat," this is EGA IV 11.3.10. The properties "smooth," "étale," and "flat relative complete intersection" are equivalent to "flat" plus a property of the fibers. Finally, "open immersion" is equivalent to "étale and radicial" (EGA IV.17.9.1), where "radicial" is a property of the fibers, and "isomorphism" is equivalent to "open immersion and surjective."

8. Coarse moduli schemes

Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} be an algebraic stack over a base scheme S.

Definition 8.1.     A coarse space of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} is an algebraic space M over S, equipped with an S-morphism π : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} → M having the following properties:

(i) Every S-morphism from \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} to an algebraic space X over S factors uniquely through π.

(ii) If s̄ : Spec(k) → S is a geometric point of S (with k algebraically closed), π induces a bijection between the set of isomorphism classes of objects of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} over s̄ (= an S-morphism from s to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}) and M(s̄).

8.2.     If S is Noetherian and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} is separated and of finite type over S, one can show that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} admits a coarse space M. Here are some of its properties.

(8.2.1)     Let m : Spec(k) → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} be a geometric point of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}, let \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}̃\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}},m\} be the strict henselization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} at m, let \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{M,π(m)\} be the strict henselization of M at π(m), and let Aut(m) be the group of automorphisms of the object m of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} over Spec(k). The map π induces an isomorphism

\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0029-A02.}

Spec(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}},m\})/Aut(m) ≃ Spec(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{M,π(m)\}).

Let H ⊂ Aut(m) be the subgroup of automorphisms of the object m of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} over k that extend to an automorphism of the object Spec(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}},m\}) → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} over Spec(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}},m\}). The group Aut(m)/H acts effectively on \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textasciicircum{}h\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}},m\}.

(8.2.2)     If u : X → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} is étale and surjective, M is the quotient of X by the equivalence relation X ×\textunderscore{}\ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} X. In particular, for \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} = [X/G] (see [8] §4), one has M = X/G.

(8.2.3)     No more than passage to the quotient does formation of the coarse scheme commute in general with base changes. It does, however, commute with flat base changes, and with every base change when π is étale. Moreover, if M' is the coarse scheme of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} ⊗\textunderscore{}S S', the map

M' → M ⊗\textunderscore{}S S'

is radicial

II    Generalized elliptic curves.

In this chapter, we define generalized elliptic curves over an arbitrary base scheme and prove the theorems that make the adopted definition workable.

1. Néron polygons.

1.1.     Let C̃ = ℙ¹ × ℤ/n be the disjoint union of n copies of ℙ¹, indexed by ℤ/n (n ≥ 1). By gluing the i-th copy of ℙ¹ to the (i+1)-st, identifying the section 0 of the i-th copy with the section ∞ of the (i+1)-st, one obtains a genus-one curve C over Spec(ℤ), with normalization C̃.

For every scheme S, the scheme over S obtained from this one by extension of scalars is called the standard Néron polygon with n sides over S, or simply the standard n-gon over S. A Néron polygon (respectively, an n-gon) over an algebraically closed field k is a scheme over k isomorphic to one of the standard polygons (respectively, to the standard n-gon).

Examples.

n = 1

n = 3

Lemma 1 2.     Let C be a Néron polygon over an algebraically closed field k.

(i)        H⁰(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C) = k.

(ii)       H⁰(C,ω\textunderscore{}C) has dimension 1 over k, and the canonical morphism

H⁰(C,ω\textunderscore{}C) ⊗\textunderscore{}k \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C → ω\textunderscore{}C

is an isomorphism. In particular, ω\textunderscore{}C ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0031-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0031-A02.}
\end{flushright}

Proof: (i) follows from the fact that C is reduced and connected.

(ii) We may suppose that C is the standard n-gon.

Let η be the differential form on the normalization C̃ = ℙ¹ × ℤ/n whose restriction to the i-th copy of ℙ¹ is dx/x. It is clear that η has at most simple poles, with residues +1 and −1 at the “0” and “∞,” respectively, of the i-th copy of ℙ¹. The form η therefore defines a global section of ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} (cf. I. 2.3.). It generates ω\textunderscore{}C at every point of C.

Lemma 1.3.     Let C be a reduced, connected genus-one curve over an algebraically closed field, whose only singularities are ordinary double points, and such that ω\textunderscore{}C ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}. Then C is smooth or a Néron polygon.

Proof: Let π : C̃ → C be the normalization of C, with irreducible components (C\textunderscore{}i)\textunderscore{}\{1≤i≤a\}; let g\textunderscore{}i ≥ 0 be the genus of C\textunderscore{}i, and let b be the number of double points of C. The long exact cohomology sequence deduced from

0 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C → π\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C̃\} → cokernel → 0

and 1.2(i) gives

a = ∑ g\textunderscore{}i + b        and

b ≥ a−1 ≥ 0.

Let b\textunderscore{}i ≥ 0 be the number of points of C\textunderscore{}i whose image is a singular point of C. One has

2b = ∑\textunderscore{}\{i=1\}\textasciicircum{}a b\textunderscore{}i.

The existence of a nonzero ω on each component gives

g\textunderscore{}i = 0 ⇒ b\textunderscore{}i ≥ 2.

Connectedness gives

a > 0 ⇒ b\textunderscore{}i ≥ 1.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0032-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0032-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip

These inequalities have the following solutions:

a)    a = 1, b = 0, g = 1        (the nonsingular case)

b)    a = n, b = n, g\textunderscore{}i = 0, b\textunderscore{}i = 2        (n ≥ 1): the C\textunderscore{}i are ≃ ℙ¹,

and on each C\textunderscore{}i two points map to a double point of C. Since C is connected, it is a Néron polygon.

Definition 1.4.     A stable genus-one curve C over a scheme S (or an algebraic space S, or an algebraic stack S) is a scheme of curves over S (cf. I.1.0.) every geometric fiber of which is either a proper smooth connected genus-one curve or a Néron polygon.

Proposition 1.5. : Let p : C → S be a proper flat morphism of finite presentation. The set of points s ∈ S such that C\textunderscore{}s is a stable genus-one curve is an open subscheme of S.

Proof: The conditions that C\textunderscore{}s be reduced, reduced and connected, reduced and purely one-dimensional, of genus one, and have only ordinary quadratic singularities are all open. That the condition ω ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} of 1.3 is open follows from the proof of 1.6 below.

Proposition 1.6.     Let p : C → S be a stable genus-one curve.

(i)    p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C = \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S universally.

(ii)   ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} and p\textunderscore{}*(ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}) are invertible, and the canonical map

(1.6.1)        p\textasciicircum{}*p\textunderscore{}*(ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}) → ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.     We immediately reduce to the case where S is Noetherian. Since the geometric fibers of p are connected and reduced, (i) follows from EGA III. 7.8.8.

Since the geometric fibers of p are local complete intersections, p is a relative local complete intersection and ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} is invertible. From (i) and from


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0033-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0033-A02.}
\end{flushright}

the fact that R²p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} = 0, it follows that R¹p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} is locally free and its formation is compatible with every base change S' → S (EGA III. 7.8.). Coherent-sheaf duality (I.2.) then shows that p\textunderscore{}*(ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}) is locally free and its formation is compatible with every base change. Since (ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\})\textunderscore{}s = ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}s/s\}, it remains to prove (ii) when S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field. In that case, 1.6.1 is well known when C is smooth, and follows from 1.2 for a Néron polygon.

The following Lemma 1.7 is a special case of I 3.7.

Lemma 1.7.     Let C be a Néron polygon over an algebraically closed field k.

(i)    Pic\textasciicircum{}o(C) = \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m ⊗ H¹(Γ(C),ℤ) is isomorphic to \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m.

(ii)   An automorphism g of C induces the identity on Pic\textasciicircum{}o(C) if and only if g acts by rotations on the component diagram Γ(C) of C.

Lemma 1.8.     Let C̃ be the standard n-gon over ℤ. There is an exact sequence of algebraic groups

0 → \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}n ⟶\textasciicircum{}a Aut(C) ⟶\textasciicircum{}b Aut(Γ(C)) → 0.

Let C̃ = ℙ¹ × ℤ/n; C is defined by gluing from C̃, whence π : C̃ → C. A local computation shows that C̃ is obtained from C by blowing up the non-smoothness subscheme C\textasciicircum{}sing, and this remains true after every base change. The union of the marked sections “0” and “∞” of the components of C̃ is the inverse image of the non-smoothness subscheme. Hence, after every base change, every automorphism of C induces an automorphism of C̃ that preserves the union of the marked sections. This defines b and shows that Ker(b) is identified with the subgroup \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}n of Aut(C) consisting of the automorphisms that take every component ℙ¹ × \{i\} to itself and preserve the sections (0,i) and (∞,i). After every base change, a section λ = (λ\textunderscore{}i) of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}n acts on ℙ¹ × \{i\} as scaling by the factor λ\textunderscore{}i.

It remains to prove that b is surjective; we shall do so by exhibiting automorphisms of C compatible with the gluing data and defining

automorphisms of C whose images under b generate the dihedral group Aut(Γ(C)). They are

τ : (x,i) ↦ (x\textasciicircum{}\{−1\},−i)

σ\textunderscore{}j : (x,i) ↦ (x,i+j)        (j ∈ ℤ/n).

1.9.     Let C be the standard n-gon over Spec(ℤ). Identify the normalization C̃ of C with ℙ¹ × ℤ/nℤ and the inverse image of C\textasciicircum{}reg in C̃ with \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m × ℤ/nℤ. An action of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m × ℤ/nℤ on C̃ = ℙ¹ × ℤ/nℤ is obtained by setting

(a,i) + (b,j) = (a·b,i+j),

where a·b denotes the natural action of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m on ℙ¹.

This action descends to the quotient and defines a morphism

(1.9.1)        + : C\textasciicircum{}reg × C → C.

The restriction of + to C\textasciicircum{}reg × C\textasciicircum{}reg defines a commutative group-scheme structure on C\textasciicircum{}reg, and + is an action of the group C\textasciicircum{}reg on C.

Automorphisms of (C,+) are defined as follows:

a) the automorphism of C̃ = ℙ¹ × ℤ/n

τ : (x,i) ↦ (x\textasciicircum{}\{−1\},−i)

descends to the quotient and defines an automorphism τ of (C,+);

b) for every scheme S and every ζ ∈ μ\textunderscore{}n(S), the automorphism u(ζ) of C\textunderscore{}S

u(ζ) : (x,i) ↦ (x·ζ\textasciicircum{}i,i)

descends to the quotient and defines an automorphism of (C\textunderscore{}S,+).

Parts a) and b) give an action on C by the semidirect-product group scheme of the two-element group \{e,τ\} by μ\textunderscore{}n.

Proposition 1.10.     Let C be the standard n-gon over Spec(ℤ). The preceding construction identifies the functor Aut(C,+) of automorphisms of C compatible with + with the semidirect product \{e,τ\} ×' μ\textunderscore{}n. The subgroup Aut⁺(C,+) of automorphisms acting trivially on Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/ℤ\} is identified with the subgroup μ\textunderscore{}n.

One has Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/ℤ\} ≃ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m, and τ acts on Pic\textasciicircum{}o by the automorphism x ↦ x\textasciicircum{}\{−1\}

1.11.     Let k be an algebraically closed field, and let (C,+) be obtained from the curve of 1.10 by extension of scalars from ℤ to k. One checks that, for x ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(k), translation y ↦ x+y acts by rotations on the component diagram Γ(C).

Definition 1.12.     A generalized elliptic curve (over a base S) is a stable genus-one curve

p : C → S,

equipped with a morphism

(1 12.1)        + : C\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}S C → C

such that

a) The restriction of (1.1 .1) to C\textasciicircum{}reg makes C\textasciicircum{}reg a commutative group scheme.

b) (1.12.1) defines an action of the group scheme C\textasciicircum{}reg on C.

c) For every geometric point s̄ of S such that C\textunderscore{}s̄ is singular, the translations y ↦ x+y of C\textunderscore{}s (x ∈ C\textunderscore{}s\textasciicircum{}reg(s̄)) act by rotations on Γ(C\textunderscore{}s).

Of course, a smooth generalized elliptic curve is just an elliptic curve in the usual sense.

Proposition 1.13.     Let (C,+) satisfy 1.12 a) and b). The set U of s ∈ S such that C\textunderscore{}s/s satisfies c) is open and closed in S. Over U, C\textasciicircum{}reg acts trivially on Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

Proof:     Let x be a geometric point of C\textunderscore{}s̄. Translation y ↦ x+y acts by translation if and only if it acts trivially on Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}s\}. Apply the following lemma (a variation on a theme of SGA 3) to Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} ×\textunderscore{}S C\textasciicircum{}reg over C\textasciicircum{}reg.

Lemma 1.14.     Let p : A → S be a group scheme over S whose fibers are extensions of abelian varieties by tori. Let g and h be two endomorphisms of A. There exists an open and closed part of S such that, for every S-scheme T,

g\textunderscore{}T = h\textunderscore{}T    if and only if    T factors through V.

The lemma shows that the U of 1.13 is closed, since its complement is the image of an open (and closed) part C\textasciicircum{}reg−V of C\textasciicircum{}reg. It also proves that, if S = U, C\textasciicircum{}reg acts trivially on Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}. We shall prove that U is open at the same time as 1.15 below.

Proof of 1.14.     If S is local Artinian, with point s, and g\textunderscore{}s = h\textunderscore{}s, then g = h on A\textunderscore{}n, for n invertible, since this group is finite étale. The union of the A\textunderscore{}n is schematically dense, and hence g = h.

We may reduce to the case where S is Noetherian. If s ∈ S and g\textunderscore{}s = h\textunderscore{}s, it follows that Ker(g−h) has the same formal completion as A at 0 ∈ A\textunderscore{}s ⊂ A, and then that g = h in a neighborhood of s. There therefore exists an open V as in 1.14. Let V̄ be the closure of V. One has Ā\textunderscore{}V = A\textunderscore{}V̄, and hence g = h over V̄, and V ⊃ V̄ is closed.

a)    ⋃\textunderscore{}n S\textunderscore{}n = image of the non-smoothness subscheme C\textasciicircum{}sing    (I. 4.)

Proof:     The problem is local on S, and trivial in a neighborhood of the points of S where C\textunderscore{}s is smooth. Let therefore s̄ be a geometric point of S, localized at s ∈ S, such that the curve C\textunderscore{}s̄ is an n-gon. Let α be an isomorphism between C\textunderscore{}s̄ and the standard polygon, such that α(e) is the point 1 of the component of index 0 ∈ ℤ/n, and index the components of C\textunderscore{}s̄ by ℤ/n using α. Translation by x ∈ C\textunderscore{}s̄\textasciicircum{}reg, in the component of index i, induces the rotation of Γ(C\textunderscore{}s̄) taking the component of index 0 to that of index i: addition induces on π₀(C\textunderscore{}s\textasciicircum{}reg) ≃ ℤ/n addition in ℤ/n, and, if x lies in the component of index one of C\textunderscore{}s̄\textasciicircum{}reg, translation by x transitively permutes the singular points of C\textunderscore{}s̄. In an étale neighborhood of s̄, C\textunderscore{}reg/S admits sections x such that x(s̄) lies in the component of index one of C\textunderscore{}s̄. The automorphism g = x + of C/S then satisfies the hypothesis of the following lemma.

Lemma 1.16.     Let C/S be a stable genus-one curve over S, let g be an S-automorphism of C, and let s̄ be a geometric point of S localized at s ∈ S. Suppose that C\textunderscore{}s̄ is an n-gon and that g transitively permutes the singular points of C\textunderscore{}s. Let T ⊂ S be the image of the non-smoothness subscheme C\textasciicircum{}sing. Over a neighborhood of s, the curve C\textunderscore{}T/T is isomorphic, locally for the étale topology, to the standard n-gon.

One verifies fiber by fiber that C\textasciicircum{}sing is unramified over S. In an étale neighborhood of s̄, C\textasciicircum{}sing is therefore a disjoint sum of n subschemes F\textunderscore{}i, each F\textunderscore{}i → S being a closed immersion. Since g is an automorphism, g(⋃ F\textunderscore{}i) = ⋃ F\textunderscore{}i. At s̄, and hence in an étale neighborhood of s̄, g transitively permutes the F\textunderscore{}i, and all of these have the same image T.

To prove the lemma, we may suppose that S = T. What precedes shows that C\textasciicircum{}sing is then finite étale over S; we may suppose it is a sum of disjoint sections x\textunderscore{}i. By I 5.3, for every s ∈ S, (C,x\textunderscore{}i(s))/(S,s) is locally for the étale topology isomorphic to the subscheme of \ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}²\textunderscore{}S with equation xy = t, where t is a suitable section of \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S, vanishing at s. The image of the non-smooth locus x = y = 0 of this

Blow up C\textasciicircum{}sing in C to obtain C̃. A local calculation shows that C̃ is smooth over S, with fibers isomorphic to the normalizations of the fibers of C, and that the inverse image of C\textasciicircum{}sing in C̃ is étale over S.

Let π : C̃ → S' be the Stein factorization of C̃. The scheme S' is finite étale over S, and the geometric fibers of π are isomorphic to ℙ¹. Locally on S for the étale topology, C̃ is therefore a sum of n copies of ℙ¹. In an étale neighborhood of s, C is obtained from C̃ just as C\textunderscore{}s̄ is obtained from C̃\textunderscore{}s̄, and hence is a standard Néron polygon.

Variant: We easily reduce to supposing S Noetherian. We may then replace the étale-localization argument used above by passage to completions; this avoids the delicate theorem 5.3, replaced by 5.2, but one must verify compatibility between blowups and completions.

One then verifies that the image of an open and closed part of C\textasciicircum{}reg (here, = V) is open and closed.

The following proposition will play a key technical role in questions of representability.

Proposition 1.17.     Let X/S be a generalized elliptic curve over S, with identity section e ∈ X(S), and let u : Y → X be a finite étale cover of X, equipped with e ∈ Y(S) over e. Suppose that the geometric fibers of Y/S are connected. There is then one and only one generalized-elliptic-curve structure on Y, with identity e, such that the diagram

+ Y\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}S Y ─────────→ Y │                │ u ↓                ↓ (1.17.1) X\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}S Y ───+────→ X

is commutative.

Let t ∈ Y\textasciicircum{}reg(S). Let F' be the functor associating to T/S the set of τ : Y\textunderscore{}T → Y\textunderscore{}T making the diagram

τ Y\textunderscore{}T ─────→ Y\textunderscore{}T │          │ ↓          ↓ X\textunderscore{}T ─────→ X\textunderscore{}T u(t)+

commutative.

This functor is represented by an étale cover S' of S (open and closed in the cover defined by the Stein factorization of Y\textunderscore{}T ×\textunderscore{}\{X\textunderscore{}T\} Y\textunderscore{}T (for the morphisms (u(t)+)∘u and u)). The map e ↦ τ(e) sends S' into u\textasciicircum{}\{−1\}ut(S), finite étale over S. The subfunctor F' of F corresponding to the τ such that τ(e\textunderscore{}T) = t\textunderscore{}T is therefore again represented by an S'' finite and étale over S. Let us prove that S'' ⟶̃ S. It is enough to verify that, when S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field, there exists one and only one τ lifting u(t)+ such that τ(e) = t.

This amounts to showing that the cover Y of X and its inverse image under u(t)+ : X → X are isomorphic. For smooth X, this follows from the fact that u(t) lies in the neutral component of X\textasciicircum{}reg. For X a Néron polygon, one observes that X has only one irreducible cover of a given degree.

The unique section of S'' over S defines a morphism t+ : Y → Y. Since this construction is compatible with base changes, it defines

+ : Y\textasciicircum{}reg × Y → Y,

making (1.17.1) commutative, such that t + e = t, and characterized by these properties. It remains to prove that + makes Y a generalized elliptic curve, i.e. that various identities, such as x+y = y+x (x,y ∈ Y\textasciicircum{}reg(S) are satisfied. Since u(x+y) = u(x) + u(y) = u(y) + u(x) = u(y+x), x+y is a section of u\textasciicircum{}\{−1\}(u(y+x)(S)), étale over S, and it is enough to verify that x+y = y+x on the geometric fibers. For proper smooth fibers, a composition law with identity is automatically an abelian group law. For the others, the Néron polygon with n sides, with its standard + law, has as covers only Néron polygons with nk sides, with their standard + law.

The other identities to be verified are proved in the same way.

n : x ↦ nx : C\textasciicircum{}reg → C\textasciicircum{}reg

is flat. Its kernel C\textunderscore{}n is therefore flat over S.

Suppose that S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field k.

a)    if C is smooth, one knows that C\textunderscore{}n is finite of rank n²

b)    if C is an m-gon, one has C\textasciicircum{}reg ≃ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m × ℤ/m.

Writing (n,m) for the gcd of n and m, one therefore has

C\textunderscore{}n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/((n,m))

Recall the following lemma.

Lemma 1.19.     Let f : X → S be a quasi-finite, flat, and separated morphism, with S Noetherian. If the rank of the fibers of f is constant, then f is finite.

Proof:     Let us prove that f is proper. By the valuative criterion for properness, we may suppose that S is a trait. By the Main theorem of Zariski, X is an open subscheme of a scheme X̄ finite over S, which may even be taken flat over S. Comparing the ranks of the special and generic fibers of X and X̄, one finds that X = X̄, whence 1.19.

Corollary 1.20.     Let p : C → S be a generalized elliptic curve over S, and let n be an integer. Suppose that, for every geometric point s̄ of S, C\textunderscore{}s̄ is smooth, or is an m-gon with n|m. Then C\textunderscore{}n is finite locally free over S, of rank n².

We reduce to supposing S Noetherian, and apply 1.18 and 1.19.

2. Irreducible stable curves.

In this §, we prove that, for C/S a stable genus-one curve with irreducible geometric fibers, and for e ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(S), there exists on C one and only one generalized-elliptic-curve structure with identity e.

2.1     Certain preliminary results will hold for a proper flat morphism of finite presentation p : C → S satisfying the following condition.

(2.1.1)     C is a Cohen-Macaulay genus-one curve over S, p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C = \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S universally, and p\textasciicircum{}*p\textunderscore{}*ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} ⟶̃ ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

A stable genus-one curve over S satisfies (2 1.1) (1.6). If a geometric fiber C\textunderscore{}s̄ of p satisfies (2.1.1), then C/S satisfies (2.1.1) in a neighborhood of s (for the condition: “Cohen-Macaulay,” see EGA IV 12.2.1.; for

After shrinking S, suppose U = S. Since K\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} has finite support, K has finite support over S. Lemma 2.3. With the notation of 2.2, suppose H⁰(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}) = 0 and dim\textunderscore{}\{k(s̄)\} H¹(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}) = 1. p\textunderscore{}* Hom(\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}},ω\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}) ≃ (R¹p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}})\textasciicircum{}∨

Corollary 2.4. : Let p : C → S be a genus-one curve over S satisfying (2.1.1). Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}} be an invertible sheaf on C defining an element of Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}(S) (cf. I 3.2). For every section t of C over S, there is a unique section t' such that, locally on S, m(t') is isomorphic to m(t) ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}}.

Proof. We reduce to the case where S is noetherian. By 2.2 we may suppose that S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field. We treat a “universal” case.

Make the base change to T = C ×\textunderscore{}S Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} → S, take t to be the universal section and \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}} the universal invertible sheaf. For every point of the form (x,1\textunderscore{}\{Pic\}) (x ∈ C) of T the assertion is trivially true. Hence by 2.2 it holds on a neighborhood of C × 1\textunderscore{}\{Pic\} ⊂ T. Since C is proper, there is an open neighborhood U of 1\textunderscore{}\{Pic\} in Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} over which the assertion holds above C × U ⊂ T. The assertion therefore holds on the product of C with the group generated by U, which is all of Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}, and hence on T = C ×\textunderscore{}S Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

(2.5.2)                 φ : C\textasciicircum{}reg → Pic\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} : t ↦ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(t).

Theorem 2.6. Let p : C → S be a genus-one curve satisfying (2.1.1).

(i)     φ is an open immersion.

(ii)    If p has geometrically integral fibers, the image of φ is a torsor under Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

(iii)   If p has geometrically reduced fibers, the action + of Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} on C induces the trivial action of Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} on Pic\textasciicircum{}[0]\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

Proof. It is enough to prove (i) and (ii) when S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field k (EGA IV. 17.8.2.). The images under φ of the various connected components of C\textasciicircum{}reg are then disjoint. Let D be one of them and e ∈ D(k). The maps λ ↦ λ + e and t ↦ φ(t) − φ(e) are inverse isomorphisms between D and Pic⁰. In particular φ is an isomorphism D ≃ φ(e) + Pic⁰, and (i), (ii) follow.

Let us prove (iii). Let λ ∈ Pic⁰, represented by an invertible sheaf \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}}. Locally for the fppf topology, every element of Pic\textasciicircum{}[0] has the form \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(Σ n\textunderscore{}i t\textunderscore{}i) =\textunderscore{}\{dfn\} ⊗\textunderscore{}i m(t\textunderscore{}i)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(−n\textunderscore{}i)\}, with Σ n\textunderscore{}i = 0, for suitable sections t\textunderscore{}i of C\textasciicircum{}reg. Transforming by λ + gives

\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(Σ n\textunderscore{}i(λ+t\textunderscore{}i)) = ⊗\textunderscore{}i m(λ+t\textunderscore{}i)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(−n\textunderscore{}i)\} ≃ ⊗\textunderscore{}i [m(t\textunderscore{}i)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(n\textunderscore{}i)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}}\textasciicircum{}\{⊗ n\textunderscore{}i\}] ≃ ⊗\textunderscore{}i m(t\textunderscore{}i)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(−n\textunderscore{}i)\} ⊗ ⊗\textunderscore{}i \ensuremath{\mathcal{L}}\textasciicircum{}\{⊗ n\textunderscore{}i\} ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(Σ n\textunderscore{}i t\textunderscore{}i),

with Σ n\textunderscore{}i = 0, so it remains equivalent to itself.

Here is a variant of 2.2.

Proposition 2.6.1. Let S be noetherian, s̄ a geometric point of S localized at s ∈ S, p : C → S a genus-one curve scheme over S, and \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} a coherent sheaf on C flat over S. Suppose that C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is an integral curve,
