Then over a neighborhood U of s there exists a unique section t ∈ C(U) such that \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}|\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(U)\} is isomorphic to m(t).

As observed in 2.1, C/S satisfies (2.1.1) near s; by 2.2 it is therefore enough to treat the case where S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field, S = s̄.

Under this hypothesis, let us prove H⁰(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) = 0. If a nonzero f ∈ H⁰(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) existed, the sequence

0 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C →ᶠ \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} → \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}/\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C·f → 0

would be exact, since \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} is torsion-free on integral C. We would obtain χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) = χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C) + χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}/\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C·f) = 0 + χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}/f·\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) ≥ 0, contradicting the hypothesis.

Since χ = −1, the hypotheses of 2.3 hold: there is a nonzero e : \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C. Since \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} is torsion-free of generic rank one and C is integral, the sequence

0 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} →ᵉ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C/e\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} → 0

is exact and \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C/e\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} has finite support. We have χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C/e\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) = χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C) − χ(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) = 0 − (−1) = 1, i.e. dim H⁰(C,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C/e\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) = 1: the ideal e(\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}) defines a point t of C, and \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} ≃ m(t).

φ\textunderscore{}e : C\textasciicircum{}reg → Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} : x ↦ φ(x) − φ(e)

is an isomorphism of S-schemes. We have φ\textunderscore{}e(x+y) = φ(x+y) − φ(e) = (φ(x) + φ(y) − φ(e)) − φ(e) = φ\textunderscore{}e(x) − φ\textunderscore{}e(y), so φ\textunderscore{}e is a group isomorphism. For x ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(S) and y ∈ C(S), x+y = φ\textunderscore{}e(x) + y, which proves (ii).

If C/S is stable, (C,+) is a generalized elliptic curve by (ii). If +′ is another generalized elliptic-curve law with identity e and x ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(S), translation by x +′ acts trivially on Pic⁰\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} (1.13). Thus, for y ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(S) and z ∈ C(S),

x +′ (y+z) = x +′ (φ\textunderscore{}e(y) + z) = φ\textunderscore{}e(y) + (x +′ z) = y + (x +′ z).

Putting z = e gives x +′ y = y+x = x+y, and +′ agrees with + on C\textasciicircum{}reg × C\textasciicircum{}reg. By schematic closure, +′ = + everywhere, proving (iv).

(2.7.3)        0 → m(t) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C → t\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S → 0

deg\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}\}(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(-2(e))) = -2,

so

χ(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(-2(e))) = -2,    and    χ(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},t\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}) = 1.

Thus indeed

Corollary 2.8. Let p : C → S be a generalized elliptic curve over S. The map

x ↦ -x : C\textasciicircum{}reg → C\textasciicircum{}reg

extends to an automorphism of C/S.

The required extension is unique because C\textasciicircum{}reg is schematically dense in C. The problem is therefore local for the fpqc topology. It is trivial over the open subset where C is smooth. Let s̄ be a geometric point of S, localized at s ∈ S, such that C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is an n-gon. Near this point C\textunderscore{}n is finite and flat and, locally for the fppf topology, admits a section t whose multiples meet the various components of C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}. Near s, t generates a subgroup H of C\textunderscore{}n isomorphic to ℤ/n. The group H\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} acts freely on C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}, and C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}/H is a 1-gon. On a neighborhood of s we then have:

a) H acts freely on C, and C/H is a generalized elliptic curve over S.

b) C/H has geometrically irreducible fibers (apply 1.15 to C/H).

c) C is a finite étale covering of C/H.

We know that C/H admits an involution x ↦ -x (2.7(iii)). We then proceed as in 1.17 to lift this involution to an involution of C.

3. Construction of generalized elliptic curves.

This section will not be used later in this article, and we recommend that the reader omit it on a first reading.

Here we prove Theorem 3.2 below.

3.1. Let p : C → S be a stable genus-one curve equipped with a section e ∈ C\textasciicircum{}reg(S). Let G be a commutative S-group scheme, flat and locally of finite presentation, acting on C. Consider the following conditions.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0050-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0050-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0051}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 51}{D018-P0051-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 51; collected-paper page 544; original folio 193.}\par\smallskip
(3.1.1)        G acts trivially on Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

(3.1.2)        For every geometric point s̄ of S, G(s̄) acts transitively on the set of irreducible components of C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}.

In the important special case where G is the constant group scheme ℤ, the action ζ of G is defined by the automorphism g = ζ(1) of C, and (3.1.1) and (3.1.2) become

(3.1.3)        g acts trivially on Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}

(3.1.4)        For every geometric point s̄ of S, g transitively permutes the irreducible components of S\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}.

Theorem 3.2. Let G act on C as in 3.1. If conditions (3.1.1) and (3.1.2) hold, there is on C a unique generalized-elliptic-curve structure with identity e for which G acts on C by translations. Moreover, every automorphism of C over S that commutes with G and satisfies (3.1.1) is a translation.

Plan of the proof. We shall prove the following assertions.

(A) Let s be a geometric point of S such that G\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} acting on C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} satisfies (3.1.1) and (3.1.2). There are an fppf neighborhood U of s̄ and an element g ∈ G(U) such that the action of g on C\textunderscore{}U satisfies (3.1.3) and (3.1.4).

(B) Theorem 3.2 for G = ℤ.

Let us first show that (A) and (B) imply 3.2. The questions are local for the fppf topology. If G\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} acting on C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} satisfies (3.1.1) and (3.1.2), we may therefore suppose that there is a g ∈ G(S) as in (A). By (B), there is a unique generalized-elliptic-curve structure with identity e for which g is a translation. Moreover, since G and g commute and G satisfies (3.1.1), G acts by translations. Finally, every automorphism satisfying (3.1.1) that commutes with G also commutes with g, and hence is a translation.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0051-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0051-A02.}
\end{flushright}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 52; collected-paper page 545; original folio 194.}\par\smallskip
Proof of (A). If C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is smooth, take for U the open subset of S over which C is smooth, and take g = e. Otherwise C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is an n-gon, and the image of G(s̄) in the group of automorphisms of the component diagram Γ(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}) has order at least n. By (3.1.1), G(s̄) acts by rotations on Γ(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}). Since the rotation group Aut⁺(Γ(C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\})) is cyclic of order n, there is a g\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} ∈ G(s) whose image generates this group, and g\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} transitively permutes the irreducible components of C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}. Locally for the fppf topology there is a section g of G inducing g\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}. We may apply 1.16 to g to verify (3.1.4) near s̄. That g satisfies 3.1.3 near s̄ follows from 1.13.

Proof of 3.2 for G = ℤ.

Let ζ be the action of G on C and let g = ζ(1). Applying 1.16 to g, we reduce to the case in which the geometric fibers of C are all smooth or n-gons (with n fixed). In this case gⁿe lies fiber by fiber in the same connected component of C as e, and gⁿ is covered by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3. Let h be an automorphism of C that commutes with g and satisfies (3.1.3). If, fiber by fiber, he lies in the same irreducible component of C as e, then h is of the form

x ↦ λ + x

for a section λ of Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/S\}.

Proof. We must have λ equal to the class of \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(he - e). Hypothesis 3.1.3 ensures that x ↦ λ + x commutes with g. Replacing h by h(x - λ), we reduce to the case he = e. Since h satisfies (3.1.3), for every section μ of Pic\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} we have

h(gᵏ(μ+e)) = gᵏ(μ+e).

Every section of C\textasciicircum{}reg is locally for the fppf topology of the form gᵏ(μ+e). Thus h acts trivially on C\textasciicircum{}reg, and hence on C.

Continuation of the proof of 3.2. We have

gⁿx = λ + x.

Choose μ such that λ = μ\textasciicircum{}\{-n\}, and put

g₁ = g ∘ (x ↦ μ + x).

Then g₁ⁿ = Id and g₁ defines an action of ℤ/n on C. This action is free, and the geometric fibers of C/(ℤ/n) are integral. On C/(ℤ/n) there is therefore a unique generalized-elliptic-curve structure with identity section e (2.7). On C there is a unique generalized-elliptic-curve structure with identity e for which g₁ is a translation


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0052-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0052-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0053}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 53}{D018-P0053-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 53; collected-paper page 546; original folio 195.}\par\smallskip
(cf. 1.17). By 3.3, for a section λ of Pic\textasciicircum{}o and for every generalized-elliptic-curve structure, (λ + e) + x equals λ + x. Thus the law obtained is also the unique one for which g is a translation. Finally, if h commutes with g and satisfies (3.1.3), locally there is a k such that hgᵏ is covered by 3.3, and h is a translation.

Remark 3.4. If C is a generalized elliptic curve over S, one can show that there is a unique homomorphism

u : Pic\textasciicircum{}[0]\textunderscore{}\{C/S\} → C\textasciicircum{}reg

such that, after every base change, for every section t of C\textasciicircum{}reg over S,

u(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}((t)-(e))) = t.

III. Representability theorems.

0. Introduction and notation.

In this chapter and those that follow, we define and study various algebraic stacks classifying generalized elliptic curves equipped with various additional structures.

0.1. Write \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} for the category fibered in groupoids over the category (Sch) of schemes defined as follows: \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}(S) is the category whose objects are generalized elliptic curves over S and whose morphisms are S-isomorphisms; the functor "inverse image by u : S → T" is

E ↦ E ×\textunderscore{}T S.

The category \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} is a stack over (Sch) for the fpqc topology (2.1), but it is not an algebraic stack.

0.2. Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}* be the following substack of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}: \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}* classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S such that, for every geometric point s̄ of S, the characteristic of k(s̄) does not divide the number of irreducible components of the geometric fiber C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}. The principal result 2.5 of this chapter is that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}}\textunderscore{}* is an algebraic stack. The representability theorems of the following chapters will follow from this technical theorem. We shall deduce it from M. Artin's general criteria. To apply them, the essential point will be to prove the effective prorepresentability of certain functors; this will be done in Section 1.

0.3. We adopt the following notational conventions.

a) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{M}} with a subscript denotes the algebraic stack classifying generalized elliptic curves subject to certain restrictions or equipped with additional structures of the type indicated by the subscript.

b) The superscript o denotes the open substack corresponding to elliptic curves in the strict sense.

c) The superscript h denotes the open substack obtained by removing the points corresponding to supersingular elliptic curves in characteristic p dividing n, where n depends on the context.

d) The superscript ∞ denotes the closed substack that is the image of the singular locus of the curve


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0054-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0054-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0055}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 55}{D018-P0055-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 55; collected-paper page 548; original folio 197.}\par\smallskip
universal.

e) A notation \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}[1/n] indicates either that a stack over ℤ[1/n] is being considered or, if \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}} has already been defined, denotes \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}} ⊗ ℤ[1/n].

f) The coarse moduli scheme defined by an algebraic stack (I.8) is denoted by the same symbol, with \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}} replaced by M.

We shall extend these notations to certain stacks over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}, not necessarily defined by a "moduli problem" stricto sensu.

1. A prorepresentability theorem.

1.1 Let Λ be a complete local ring with residue field k and let C\textunderscore{}o be a generalized elliptic curve over k. Assume that C\textunderscore{}o is smooth or is the base change to k of the standard generalized elliptic curve with n sides (II.1.9). Let D be the deformation functor of C\textunderscore{}o: for a complete local Noetherian Λ-algebra A with residue field k, D(A) is the set of isomorphism classes of generalized elliptic curves C/Spec(A), equipped with an isomorphism C ⊗\textunderscore{}A k ≃ C\textunderscore{}o. This section is devoted to the proof of the following theorem.

Theorem 1.2. Suppose that C\textunderscore{}o is smooth or that n is prime to the characteristic exponent p of k. Then:

(i) (C\textunderscore{}o,+) has no nonzero infinitesimal automorphisms.

(ii) The deformation functor D of (C\textunderscore{}o,+) is effectively prorepresentable.

(iii) D is prorepresented by a curve C over Spec(Λ[[t]]).

(iv) If C\textunderscore{}o is not smooth, t may be chosen so that t = 0 is the image of the non-smoothness subscheme.

The infinitesimal automorphisms of (C\textunderscore{}o,+) form the group Lie Aut(C\textunderscore{}o,+), so (i) follows from (II.1.10). It follows from (i) and [25] that D is prorepresentable.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0055-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0055-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0056}{}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 56; collected-paper page 549; original folio 198.}\par\smallskip
1.3. The case in which C\textunderscore{}o is irreducible.

In this case the deformation functor of (C\textunderscore{}o,+) is identified by II.2.7 with the functor D' of deformations of (C\textunderscore{}o,e), the pair consisting of the curve C\textunderscore{}o and the marked section e. This latter functor is effectively prorepresentable, since a formal scheme in complete curves is always projective and hence algebraizable. That D' satisfies (iii) follows from the two formulas

(1.3.1)        dim Ext¹(Ω¹\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e), \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}) = 1

(1.3.2)        dim Ext²(Ω¹\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e), \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}) = 0.

Indeed, Ext² is the group in which the obstructions to deformations live. If it vanishes, D' is prorepresented by a formal power-series algebra over Λ. Ext¹ is identified with D'(k[ε]/(ε²)) and determines the relative dimension.

Let us prove (1.3.1) and (1.3.2). Since ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\} is isomorphic to \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}, duality (I.2.2) gives

dim Extⁱ(Ω¹\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e), \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}) = dim H\textasciicircum{}\{1−i\}(Ω¹\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e)).

Formula (1.3.2) follows trivially. For (1.3.1), we may suppose that k is algebraically closed.

If C\textunderscore{}o is smooth, Ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e) ≃ ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e) ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e) is an invertible sheaf of degree 1, whence dim H⁰(C\textunderscore{}o, Ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e)) = 1 in this case.

Otherwise, the normalization π : C̃\textunderscore{}o → C\textunderscore{}o of C\textunderscore{}o is isomorphic to ℙ¹, and a local calculation shows that the canonical morphism

Ω\textunderscore{}\{C\textunderscore{}o\}(e) → π\textunderscore{}* Ω\textunderscore{}\{C̃\textunderscore{}o\}(e)

is surjective with a kernel of length one, concentrated at the unique singular point of C\textunderscore{}o. In this case the assertion follows from the long exact cohomology sequence: we use that

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0056-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0056-A02.}
\end{flushright}

H⁰(C, π\textunderscore{}* Ω\textunderscore{}C(e)) ≃ H⁰(ℙ¹, Ω\textunderscore{}\{ℙ¹\}(e)) = 0        (since deg\textunderscore{}\{ℙ¹\}(e) = −1).

This proves (iii).

Let us prove (iv). Here C\textunderscore{}o is the Néron 1-gon. The deformation theory of ordinary double points shows that there is a u ∈ Λ[[t]] such that u = 0 is the image of the non-smooth locus of C. By (II.1.15), over Spec(Λ[[t]]/(u)), C is isomorphic to the base change of the standard Néron 1-gon (1.1). The universal property of Λ[[t]] then implies that Spec(Λ[[t]]/(u)) is unramified over Spec(Λ), and hence that Λ[[t]] ≃ Λ[[u]]. This completes the proof for irreducible C\textunderscore{}o.

1.4 The general case.

We may suppose that C\textunderscore{}o is the standard Néron n-gon over k, obtained from ℙ¹ × ℤ/n by gluing. Let H\textunderscore{}o be the cyclic subgroup of order n in C\textunderscore{}o, the image of \{1\} × ℤ/n.

Let D'' be the deformation functor of (C\textunderscore{}o,+,H\textunderscore{}o): D''(A) is the set of isomorphism classes of systems (C,+,H), where (C,+) is a generalized elliptic curve over A and H is a subgroup of C\textasciicircum{}reg isomorphic to ℤ/n, equipped with an isomorphism of their special fiber with (C\textunderscore{}o,+,H\textunderscore{}o).

Lemma 1.4.1. We have D'' ≃ D.

This follows because C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}reg is finite étale over A (since n is prime to p).

1.4.2 For the rest of the proof we no longer suppose that n is prime to p (this hypothesis will be used only through 1.4.1). Let (C,+,H) be as above (over a complete local A). The group H acts freely on C (because H\textunderscore{}o acts freely on C\textunderscore{}o), so C/H (which exists because C is projective) is flat over A. The law + descends to the quotient, and (C/H,+) is a deformation of the Néron 1-gon (C\textunderscore{}o/H\textunderscore{}o,+). Writing D\textunderscore{}1 for the deformation functor of (C\textunderscore{}o/H\textunderscore{}o,+), we obtain


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0057-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0057-A02.}
\end{flushright}

in this way a morphism from D'' to D\textunderscore{}1.

Lemma 1.4.3. The morphism 1.4.2 from D'' to D\textunderscore{}1 is an isomorphism (here n is not assumed prime to p).

By EGA IV.18.1.2 when A is Artinian, together with the existence theorem in formal geometry, EGA III.5, when A is complete local, for every deformation (C,+) of (C\textunderscore{}o/H\textunderscore{}o,+) over A there exists one and only one deformation C of the curve C\textunderscore{}o over A, equipped with π\textunderscore{}o : C\textunderscore{}o → C\textunderscore{}o/H\textunderscore{}o. We then apply II.1.17.

Let us complete the proof of 1.2. By 1.4.1 and 1.4.3, D is isomorphic to D\textunderscore{}1, studied in 1.3, and therefore satisfies 1.2(ii) and (iii). Assertion (iv) follows from the analogous assertion for D\textunderscore{}1, because the image of the non-smoothness subscheme is the same for C and C/H (notation of 1.4.2).

2. Construction of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*.

Lemma 2.1. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}} (defined in 0.1) is a stack for the fpqc topology.

Proof. Let S' → S be an fpqc morphism and let p' : C' → S' be a generalized elliptic curve equipped with descent data. The subschemes S'\textunderscore{}n of S' defined in II.1.15 descend to subschemes S\textunderscore{}n of S (fpqc descent of closed subschemes). By localizing on S for the Zariski topology, we may therefore suppose that the geometric fibers of C' are smooth or have n irreducible components. The subscheme C'\textunderscore{}n of C' is then finite over S' (cf. II.1.20) and meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber of C'. The invertible sheaf ℒ = \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}\{C'\}(C'\textunderscore{}n) is relatively ample and is equipped with descent data. The assertion thus follows from descent for quasi-projective schemes (SGA 1, VIII.7.8 and SGA 1, VIII.5.2).

Recall that for a stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} over (Sch/S), the 1-morphisms ξ : X → \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} from an S-scheme X to \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} are identified with the objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(X) ([8]).


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0058-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0058-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 59; collected-paper page 552; original folio 201.}\par\smallskip
2.2. M. Artin proved a manageable criterion for showing that a functor is representable by an algebraic space ([2], [3]). The same proof gives the following criterion for verifying that a stack is algebraic.

Theorem 2.3 (M. Artin). Let S be a scheme of finite type over a field or over an excellent Dedekind ring. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} be a category fibered in groupoids over (Sch/S).

\ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} is an algebraic stack locally of finite type over S if (and only if):

(0) \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} is a stack for the étale topology.

(1) \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} is locally of finite presentation; that is, for every filtered projective system of affine S-schemes Spec(A\textunderscore{}i), the canonical functor

lim→\textunderscore{}i \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(Spec(A\textunderscore{}i)) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(lim←\textunderscore{}i Spec(A\textunderscore{}i))

defines an equivalence of categories.

(2) Let ξ,η ∈ Ob(\ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(X)) be two 1-morphisms from an S-scheme X of finite type over S to \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}. Then Isom(X; ξ,η) is an algebraic space locally of finite type over S.

(3) Let k\textunderscore{}o be an \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S-field of finite type (that is, Spec(k\textunderscore{}o) is of finite type over S), and let ξ\textunderscore{}o be a 1-morphism from Spec(k\textunderscore{}o) to \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}. There exist a complete local ring R, a morphism u from the spectrum of a finite separable extension k'\textunderscore{}o of k\textunderscore{}o into the closed point s of Spec(R), and a commutative diagram

Spec(k'\textunderscore{}o)  →  Spec(k\textunderscore{}o) |              | u |              | ξ\textunderscore{}o ↓              ↓ Spec(R)   ──ξ──→ \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}

with ξ formally étale at s.

(4) If ξ is a 1-morphism from an S-scheme X of finite type into \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} and ξ is formally étale at a point x of finite type over S, then ξ is formally étale at every point of finite type over S in an open neighborhood of x.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0059-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0059-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0060}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 60}{D018-P0060-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 60; collected-paper page 553; original folio 202.}\par\smallskip
Remarks 2.4.

(i) If the residue fields of finite type of S are perfect, then, to establish that \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} is an algebraic stack, one may replace (0)-(4) by (0), (1), (2), (3'), and (4), where (3') is the following condition.

(3') Let s ∈ S and let k\textunderscore{}o be a finite extension of k(s). Suppose that u : Spec(k\textunderscore{}o) → S is of finite type. By hypothesis k(s) is then perfect and k\textunderscore{}o/k(s) is separable. There is therefore a unique complete local ring Λ(k\textunderscore{}o) with residue field k\textunderscore{}o, equipped with a formally étale morphism ū : Spec(Λ(k\textunderscore{}o)) → S extending u.

For ξ\textunderscore{}o ∈ Ob \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(k\textunderscore{}o), write Def(ξ\textunderscore{}o) for the following category over the dual of Ĉ\textunderscore{}\{Λ(k\textunderscore{}o)\}: for A ∈ Ob Ĉ\textunderscore{}\{Λ(k\textunderscore{}o)\}, an object of Def(ξ\textunderscore{}o)(A) is an object ξ of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(A), equipped with an isomorphism ξ\textunderscore{}o →̃ (the image of ξ in \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(k\textunderscore{}o)). Condition (3') is the conjunction of (3'a) and (3'b):

(3'a) The objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} have no infinitesimal automorphisms; more precisely, for k\textunderscore{}o and ξ\textunderscore{}o as above, and for ξ'\textunderscore{}o the pullback of ξ\textunderscore{}o to k\textunderscore{}o[ε] (ε² = 0), one has Aut(ξ'\textunderscore{}o) →̃ Aut(ξ\textunderscore{}o). This condition, together with (2), implies that the objects of Def(ξ\textunderscore{}o)(A) have no nontrivial automorphisms.

(3'b) For k\textunderscore{}o and ξ\textunderscore{}o as above, the functor

A → the set of isomorphism classes in Def(ξ\textunderscore{}o)(A)

is effectively prorepresentable (by a complete local ring R and ξ ∈ \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(R) reducing to ξ\textunderscore{}o).

It even suffices to verify (3'b) after extension of scalars to a finite extension of k\textunderscore{}o.

(ii) If S is of finite type over a field or over an excellent Dedekind ring having infinitely many points, and if the complete local rings R that occur in condition (3'b) are all normal and of the same Krull dimension, condition (4) may be omitted.

(For a reader familiar with [2], (i) is easy to prove. For (ii), see [2], Thm. 3.9).

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0060-A01.png}}

The standard Néron n-gon defines a morphism

f\textunderscore{}n : Spec(ℤ[1/n]) → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}*.

Theorem 2.5.

(i) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* (defined in 0.2) is an algebraic stack smooth over Spec(ℤ).

(ii) The image \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}∞ of the non-smoothness scheme of the universal generalized elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* is the union of the images of f\textunderscore{}n (n ≥ 1).

Proof. To prove that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* is an algebraic stack, we use criterion 2.3 (with S = Spec(ℤ)). Let us review conditions (0) through (4).

(0) follows from 2.1.

(1) follows from standard arguments in EGA IV.8.8.

(2) Let X be an S-scheme of finite type and let (p₁ : C₁ → X,+), (p₂ : C₂ → X,+) be two generalized elliptic curves. Let us prove that Isom\textunderscore{}X((C₁,+),(C₂,+)) is representable. This functor is an fpqc sheaf (2.1), so the problem is local. We may therefore suppose that the geometric fibers of C₁ are smooth or n-sided, for a suitable n, and that there is a φ : ℤ/n ↪ C₁ whose image meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber of C₁. The morphism f ↦ f∘φ(1) maps Isom into the open subscheme of C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}reg consisting of those g for which the g\textasciicircum{}k meet all components of the geometric fibers. The following assertion implies that this morphism is relatively representable and hence that Isom is representable.

(*) For C\textunderscore{}i with smooth or n-sided fibers, equipped with H\textunderscore{}i ⊂ C\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}reg, with H\textunderscore{}i isomorphic to ℤ/n and meeting all components of all geometric fibers (i=1,2), Isom((C₁,H₁,+),(C₂,H₂,+)) is representable.

By II 1.17, the morphism

Isom((C₁,H₁,+),(C₂,H₂,+)) → Isom((C₁/H₁,e),(C₂/H₂,e))

is representable by an étale morphism, while the theory of the Hilbert functor implies that Isom((C₁/H₁,e),(C₂/H₂,e)) is representable.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0061-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0061-A02.}
\end{flushright}

(3') Let k\textunderscore{}o be a Spec(ℤ)-field of finite type; it is therefore a finite field.

(3')a) follows from 1.2(i).

(3')b) follows from 1.2(ii).

(4) By 1.2(iii), the deformation functors are prorepresented by regular rings of dimension 2. By 2.4(ii), condition (4) is verified automatically.

The smoothness of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* follows from its formal smoothness in 1.2(iii). The second assertion of 2.5 follows from II 1.15 and 1.2(iv).

Remark 2.6. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* is horribly non-separated! It will follow from (IV.2.2) that the open substack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S with integral geometric fibers is proper and smooth over ℤ. The "catch-all" \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* will be useful to us for compactifying moduli of elliptic curves with level structure.

The open substack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n)[1/n] of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*[1/n] classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, whose geometric fibers are smooth or Néron n-gons is likewise proper and smooth over Spec(ℤ[1/n]). It is, however, less natural than \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n].

IV. Level structures.

In this chapter we define and study the moduli schemes of elliptic curves whose n-division points are equipped with additional structures. We study in detail the structure at infinity of these schemes. Their reduction modulo p, for p∣n, will not be considered seriously until the following chapter.

1. Contractions.

1.1. Let p : C → S be a stable curve of genus one, and let H ⊂ C\textasciicircum{}reg be a finite locally free subscheme over S of the smooth locus of C. Fiber by fiber, one verifies that H is automatically a Cartier divisor on C. We suppose that the fibers of H are nonempty.

Proposition 1.2. There exists a unique S-scheme C̄, equipped with u : C → C̄, such that

(a) C̄ is a stable curve of genus one over S;

(b) for every geometric point s̄ of S, C̄\textunderscore{}s̄ is obtained from C\textunderscore{}s̄ by contracting to a point each irreducible component of C\textunderscore{}s̄ that does not meet H\textunderscore{}s̄.

A. Existence. A limit argument reduces us to the case in which S is Noetherian. For every s̄ and every n > 0,

H⁰(C\textunderscore{}s̄, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(−nH\textunderscore{}s̄)) = 0.

By duality (I 2.2 and II 1.2), it follows that

H¹(C\textunderscore{}s̄, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH\textunderscore{}s̄)) = 0.

By EGA III 7.8, the p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH) for n > 0 are locally free and their formation is compatible with every base change. The same assertion holds for n = 0 (II.1.6). Let G be the homogeneous algebra

G = ⊕\textunderscore{}\{n>0\} p\textunderscore{}* \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH).

When C\textunderscore{}s̄ is irreducible, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(H\textunderscore{}s̄) is ample, and G\textunderscore{}s̄ is generated by

⊕\textunderscore{}\{n≤A\} H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH\textunderscore{}s̄)),

where A is independent of s.

Suppose that C\textunderscore{}s̄ is a Néron polygon, and let C̄\textunderscore{}s̄ be the Néron polygon obtained from it by contracting to a point every irreducible component that


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0063-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0063-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0064}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 64}{D018-P0064-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 64; collected-paper page 557; original folio 206.}\par\smallskip
does not meet H. One verifies that

H⁰(C̄\textunderscore{}s̄, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH̄\textunderscore{}s̄)) →̃ H⁰(C\textunderscore{}s̄, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH\textunderscore{}s̄)).

On C̄\textunderscore{}s̄, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH̄\textunderscore{}s̄) is ample, and Ḡ\textunderscore{}s̄ is generated by

⊕\textunderscore{}\{n≤B\} H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH\textunderscore{}s̄)),

where B is independent of s.

The homogeneous algebra G = ⊕\textunderscore{}n p\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(nH) is therefore of finite type, and its homogeneous spectrum C̄ is proper and flat over S, with formation compatible with every base change. The natural morphism u : C → C̄ satisfies (a) and (b).

B. Uniqueness. Let

u C ───────→ C̄ ╲ p      / p̄ ↘      ↙ S

satisfy (i) and (ii). Fiber by fiber, one verifies that u induces an isomorphism

u⁻¹(C̄\textasciicircum{}reg) →̃ C\textasciicircum{}reg       (EGA IV 17.8.2).

The arguments of A show that \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C̄(H) is ample and that Ḡ = ⊕ p̄\textunderscore{}*\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}C̄(nH) is flat and commutes with every base change. Fiber by fiber one verifies that Ḡ →̃ G and that C̄ = Sph(Ḡ).

Proposition 1.3. Let p : C → S be a generalized elliptic curve whose geometric fibers are smooth or are nm-gons. There exists a unique generalized elliptic curve p̄ : C̄ → S, equipped with u : C → C̄, such that

(i) The geometric fibers of C̄ are smooth or n-gons. C̄\textunderscore{}s̄ is obtained from C\textunderscore{}s̄ by contracting to a point the irreducible components of C\textunderscore{}s̄ that are the closures of those connected components of C\textunderscore{}s̄\textasciicircum{}reg whose order in π₀(C\textunderscore{}s̄\textasciicircum{}reg) does not divide n.

(ii) The following diagram is commutative:

u⁻¹(C̄\textasciicircum{}reg) ×\textunderscore{}S C  ──+──→  C │ u                    │ u ↓                      ↓ C̄\textasciicircum{}reg ×\textunderscore{}S C̄   ──+──→  C̄

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0064-A03.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0064-A03.}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 65; collected-paper page 558; original folio 207.}\par\smallskip
Locally for the fppf topology on S, there exists an H ⊂ C as in 1.1 that meets exactly those irreducible components of the C\textunderscore{}s̄ that we do not wish to contract; one may take H = C\textunderscore{}n, which is finite and flat by (II.1.20). By 1.2, there exists a unique stable curve of genus one C̄, equipped with u : C → C̄, that satisfies (i). The subgroup u⁻¹(C̄\textasciicircum{}reg) of C\textasciicircum{}reg acts on C̄ by transport of structure; since u⁻¹(C̄\textasciicircum{}reg) →̃ C\textasciicircum{}reg, this gives the desired generalized elliptic-curve structure on C̄.

Example 1.4. Let C/S be a generalized elliptic curve and apply 1.3 with n = 1. Locally on S, the hypothesis of 1.3 is satisfied for a suitable m (II.1.15). Hence there exists a unique generalized elliptic curve c(C) over S, with irreducible geometric fibers, equipped with u : C → c(C), such that u induces an isomorphism

(1.4.1)        (C\textasciicircum{}reg)\textasciicircum{}o →̃ c(C)\textasciicircum{}reg.

According to the proof of 1.3.4, the curve c(C) is in fact uniquely determined by condition (i) of 1.3, which follows from (1.4.1), while the generalized-curve structure is uniquely determined by the neutral section (II.2.7), implicit in 1.4.1.

1.5. Let S be the spectrum of a complete discrete valuation ring with algebraically closed residue field, and let η and s be its generic and special points. For every finite extension k(η') of k(η), write (S',η',s') for the normalization trait of S in k(η'). Let C\textunderscore{}η be an elliptic curve over η. We say that C\textunderscore{}η has stable reduction if the minimal model of C\textunderscore{}η over S is a stable curve of genus one.

Proposition 1.6. (i) There exists a finite extension k(η') of k(η) such that the curve C\textunderscore{}η' = C\textunderscore{}η ⊗\textunderscore{}η η' has stable reduction over η'.

(ii) If C\textunderscore{}η has stable reduction, the minimal model C̃ of C\textunderscore{}η over S has a unique generalized elliptic-curve structure extending that of C\textunderscore{}η.

(iii) If, in addition, the points of order n of C\textunderscore{}η are defined over k(η), then either the minimal model C̃ of C\textunderscore{}η is smooth or C̃\textunderscore{}s is an m-gon, with n∣m, and there


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0065-A02.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0065-A02.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0066}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 66}{D018-P0066-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 66; collected-paper page 559; original folio 208.}\par\smallskip
exists a generalized elliptic curve C over S whose special fiber is smooth or an n-gon, together with an isomorphism α from its generic fiber to C\textunderscore{}η.

(iv) The pair (C,α) is unique up to a unique isomorphism.

Proof. (i) This is a theorem of Néron and Kodaira ([4] or [20] or [14]).

(ii) The universal property of the Néron model ensures that e ∈ C\textunderscore{}η(η) extends to a section of C̃\textasciicircum{}reg over S. If u ∈ C̃\textasciicircum{}reg(S), the translation u+ on C\textunderscore{}η extends by transport of structure to an automorphism of C̃; similarly, x → −x extends to C̃.

Let U be the largest open subset of C̃ such that the group law + of C\textunderscore{}η extends to + : U × C → C. It is enough to prove that U = C̃\textasciicircum{}reg. Once this is established, the identities expressing that + is a generalized elliptic-curve structure follow from the schematic density of C\textunderscore{}η in C̃. For v ∈ C̃\textasciicircum{}reg(S), U is stable under translation by v: extend + to v+U by (v+u) + x = v+(u+x). Since U is also stable under étale localization on S, as follows by descent and the compatibility of minimal-model formation with étale localization on S, it is enough to show that U\textunderscore{}s ≠ ∅.

Let x be a maximal, hence generic, point of C̃\textunderscore{}s, and let S₁ be the trait given by the spectrum of the local ring of C̃ at x. Since C̃ is smooth over S at x, C̃₁ = C̃ ×\textunderscore{}S S₁ is again a minimal model over S₁. Let u ∈ C₁(S) be defined by the inclusion S₁ ↪ C̃. By transport of structure, translation by u on the generic fiber of C̃₁ extends to an automorphism of C₁, whence

+ : S₁ ×\textunderscore{}S C̃ → C̃.

Writing S₁ as the projective limit of the neighborhoods of x, one finds that the morphism + : C\textunderscore{}η × C\textunderscore{}η → C\textunderscore{}η extends to + : U ×\textunderscore{}S C̃ → C̃, with x ∈ U.

(iii) We know that C̃\textunderscore{}n is quasi-finite and flat. By the hypothesis and the universal property of the Néron model, all geometric points of (C\textunderscore{}η̄)\textunderscore{}n come from sections of C̃\textunderscore{}n over S. This group is therefore finite over S, and (C̃\textunderscore{}s)\textunderscore{}n has rank n². Thus the special fiber C̃\textunderscore{}s is smooth or an m-gon, with n∣m (cf. II 1.13), and 1.3 is applied to obtain C by contraction.

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0066-A02.png}}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 67; collected-paper page 560; original folio 209.}\par\smallskip
(iv) Let C be a generalized elliptic curve over S, with generic fiber C\textunderscore{}η, and suppose that C\textunderscore{}s is smooth or an n-gon. At a non-smooth point u of C, the formal completion of C is isomorphic to the completion at the origin of the affine curve in A\textunderscore{}S² with equation xy = t\textasciicircum{}k, for a suitable k. By homogeneity, k does not depend on the point chosen. For k > 1, C is singular at u. These singularities are resolved by repeatedly blowing up the singular points, a process that replaces u by k−1 projective lines:

u ╱╲ ╱  ╲       gives       ╱╲ ┈ ┈ ┈ ╱╲

(cf. [8]). Let C̃ be the resolved curve. There is no exceptional curve of the first kind contained in C̃\textunderscore{}s, so C̃ is the minimal model of C\textunderscore{}η. The curve C is smooth if and only if the minimal model C̃ of C\textunderscore{}η is smooth, in which case C = C̃. Otherwise C̃\textunderscore{}s is a Néron nk-gon, and C is obtained from it by contraction in the manner used in the existence proof.

2. Level-n structures.

2.1. Let n be an integer. In this section we consider only schemes S on which n is invertible.

In this section, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n) denotes the algebraic stack over ℤ[1/n] classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S whose geometric fibers are smooth or Néron n-gons. By (II.1.15), \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n) is an open substack of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}* and hence an algebraic stack, smooth over ℤ[1/n] by (III.2.5). Let f\textunderscore{}n be the section of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n) defined by curve 1.9 over ℤ[1/n].

Proposition 2.2. The algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n) is proper and smooth over ℤ[1/n]. Its locus at infinity \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n)\textasciicircum{}∞ = \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n) ∩ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}∞ is the image of f\textunderscore{}n.

The second assertion is included only as a reminder. It follows from III.2.5 and implies that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n)\textasciicircum{}o is dense in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n). Properness then follows from 1.6 and the valuative criterion for properness [8].


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0067-A03.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0067-A03.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0068}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 68}{D018-P0068-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 68; collected-paper page 561; original folio 210.}\par\smallskip
2.3. For C/S of the type under consideration, C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}reg is finite étale of rank n² (II.1.20), hence locally isomorphic to (ℤ/n)² in the étale topology. A level-n structure on C is an isomorphism

α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)².

Definition 2.4. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is the following algebraic stack over ℤ[1/n]: for a scheme S on which n is invertible, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n](S) is the category of generalized elliptic curves C/S whose geometric fibers are smooth or Néron n-gons, equipped with a level-n structure.

It is clear that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is finite étale and representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n), and is in fact a principal homogeneous space under GL(2,ℤ/n). We write \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] for the inverse image of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}(n)\textasciicircum{}∞, the image of the non-smoothness subscheme of the universal curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n]. The following theorem follows immediately from 2.2.

Theorem 2.5. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is proper and smooth over ℤ[1/n], and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] is finite étale over ℤ[1/n].

2.6. Recall that an algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} “is” an algebraic space when the objects it classifies have no automorphisms. More precisely, if for every algebraically closed field k the objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(k) have no nontrivial automorphisms, then

(a) for every scheme S, the objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(S) have no nontrivial automorphisms;

(b) the functor S ↦ (the set of isomorphism classes in \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}(S)) is representable by an algebraic space.

Theorem 2.7. If n ≥ 3, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is an algebraic space.

Let C be a generalized elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k, either smooth or a Néron n-gon. One must prove that an automorphism σ of C that is trivial on C\textunderscore{}n is the identity. When C is an elliptic curve, apply ([26], appendix to Exposé 17). When C is a Néron polygon, II.1.10 shows that Aut(C) acts faithfully on C\textunderscore{}n.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0068-A03.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0068-A03.}
\end{flushright}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 69; collected-paper page 562; original folio 211.}\par\smallskip
Variant 2.8. This argument, together with III.2.1, also immediately implies that the functor

F\textunderscore{}n : S ↦ (the set of isomorphism classes of generalized elliptic curves over S equipped with a level-n structure)

is a sheaf for the fpqc topology. The arguments of III.2.5 then show that this functor satisfies M. Artin's representability criterion. To prove that F\textunderscore{}n is representable by an algebraic space for n ≥ 3, it is therefore unnecessary first to generalize M. Artin's criterion to algebraic stacks.

Corollary 2.9. If n ≥ 3, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is a projective smooth scheme over ℤ[1/n].

Indeed, a regular algebraic space in curves over ℤ is always quasi-projective, and hence a scheme. Here is a more constructive proof: by transcendental methods (cf. §5), one verifies that after extension of scalars to ℂ, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞ meets every irreducible component of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n. By specialization it follows that the divisor \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞ meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n; here one uses that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n is proper and smooth. Consequently \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞) is a relatively ample invertible sheaf.

3. Level-H structures.

3.1. Let n be an integer and H a subgroup of GL(2,ℤ/n). Let S be a scheme on which n is invertible and C an elliptic curve over S. For every S-scheme T, let F(T) be the set of level-n structures on C\textunderscore{}T, the inverse image of C over T. The functor F is represented by a finite étale cover of S that is a principal homogeneous space under GL(2,ℤ/n). Let F\textunderscore{}H be the sheaf for the étale topology generated by the presheaf

T ↦ F\textunderscore{}H(T) = F(T)/H.

(Since H acts on the left, it might be better to write H\textbackslash{}F(T).) In canonical language,

F\textunderscore{}H = Hom(C\textunderscore{}n,(ℤ/n)²)/H.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0069-A03.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0069-A03.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0070}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 70}{D018-P0070-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 70; collected-paper page 563; original folio 212.}\par\smallskip
This functor is represented by a finite étale cover of S.

A level-H structure on C is an element of F\textunderscore{}H(S). An isomorphism α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)² defines a level-H structure, and two such isomorphisms define the same structure if and only if, locally on S, there is a g ∈ H such that gα = β. This g is uniquely determined and hence constant on each connected component of S. One should keep in mind that a level-H structure does not in general arise from an α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)². It arises from such an α only locally for the étale topology. For example, when H = GL(2,ℤ/n), C has exactly one level-H structure; it comes from an α only if C\textunderscore{}n is isomorphic to (ℤ/n)² over S.

Definition 3.2. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is the following algebraic stack: for a scheme S on which n is invertible, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n](S) is the category of elliptic curves C/S equipped with a level-H structure, with isomorphisms as morphisms.

It is clear that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is finite étale and locally representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/n].

Definition 3.3. The algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H is the normalization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n].

Theorem 3.4. (i) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H is proper over Spec(ℤ).

(ii) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] is smooth over Spec(ℤ[1/n]), and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is the complement of a substack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] that is finite étale over Spec(ℤ[1/n]).

Proof. (i) By definition, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H is finite and relatively representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1. Its properness therefore follows from that of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1.

(ii) The assertion follows from Abhyankar's lemma (SGA 1, XIII 5), which applies here because \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n] is smooth over ℤ[1/n], because \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] is a divisor of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n], smooth over ℤ[1/n] and with generic point of characteristic zero, and because \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is finite étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n] − \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n].

In 6.7 we shall give a “modular” proof of 3.4.

For H = \{e\}, we set \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n = \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H. This notation is justified by the following proposition.

Proposition 3.5. For H = \{e\}, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] is the stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] of 2.4.

Construction 1.4 defines a morphism of stacks

c : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n] : (C,α) ↦ c(C).

Since \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is normal, indeed smooth over ℤ[1/n], and proper over ℤ[1/n], and since plainly \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n], it is enough to prove that c is finite and locally representable. Its fibers are plainly finite. To test local representability, it therefore suffices to verify the following (cf. 2.6).

Lemma 3.5.1. Let k be an algebraically closed field, let C be an object of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n](k), and let c(C) be its image in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1(k). The natural map i : Aut(C) → Aut(c(C)) is injective.

For n ≥ 3, Aut(C) is trivial (cf. 2.7). For n = 2, Aut(C) = \{±1\}. Finally, for n = 1, i is the identity.

3.6. Let n and m be integers with n∣m. Let H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/n), and let H' be its inverse image in GL(2,ℤ/nm). For an elliptic curve E over S, a level-nm structure

E\textunderscore{}nm ──α'→̃ (ℤ/nm)² │ x\textasciicircum{}m            │ reduction ↓                ↓ E\textunderscore{}n  ──α→̃  (ℤ/n)²

defines a level-n structure α and makes the displayed square commute.

When nm is invertible on S, this construction defines an isomorphism from the sheaf of H'-structures on E to the sheaf of H-structures. Thus

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H'\textasciicircum{}o[1/nm] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n][1/nm].

Since \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is finite étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/n], it is the normalization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H'\textasciicircum{}o[1/nm]. It follows that

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H' ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H.

Thus the stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H depends only on the open subgroup K of GL(2,ℤ̂) that is the inverse image of H. We shall sometimes denote it by \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K.

3.7. Let n and m be relatively prime integers, let H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/n), and let I ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/m). Let K and L be the inverse images of H and I in GL(2,ℤ̂). Reduction modulo n and m induces an isomorphism

GL(2,ℤ/nm) →̃ GL(2,ℤ/n) × GL(2,ℤ/m).

Via this isomorphism, K ∩ L is the inverse image of H × I ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/n) × GL(2,ℤ/m).

Construction 3.8. With the preceding notation,

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L\textasciicircum{}o[1/m] →̃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{K∩L\}\textasciicircum{}o[1/nm].

Let C/S be an elliptic curve. The maps x → x\textasciicircum{}m and x → x\textasciicircum{}n define an isomorphism

(3.8.1)        C\textunderscore{}nm →̃ C\textunderscore{}n × C\textunderscore{}m.

If ᾱ (respectively β̄) is a level-H (respectively level-I) structure on C, locally represented by α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)² (respectively β : C\textunderscore{}m →̃ (ℤ/m)²), define the level-H × I structure ᾱ × β̄ on C to be the class of the isomorphism α × β that makes the diagram commute:

C\textunderscore{}nm  ──α × β→̃  (ℤ/nm)² │ (3.8.1)          │ reduction ↓                  ↓ C\textunderscore{}n × C\textunderscore{}m ──(α,β)→̃ (ℤ/n)² × (ℤ/m)².

The isomorphism in 3.8 is defined by

(C,ᾱ,β̄) ↦ (C,ᾱ × β̄).

Proposition 3.9. With the notation of 3.7, the isomorphism of 3.8 extends to

(3.9.1)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L →̃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{K∩L\}.

Proof. It is enough to prove that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L is normal. We prove this over ℤ[1/m]; over ℤ[1/n] the argument is symmetric. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L[1/m] is smooth over ℤ and hence flat over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 by (I.7.1). Thus \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L[1/m] is flat over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K and Cohen-Macaulay by (I.7.2 and I.7.1). Moreover \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L\textasciicircum{}o[1/m] is étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1, so \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L\textasciicircum{}o[1/m], being étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K, is normal.

By Abhyankar's lemma, the ramification of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K[1/n] over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n] (respectively of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L[1/m] over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/m]) is tame. The ramification indices for \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L are therefore relatively prime; in fact they divide n and m. Hence \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K[1/mn] ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}L[1/mn] is smooth, and Serre's criterion (I.7.2) completes the proof.

Corollary 3.9.2. If n is the product of two relatively prime integers and n ≥ 3, then \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n is a scheme.

Write n=n'n''. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n'] is a scheme because it is representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{n'\}[1/n]. Likewise \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n''] is a scheme; therefore \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n is a scheme.

Proposition 3.10. (i) The coarse space M\textunderscore{}H defined by \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H is proper and flat over Spec(ℤ).

(ii) It is the normalization of M\textunderscore{}1, which is isomorphic to ℙ¹ by VI.1.1, in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n].

(iii) One has M\textunderscore{}H=M\textunderscore{}n/H.

(iv) The geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}H[1/n] → Spec(ℤ[1/n]) are geometrically unibranch.

Proof. Properness of M\textunderscore{}H follows from properness of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H. The description in I.8.2 of the strict henselization of a local ring of M\textunderscore{}H, together with normality of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H, shows that M\textunderscore{}H is normal. Similarly M\textunderscore{}H is flat over ℤ. The finite map \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 induces a finite map M\textunderscore{}H→M\textunderscore{}1, and (ii) follows from (i) and normality.

and consequently M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n]=M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o[1/n]/H. Assertion (iii) now follows from (ii).

The map

(M\textunderscore{}n ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p)/H → (M\textunderscore{}n/H) ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p = M\textunderscore{}H ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p

3.11. The category of elliptic curves up to isogeny over a scheme S is obtained from the category of elliptic curves by inverting isogenies. Writing E⊗ℚ for the elliptic curve up to isogeny over S underlying E/S, one has

Hom\textunderscore{}S(E⊗ℚ,F⊗ℚ)=Hom\textunderscore{}S(E,F)⊗ℚ.

3.12. Let k be an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero. For every elliptic curve E over k, put

T̂(E)=lim← E\textunderscore{}n                 (isomorphic to ℤ̂²),

V̂(E)=T̂(E)⊗ℚ                (isomorphic to ℤ̂²⊗ℚ=(\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f)²).

The group of division points of E is canonically isomorphic to V̂(E)/T̂(E): to x∈(1/n)T̂(E)/T̂(E) one associates the image in E\textunderscore{}n of nx∈T̂(E).

An isogeny E→F induces an isomorphism V̂(E)→̃V̂(F). This permits one to define V̂(E\textunderscore{}o) for an elliptic curve E\textunderscore{}o up to isogeny. If f:E→F is an isogeny, then

Ker(f)=Ker(V̂(E)/T̂(E)→V̂(F)/T̂(F))=coker(T̂(E)→T̂(F)).

The assignment f↦T̂(F)⊂V̂(E) identifies isogenies with source E with the lattices T̂' containing T̂(E). It follows that the functor

from elliptic curves to elliptic curves E\textunderscore{}o up to isogeny equipped with a lattice T̂⊂V̂(E) is an equivalence of categories.

3.13. Let E\textunderscore{}o be an elliptic curve up to isogeny over k. A lattice T̂⊂V̂(E\textunderscore{}o) can be interpreted as a class, modulo GL(2,ℤ̂), of isomorphisms

β:V̂(E\textunderscore{}o)→(\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f)²;

to β one associates β⁻¹(ℤ̂²).

Let K⊂GL(2,ℤ̂) be the inverse image of H⊂GL(2,ℤ/n). If (E\textunderscore{}o,T̂) corresponds to an elliptic curve E, then a level-H structure on E gives the set of isomorphisms β:V̂(E\textunderscore{}o)→̃(\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f)² such that β⁻¹(ℤ̂²)=T̂ and

β\textunderscore{}n:E\textunderscore{}n=T̂/nT̂→̃ℤ̂²/nℤ̂²=(ℤ/nℤ)²

defines that level-H structure. The maps β form a left coset under K, and the set of elliptic curves with level-H structure (E,ᾱ) and E⊗ℚ=E\textunderscore{}o identifies with

K\textbackslash{}Isom(V̂(E\textunderscore{}o),(\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f)²).

3.14. Let g∈GL(2,\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f) satisfy gKg⁻¹⊂GL(2,ℤ̂). Identifying an elliptic curve with level-K structure, equivalently level-H structure, with (E\textunderscore{}o,β), where β∈K\textbackslash{}Isom(V̂(E\textunderscore{}o),(\ensuremath{\mathbb{A}}\textasciicircum{}f)²), the assignment (E\textunderscore{}o,β)↦(E\textunderscore{}o,gβ) associates to a curve of level K a curve of level gKg⁻¹.

These constructions remain meaningful over any scheme of characteristic zero and define

(3.14.1)        g:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ)→̃\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ),

together with an isomorphism [g] between the pullback by g of the universal curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ), considered up to isogeny, and the universal curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ), considered up to isogeny.

3.15. We now make the composite map explicit:

(3.15.1)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o⊗ℚ →̃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o⊗ℚ ──c→ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o⊗ℚ.

Proposition 3.16. The isomorphism of 3.14.1 extends to an isomorphism

g:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o→̃\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o.

The isomorphism [g] extends to an isomorphism

[g]:g\textasciicircum{}*(the elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o)⊗ℚ ≃ (the elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o)⊗ℚ.

cg:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o.

Let b be the order of B as in 3.15, and let I be the stack, relatively representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o, that represents the functor of finite locally free subgroup schemes of rank b of the universal elliptic curve E. The theory of Hilbert schemes shows that the projection I→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o is proper and representable. By the next lemma it is finite.

Lemma 3.17. Let E be an elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k and let b be an integer. There are only finitely many subgroup schemes of E of rank b.

Proof of 3.17. Proceed by induction on b.

(a) There are only finitely many étale subgroup schemes of E of rank b: they identify with the subgroups of order b in E\textunderscore{}b(k).

(b) If H⊂E is not étale, then k has characteristic p>0 and H contains Ker(F). Thus the non-étale subgroup schemes of E of rank b correspond to the subgroup schemes of rank b/p of E\textasciicircum{}(p)=E/Ker(F), and the induction hypothesis applies.

Proof of 3.16, continued. Construction 3.14 defines a section s over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ) of I×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o\}\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o.

By Zariski's Main Theorem and the normality of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o, this section extends over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o, defining a subgroup B of the pullback E, along c, of the universal elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o. The required extension cg is defined by E/B, and the isomorphism [g] is defined as in 3.15.

The resulting morphism cg is quasi-finite and representable. It therefore identifies \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o with an open substack of the normalization \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o in

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ) ──g→̃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o⊗Spec(ℚ),

and hence gives

g:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o↪\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}\textasciicircum{}o.

This morphism is an isomorphism, with inverse g⁻¹.

3.18. One can show that g extends to an isomorphism

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K→̃\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{gKg⁻¹\}.

We shall only prove the analogous result for the coarse schemes.

3.20. Let C be an elliptic curve over S, with n invertible on S. The e\textunderscore{}n pairing is well known to define an isomorphism

(3.20.1)        e\textunderscore{}n:Λ²C\textunderscore{}n→̃μ\textunderscore{}n.

det(α)⁻¹:ℤ/n→̃μ\textunderscore{}n,

that is, a primitive n-th root of unity

d(α)=det(α)⁻¹(1)

on S.

Let ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] be the ring of integers in the field of n-th roots of unity. The construction α↦d(α) defines a morphism

d:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o[1/n]→Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]).

This morphism extends to the normalization and defines

(3.20.2)        d:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n→Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]).

Similarly, a level-H structure α defines

d(α)∈Isom(ℤ/n,μ\textunderscore{}n)/det(H).

which defines

3.21. Work over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{(m)\}[1/m], with n dividing m. If C is the universal generalized elliptic curve, the e\textunderscore{}n pairing extends to an isomorphism

Λ²C\textunderscore{}n≃μ\textunderscore{}n.

4. Examples.

4.1. Let Γ\textunderscore{}o(n), Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n), Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n), and Γ(n) be the subgroups of GL(2,ℤ̂) formed by the matrices with entries a,b,c,d satisfying, respectively,

Γ\textunderscore{}o(n): c≡0 (mod n); Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n): c≡0 and a≡1 (mod n); Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n): c≡0 and a≡d≡1 (mod n); Γ(n): the matrix is congruent to the identity (mod n).

Each of these is the inverse image of a subgroup H of GL(2,ℤ/n).

4.2. For Γ\textunderscore{}o(n), H is the subgroup of GL(2,ℤ/n) that stabilizes ℤ/n×\{0\} in (ℤ/n)². If C is an elliptic curve over S and

When n is invertible on S, this gives an isomorphism between the sheaf of level-H structures on C and the sheaf of subgroup schemes of C locally isomorphic to ℤ/n. In other words:

Construction 4.3. The algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] classifies elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, equipped with a subgroup scheme A locally isomorphic to ℤ/n.

4.4. For C/S and A as in 4.3, the subgroup C\textunderscore{}n/A of the quotient curve C/A is locally isomorphic to ℤ/n. Hence there is a morphism

w:\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n]→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n], (C,A)↦w(C,A)=(C/A,C\textunderscore{}n/A).

Multiplication by n factors through an isomorphism C/C\textunderscore{}n→̃C. It yields an isomorphism

ww(C,A)=((C/A)/(C\textunderscore{}n/A),(C/A)\textunderscore{}n/(C\textunderscore{}n/A))=(C/C\textunderscore{}n,…)→̃(C,A),

and therefore w²≃Id: w is an involution. It is of the type described in 3.16 for the matrix with rows (0,1) and (n,0).

4.5. A more symmetric description is obtained by associating to (E,A) the isogeny E→E/A. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] also classifies isogenies a:E\textunderscore{}1→E\textunderscore{}2 with cyclic kernel of order n. If (E,A) defines a, then w(E,A) defines E\textunderscore{}2→E\textunderscore{}1/(E\textunderscore{}1)\textunderscore{}n; through the isomorphism of 4.4 between E\textunderscore{}1/(E\textunderscore{}1)\textunderscore{}n and E\textunderscore{}1, this is the Cartier dual of a. Thus

w(a:E\textunderscore{}1→E\textunderscore{}2)=(a\textasciicircum{}*:E\textunderscore{}2→E\textunderscore{}1).

4.6. Let C/S and A be as in 4.3. For each prime divisor p of n, let A\textunderscore{}p be the p-primary component of A, so that A=∏\textunderscore{}\{p|n\}A\textunderscore{}p, and let p\textasciicircum{}\{n(p)\} be the order of A\textunderscore{}p, so n=∏\textunderscore{}p p\textasciicircum{}\{n(p)\}. Put

w\textunderscore{}p(E,A)=(E/A\textunderscore{}p,(A+E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}\{n(p)\}\})/A\textunderscore{}p).

4.7. The subgroup Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n) of GL(2,ℤ̂) is the inverse image of the subgroup H of GL(2,ℤ/n) that fixes (1,0) in (ℤ/n)², or equivalently the homomorphism β:ℤ/n→(ℤ/n)², x↦(x,0). Reasoning as in 4.2 gives:

Construction 4.8. The algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n] classifies elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, equipped with an embedding β:ℤ/n↪C\textunderscore{}n, equivalently a section β(1) having exact order n everywhere.

4.9. The group Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n) is the inverse image of the subgroup H that fixes (1,0)∈(ℤ/n)² and the class of (0,1) in (ℤ/n)²/(ℤ/n×\{0\}). Thus \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n] classifies elliptic curves C/S with n invertible, equipped with β:ℤ/n↪C\textunderscore{}n and an isomorphism γ:ℤ/n→̃C\textunderscore{}n/Im(β). The exterior product gives the chain of isomorphisms

C\textunderscore{}n/Im(β)←̃(ℤ/n)⊗C\textunderscore{}n/Im(β)→̃\textasciicircum{}\{β\}Im(β)⊗C\textunderscore{}n/Im(β)→̃\textasciicircum{}\{Λ\}Λ²C\textunderscore{}n→̃\textasciicircum{}\{e\textunderscore{}n\}μ\textunderscore{}n.

Through it, choosing γ is equivalent to choosing an isomorphism ℤ/n→̃μ\textunderscore{}n, that is, a primitive n-th root of unity on S. Hence

Construction 4.10. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n]≃\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n]⊗ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n].

This is the fiber product over Spec(ℤ) of the forgetful morphism, defined by Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)⊂Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n), and the morphism d of (3.20.3).

Definition 4.11. The stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, equipped with a subgroup scheme A locally isomorphic to ℤ/n and meeting every irreducible component of every geometric fiber of C.

If an m-gon is classified by ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}, then m divides n. The map that forgets A therefore sends ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*. It is étale, and hence the source is a smooth algebraic stack.

4.12. As in 3.5, define the morphism that forgets A and contracts as in (1.3):

c:′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n]→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n].

Let k be algebraically closed and let C be an object of the source over k. The map Aut(C)→Aut(c(C)) is injective. This is trivial for smooth C. For an m-gon equipped with A⊂C, II.1.10 shows that x↦-x is the only nontrivial automorphism of (C,A). As in 3.4, ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] is proper over ℤ[1/n]; the argument of 3.5 yields:

Construction 4.13. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n]≃′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n].

Analogous arguments give:

Construction 4.14. The algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n] classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, equipped with an embedding β:ℤ/n↪C whose image meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber.

Because ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][1/n] is étale over ℤ[1/n], normalization in 4.10 also gives:

Construction 4.15. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ'\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n]≃\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}\{oo\}(n)\}[1/n]⊗ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n].

4.16. Let A be the subgroup of GL(2,ℤ/n) consisting of scalar matrices. Level-A structures will be called projective level-n structures. They play an important role in Ihara's work. More generally:

Definition 4.17. Let C/S be a generalized elliptic curve over S, with n invertible on S, and suppose that the singular geometric fibers of C are n-gons. A projective level-n structure on C is a section of

Isom(C\textunderscore{}n,(ℤ/n)²)/A.

Lemma 4.18. Let (C,α) be a generalized elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field, equipped with a projective level-n structure. If n≥2, the only nontrivial automorphism of C preserving α is x↦-x.

For an n-gon the lemma follows easily from II.1.10 and is true even for n=1. For smooth C it is a special case of the following lemma.

Lemma 4.19. Let n≥2 and let g be a finite-order automorphism of an abelian variety X over an algebraically closed field k. If g acts as a scalar on X\textunderscore{}n, then g²=1.

By hypothesis there is r∈ℤ/n such that g-r vanishes on X\textunderscore{}n. Hence g-r=n·h for some endomorphism h of X. If ζ is a root of the characteristic polynomial of g, then ζ is a root of unity and the algebraic integer ζ-r is divisible by n. Let R be the ring of integers of ℚ(ζ). The image of ζ in R/nR is therefore the image of r. Since R=ℤ[ζ], this is impossible unless ζ=±1. Since g is semisimple, the assertion follows.

Combining Lemma 4.18 for n-gons with the now familiar arguments gives:

Construction 4.20. For A as in 4.16, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}A[1/n] is the algebraic stack classifying generalized elliptic curves equipped with a projective level-n structure.

For (C,α) classified by \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}A[1/n] and n≥2, Lemma 4.18 shows that Aut(C,α) is constant. Hence, by (1.8):

Proposition 4.21. For A as in 4.16 and n≥2, the map

5. Transcendental theory (review).

5.1. Let E be an elliptic curve over ℂ. The exponential map defines an exact sequence of analytic groups

0→H\textunderscore{}1(E,ℤ)→\textasciicircum{}\{ι\}Lie(E)→\textasciicircum{}\{exp\}E(ℂ)→0.

If β:ℤ²→̃H\textunderscore{}1(E,ℤ) is a basis of E, put

z(β)=ι(β(1,0))/ι(β(0,1))∈ℂ-ℝ.

For ι\textunderscore{}1,ι\textunderscore{}2∈Lie(E), with ι\textunderscore{}2≠0, the quotient ι\textunderscore{}1/ι\textunderscore{}2 denotes the complex number λ satisfying ι\textunderscore{}1=λι\textunderscore{}2. If γ is the matrix with rows (a,c) and (b,d) in GL(2,ℤ), then

z(βγ)=(a z(β)+b)/(c z(β)+d).

5.2. The map x↦exp((1/n)ι(x)) induces an isomorphism

(5.2.1)        H\textunderscore{}1(E,ℤ)⊗ℤ/n→̃E\textunderscore{}n.

Put X\textasciicircum{}±=ℂ-ℝ, and let X\textasciicircum{}+ be the upper half-plane. If α:E\textunderscore{}n→̃(ℤ/n)² is a level-n structure, the class of

(z(β),α∘β), with z(β)∈X\textasciicircum{}± and α∘β∈GL(2,ℤ/n),

in X\textasciicircum{}±×GL(2,ℤ/n)/GL(2,ℤ) depends only on (E,α).
