6. Level-H structures: study at infinity.

This section will not be used later in the article. We recommend omitting it on a first reading.

Let H be a subgroup of GL(2,ℤ/n). We seek a modular interpretation of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] generalizing 3.5, 4.13, 4.14, 4.15, and 4.20. We shall first define modularly a stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n], proper and smooth over ℤ[1/n], with \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] as a dense open substack. As in 3.5 we define a finite morphism c:′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n]→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n]. We then prove the analogue of (3.5.1); the proof of 3.5 gives ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n]=\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n], the desired modular interpretation.

6.1. Let A be a group isomorphic to (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2, let A'⊂A be a subgroup isomorphic to ℤ/n, and let B contain A'. For a suitable divisor m of n, (A,A',B) is isomorphic to

((ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2,ℤ/n×\{0\},ℤ/n×mℤ/nℤ).

Let μ also be isomorphic to ℤ/n, let β\textunderscore{}0:Λ²A→̃μ, and denote by β\textunderscore{}1 its restriction to Λ²B.

The essential example is a generalized elliptic curve C over a complete trait S with points η and s. Let η̄ be the spectrum of an algebraic closure of k(η), and s̄ the spectrum of the corresponding algebraic closure of k(s). If n is invertible on S, C\textunderscore{}\{η̄\} is smooth, and C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is an m-sided Néron polygon with m|n, take

μ=μ\textunderscore{}n(η̄)≃μ\textunderscore{}n(s̄), A=C\textunderscore{}n(η̄), A'=C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o(s̄), B=(C\textunderscore{}s\textasciicircum{}\{reg\})\textunderscore{}n(s̄), and β\textunderscore{}0=e\textunderscore{}n.

The restriction β\textunderscore{}1 of β\textunderscore{}0 to Λ²B depends only on the special fiber C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}; see 3.21.

6.2. Let I be the set of isomorphisms α:A→(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2, and let Ī be the set of pairs (ᾱ,β), with ᾱ:B↪(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 and β:μ→̃ℤ/n, for which

Λ²ᾱ Λ²B  ─────────────→  Λ²(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 │ β\textunderscore{}1                    │ s ↓                        ↓ μ  ────────β────────→   ℤ/n

commutes. This is diagram (6.2.1).

Define c:I→Ī by α↦(α|\textunderscore{}B,Λ²α∘β\textunderscore{}0\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}). The group GL(2,ℤ/n) acts on I and Ī on the left by g.α=g∘α and g.(ᾱ,β)=(gᾱ,det(g).β). Let U be the subgroup of Aut(A) acting trivially on A' and A/A'. It acts on the right by α.u=α∘u and (ᾱ,β)u=(ᾱ∘(u|B),β). These actions are compatible with c.

Lemma 6.2.2. The map c is surjective.

The verification is left to the reader.

Subgroups U' of U correspond bijectively to subgroups B' of A containing A': U' maps to the invariant subgroup A\textasciicircum{}\{U'\}, while B' maps to the subgroup U(B') of U acting trivially on B'. Moreover Ī=I/U(B).

Let ā∈H\textbackslash{}Ī be the image of α∈I. The set of α'∈I mapping to ā is HαU(B). We call B adequate when U(B) is the subgroup of U stabilizing the image a of α in H\textbackslash{}I. Equivalently, in the square

I  ─────────────→  Ī=I/U(B) │                    │ ↓                    ↓ H\textbackslash{}I ─────────────→ H\textbackslash{}I/U(B),

(6.2.3) a is the unique element of H\textbackslash{}I mapping to ā, and (6.2.4) every u∈U satisfying āu=ā lies in U(B).

Let \{\}\textunderscore{}H J\textunderscore{}B be the set of a∈H\textbackslash{}Ī for which B is adequate. The preceding discussion gives:

Lemma 6.2.5. H\textbackslash{}I→̃\ensuremath{\amalg}\textunderscore{}\{A'⊂B'⊂A\}\{\}\textunderscore{}H J\textunderscore{}\{B'\}, the disjoint sum over B'.

6.4. Let S be a scheme on which n is invertible, and let C be a generalized elliptic curve over S whose geometric fibers are smooth or m-sided Néron polygons with m|n. We define an étale sheaf F on S, compatible with arbitrary base change, by the following intended values.

(a) If C is smooth over S, F(S) is the set of level-H structures on C over S.

(b) If S is the spectrum of an algebraically closed field and C is an m-sided Néron polygon with m|n, apply 6.3 with B=C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}\{reg\}, A'=C\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o, μ=μ\textunderscore{}n, and β\textunderscore{}1=e\textunderscore{}n. We require F(S)=\{\}\textunderscore{}H J\textunderscore{}B.

6.5. Let F\textunderscore{}1(S) be the set of systems (B,ᾱ,β) consisting of (a) a finite étale subgroup scheme B⊂C\textunderscore{}n and (b) maps ᾱ:B↪(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 and β:μ\textunderscore{}n→̃ℤ/n such that (c), locally on S, B contains a subgroup isomorphic to ℤ/n.

ᾱ Λ²B  ─────────────→  Λ²ℤ/n │ e\textunderscore{}n                    │ s ↓                        ↓ μ\textunderscore{}n ────────β────────→   ℤ/n

commutes.

(e) For every geometric point s̄ of S, if C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} is singular, (ᾱ,β) modulo H is of the type described in 6.4. In every case we require that if g∈GL(2,ℤ/n) satisfies (gᾱ\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},det(g).β\textunderscore{}\{s̄\})=(ᾱ\textunderscore{}\{s̄\},β\textunderscore{}\{s̄\}), then g∈H.

If C/S is smooth, a system (B,ᾱ,β) determines a unique level-H structure on C, locally represented by isomorphisms α:C\textunderscore{}n→̃(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 extending ᾱ and having determinant β. Local existence follows from (6.2.1), and uniqueness modulo H from condition (e).

Elements a,b∈F\textunderscore{}1(S) are equivalent when:

(1) over the open U⊂S where C is smooth, they define the same level-H structure on C\textunderscore{}U/U;

(2) for every geometric point s̄ with C\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} singular, a\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} and b\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} are H-conjugate. This implies étale-locally near s̄ that a and b are H-conjugate, so (1) holds over U×\textunderscore{}S V for an étale neighborhood V of s̄.

The sheaf F of level-H structures on C/S is generated by the presheaf F\textunderscore{}0 obtained by quotienting F\textunderscore{}1 by this equivalence relation. It is compatible with arbitrary base change, has the values prescribed in 6.4, and is represented by an étale S-scheme S'.

Definition 6.6. (i) A level-H structure on a generalized elliptic curve C/S as in 6.4 is an element of F(S).

(ii) The algebraic stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] over ℤ[1/n] classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S, with n invertible on S, whose geometric fibers are smooth or m-sided Néron polygons with m|n, equipped with a level-H structure.

Forgetting the level-H structure defines an étale morphism ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n]→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}*. Thus ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] is smooth over ℤ[1/n], and ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] is étale over ℤ[1/n]. Contracting as in 1.3 for n=1 and forgetting the level structure defines c:′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n]→\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1.

Theorem 6.7. (i) The stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] is proper and smooth over ℤ[1/n], and the image ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] of the non-smoothness subscheme of the universal curve is finite étale over ℤ[1/n].

(ii) The morphism c is finite and representable. Consequently ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] agrees with \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n]; see 3.5.

It remains to prove that ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] is proper over ℤ[1/n] and that c is representable, that is, for every object C of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] over an algebraically closed field k, the map

(6.7)        Aut(C)→Aut(c(C))

is injective.

Properness follows from the valuative criterion and 6.2.5. Let S be a complete trait with algebraically closed residue field, let C\textunderscore{}η be an elliptic curve over the generic point η of S, and let a be a level-H structure on C\textunderscore{}η. We may suppose that the minimal model C' of C\textunderscore{}η is smooth or a k-sided Néron polygon, with n|k. If C' is smooth, the level-H structure extends uniquely to C', and set C=C'. Otherwise let C'' be obtained from C' by contraction as in 1.3, so C''\textunderscore{}s has n sides. With the notation of 6.1, let U(B') be the largest subgroup of U fixing a. Then B' corresponds to a finite étale subgroup scheme of C''\textunderscore{}n of order n·m. Let C be the curve obtained from C'' by 1.3, with the n of that reference equal to m. The structure a extends uniquely to a level-H structure on C. Using 6.2.5 and the argument of 1.6, one sees that

(C, an extension of a) is the unique extension of (C\textunderscore{}η,a) over S.

Let us prove that (6.7) is injective. For C smooth, this is clear. Otherwise, the kernel of (6.7.1) is the set A of automorphisms of C that act trivially on Pic⁰(C) and respect the level structure. Condition e) in Definition 6.5 of level structures ensures that A = \{1\}.

V. Reduction modulo p.

In this chapter we study the reduction modulo p of the stacks \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K, for K = Γ\textunderscore{}0(p), Γ\textunderscore{}∞(p), and Γ(p\textasciicircum{}k). Comparison of the results obtained for Γ\textunderscore{}0(p) and Γ\textunderscore{}∞(p) allows us to show that, in accordance with a conjecture of Shimura, certain abelian schemes studied by Shimura and Casselman [5] have good reduction over the ring of integers of suitable cyclotomic extensions of ℚ.

1. Study of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}.

In this section we propose to give a modular interpretation of the stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} when p is prime. Some preliminary results will be used again in the following sections.

1.1. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n be the stack classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S with geometrically irreducible fibers, equipped with a subgroup scheme A locally isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}n (for the étale topology), that is, finite flat locally free of rank n with étale Cartier dual.

Proposition 1.2. The morphism "forget A" : \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is étale; in particular, \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n is an algebraic stack smooth over ℤ.

Let C/S be classified by \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1. It must be checked that the functor

F : (Sch/S) → (Sets),

which assigns to T/S the set of subgroups A of (C\textunderscore{}T)\textunderscore{}n locally isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}n, is represented by a scheme M étale over S. Representability follows from the theory of the Hilbert scheme; M is étale because it is formally étale (SGA 3 IX 3.6).

1.3. Let ℬ\textunderscore{}n be the stack classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S whose geometric fibers are smooth or n-gons, equipped with a subgroup scheme B locally isomorphic to ℤ/n (for the étale topology) that meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber.

Let (C,B) be as above. The group scheme B acts freely on C

and C/B has geometrically irreducible fibers. In the exact sequence

0 → B → C\textunderscore{}n → A → 0,

the extreme terms are Cartier dual. The pair (C/B,C\textunderscore{}n/B) is therefore classified by \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n, and this defines

(1.3.1)        w : ℬ\textunderscore{}n → \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n.

Proposition 1.4. The morphism 1.3.1 is an isomorphism. In particular, ℬ\textunderscore{}n is an algebraic stack smooth over ℤ.

Proof: Again denote by w the morphism from \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ to ℬ\textunderscore{}nᵒ

w : (C,A) ↦ (C/A,C\textunderscore{}n/A).

A construction identical to that of IV.4.4 shows that this morphism is an inverse of the restriction w : ℬ\textunderscore{}nᵒ → \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ of w to ℬ\textunderscore{}nᵒ.

Let (C,A) over S be classified by \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}n. It must be proved that there exist (C̃,Ã) over S, classified by ℬ\textunderscore{}n, and an isomorphism β : w(C̃,Ã) →̃ (C,A), and that (C̃,Ã,β) is unique up to unique isomorphism. By abuse of notation, we again denote by β the composite morphism

C̃ → C̃/Ã →̃\textasciicircum{}β C.

The curve with marked section (C̃,ẽ) will necessarily be an étale covering of degree n of (C,e), endowed with its generalized elliptic-curve structure.

Recall the following lemma, which follows from SGA 4 XII.5.9 bis.

Lemma 1.5. Let f : X → S be a proper morphism with geometrically connected fibers, and let e be a section of f. If p : (Y,e) → (X,e) is a finite étale covering with marked section of (X,e), and Y/S has geometrically connected fibers, the triple (Y,p,e) has no nontrivial automorphism. The functor F on (Sch/S) of isomorphism classes of triples (Y,p,e) as above is represented by an étale sheaf.

Apply this lemma to C/S. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} be the stack of triples (C̃,Ã,β) of the required type. The lemma shows that the objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} have no nontrivial automorphism, and that the

functor [\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}] of isomorphism classes of objects of \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}} is the subfunctor of F formed by the triples (C̃,p;e) such that p⁻¹(e) is locally isomorphic to ℤ/n and A is the image of C̃\textunderscore{}n. These conditions are open (because A is of multiplicative type), so [\ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}] is represented by T étale over S.

Where C is smooth, one knows that T →̃ S. Moreover, a 1-gon over an algebraically closed field k admits a unique étale covering by an n-gon. All geometric fibers of T/S are therefore reduced to one point, and T →̃ S. This proves 1.4.

Let p be a prime number.

Theorem 1.6. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is the algebraic stack classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S equipped with a subgroup scheme A locally free of rank p that meets every irreducible component of every geometric fiber of C/S.

Proof: Recall [22] that a (commutative) group scheme locally free of rank p is killed by p. For A as above, one therefore has A ⊂ C\textunderscore{}p.

Provisionally denote by ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} the stack which assigns to each scheme S the category whose objects are the pairs (C,A) as in 1.6 and whose morphisms are the isomorphisms. In IV.4.3 we constructed an isomorphism between \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ[1/p] and ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ[1/p]. The isomorphism 1.6 will extend this isomorphism; reasoning as in IV.3.5, one finds that 1.6 follows from the conjunction of the following assertions:

A. ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is an algebraic stack.

B. ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is proper over Spec(ℤ).

C. The morphism c : ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 : (C,A) ↦ c(C), defined by IV.1.4, is finite and representable.

D. ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is normal.

E. ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ[1/p] is dense in ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}.

(1) Points at infinity of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}.

Let C be the one-sided Néron polygon over Spec(ℤ). One has C\textasciicircum{}\{reg\} ≃ G\textunderscore{}m, C\textunderscore{}p ≃ μ\textunderscore{}p, and (C,C\textunderscore{}p) is a section e\textunderscore{}1 of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} over ℤ.

Let C be the p-gon over Spec(ℤ). One has C\textasciicircum{}\{reg\} ≃ G\textunderscore{}m × ℤ/p, C\textunderscore{}p ≃ μ\textunderscore{}p × ℤ/p, and (C,e × ℤ/p) is a section e\textunderscore{}2 of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} over ℤ.

Let k be an algebraically closed field. One checks that the only objects (C,A) of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}(k), with C singular, are e\textunderscore{}1(k) and e\textunderscore{}2(k).

(2) Proof of A (representability of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}).

Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 and \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 be the following two open substacks of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}: \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 classifies the curves (C,A) as in 1.6 for which the geometric fibers of C are irreducible, and \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 classifies the curves (C,A) for which the geometric fibers of C are smooth or p-gons.

Lemma 1.7. The "forgetful" morphism φ : \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is representable.

Proof. It must be seen that, for C/S a generalized elliptic curve with geometrically irreducible fibers over S, the following functor F : (Sch/S) → (Sets) is representable. To T/S, F assigns the set of finite locally free subgroup schemes of rank p of C\textunderscore{}T\textasciicircum{}\{reg\}. The representability of F follows from the theory of Hilbert schemes.

By 1.7 and IV.2, \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 is an algebraic stack. We shall see below that \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 and \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 are isomorphic. The stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 is therefore algebraic, as is ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}, the union of \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 and \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 by (1).

1.8. Let (C,A) be classified by \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2. By II.1.20, C\textunderscore{}p is locally free of rank p². One checks fiber by fiber that A acts freely on C, and this makes it possible to define

(1.8.1)        w : \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 : (C,A) ↦ (C/A,C\textunderscore{}p/A\textunderscore{}p).

Lemma 1.8.2. The morphism (1.8.1) is an isomorphism.

With the notation of 1.1 and 1.3, one has

\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 = \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}p ∪ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ↗                         ↘ ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ = \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 ∩ \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2          ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ↘                         ↗ \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 = ℬ\textunderscore{}p ∪ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ.

Construction IV.4.4 again shows that w induces an involution of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ. In 1.4 we saw that w induces an isomorphism \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}p → ℬ\textunderscore{}p, and 1.8.2 follows.

This completes the proof of (A). The proof has supplied us with the following construction.

Construction 1.9. By gluing w : \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 and w⁻¹ : \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}1 → \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}\textunderscore{}2, one obtains an involution w : ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} → ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} that extends the involution (IV.4.4).

The union of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}p and ℬ\textunderscore{}p is the open substack of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} corresponding to nonsupersingular curves of characteristic p. Propositions 1.2 and 1.4 therefore give:

Proposition 1.10. ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is smooth over Spec(ℤ) away from the supersingular points of characteristic p.

(3) Proof of (B) (properness of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}).

By (1.10), ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ is dense in ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}. The valuative criterion of properness therefore reduces us to proving the following lemma.

Lemma 1.11. Let (S,η,s) be a complete trait with algebraically closed residue field. Let (C,A) be an object of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ(η). There exists a finite extension k(η′) of k(η) such that, writing S′ for the normalization of S in k(η′), the image (C′,A′) of (C,A) in ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ(η′) comes by base change from an object (C̄,Ā) of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}(S′), unique up to unique isomorphism.

Proof: After first making an extension of k(η), one may suppose that C has semistable reduction and that the special fiber of its minimal model C̃ is smooth or an m-gon, with p|m (IV.1.6). The group C̃\textunderscore{}p is then finite over S (II.1.20), and the schematic closure of A is a finite locally free subgroup scheme of rank p of C̃. One obtains (C̄,Ā) by contracting the components of C̃\textunderscore{}s that do not meet A (IV.1.3).

Uniqueness is proved as in (IV.1.6 (iv)): if (C̄\textunderscore{}1,Ā\textunderscore{}1) has generic fiber C, one obtains the minimal model C̃ of C by successively blowing up the singularities, as in (IV.1.6 (iv)). The inverse image of Ā\textunderscore{}1 is the schematic closure Ã of A, and it is clear that C̄ is obtained by contracting the irreducible components of C̃\textunderscore{}s whose intersection with Ã is empty; hence uniqueness.

(4) Proof of C (c finite and representable).

To verify that the geometric fibers of c are finite, we distinguish three cases.

a) Fibers at infinity: over an algebraically closed field k there are only finitely many (two) isomorphism classes of pairs (C,A), classified by ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ, with C singular.

b) Fibers at finite distance: let C be an elliptic curve over k, and let T be the fiber of c over the geometric point Spec(k) → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ defined by C. The scheme T represents the functor of rank-p subgroup schemes of C\textunderscore{}p. Distinguish the cases C\textunderscore{}p ≃ ℤ/p × μ\textunderscore{}p and, when char(k) = p, C\textunderscore{}p ≃ α\textunderscore{}\{p²\}.

b1) There is an exact sequence  0 → μ\textunderscore{}p → C\textunderscore{}p → ℤ/p → 0  (1).

For every scheme S and every A ⊂ C\textunderscore{}S locally free of rank p, one then has S = S′ \ensuremath{\amalg} S″, with A →̃ ℤ/p over S″ and A ⊂ μ\textunderscore{}p, hence A = μ\textunderscore{}p over S′. Thus T = T′ ∪ T″; T′ is reduced and consists of the point defined by μ\textunderscore{}p ⊂ C\textunderscore{}p. The scheme T″ represents the functor of trivializations ℤ/p → C\textunderscore{}p of the exact sequence (1). It is a torsor under μ\textunderscore{}p. Consequently T has p+1 (respectively 2) geometric points if char(k) ≠ p (respectively = p).

b2) C\textunderscore{}p ≃ α\textunderscore{}\{p²\}. Every subgroup of α\textunderscore{}\{p²\} is infinitesimal. If it has order p, it equals the Frobenius kernel α\textunderscore{}p, and T has only one geometric point.

Since ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is proper, c, being proper with finite fibers, is finite. To prove that c is representable, one proceeds as in IV.3.5: one checks over ⟦a blank in the authority⟧ that, for a singular pair (C,A), Aut(C,A) = \{±1\}, so that

Aut(C,A) ↪ Aut(c(C)).

(5) The key point of the proof is the following lemma.

Lemma 1.12. The morphism c : ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ is finite and flat.

Proof: We shall prove 1.12 using the following criterion, which applies because \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ is reduced.

Lemma 1.13. Let f : X → S be a finite morphism of Noetherian schemes, with S reduced. If the rank of the geometric fibers of f is constant, then f is flat.

One may suppose that S is the spectrum of a local ring A and that X is the spectrum of a finite A-algebra B. Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}} be the maximal ideal of A, and let ē\textunderscore{}1,…,ē\textunderscore{}n be a basis of the A/\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}-vector space B/\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}B. Lift the ē\textunderscore{}i to elements e\textunderscore{}i ∈ B. By Nakayama's lemma, the e\textunderscore{}i generate the A-module B. If ∑ λ\textunderscore{}i e\textunderscore{}i = 0, with λ\textunderscore{}i ∈ A, the hypothesis implies that the λ\textunderscore{}i vanish modulo every prime ideal, and hence vanish because A is reduced; thus the A-module B is free with basis the e\textunderscore{}i.

Let us verify that the morphism 1.12 satisfies the hypothesis of 1.13.

Let C be an elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k. The fiber T of c over the geometric point Spec(k) → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ defined by C represents the functor of rank-p subgroup schemes of C\textunderscore{}p. We prove that it has rank p+1.

If C\textunderscore{}p ≃ μ\textunderscore{}p × ℤ/p, we saw in (4) that T is isomorphic to Spec(k) \ensuremath{\amalg} μ\textunderscore{}p,


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0098-A04.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0098-A04.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0099}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 99}{D018-P0099-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 99; collected-paper page 592; original folio 241.}\par\smallskip
and hence has rank p+1.

It remains to treat the case in which k has characteristic p and C\textunderscore{}p ≃ α\textunderscore{}\{p²\}. In this case T represents the functor of locally free rank-p subschemes A of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}a contained in α\textunderscore{}\{p²\}.

Over an arbitrary base, a locally free rank-p subscheme A of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}a is defined by an equation

(*)        X\textasciicircum{}p + ∑\textunderscore{}\{0\}\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} a\textunderscore{}i X\textasciicircum{}i = 0.

For A to be stable under the homothety X ↦ nX (n ∈ (ℤ/p)*), it is necessary and sufficient that (*) be identical to the equation

X\textasciicircum{}p + ∑\textunderscore{}\{0\}\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} n\textasciicircum{}\{p-i\} a\textunderscore{}i X\textasciicircum{}i.

In particular, if A is a subgroup scheme of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}a, its equation reduces to

X\textasciicircum{}p - aX = 0.

For A to be a subscheme of α\textunderscore{}\{p²\}, it is necessary and sufficient that the left-hand side of this equation divide X\textasciicircum{}\{p²\}. Since

X\textasciicircum{}\{p²\} = (X\textasciicircum{}p - aX) · (∑\textunderscore{}\{i=0\}\textasciicircum{}\{p\} a\textasciicircum{}i X\textasciicircum{}\{p²-i(p-1)-p\}) + a\textasciicircum{}\{p+1\}X,

this holds if and only if a\textasciicircum{}\{p+1\} = 0. Thus

T ≃ Spec(k[a]/(a\textasciicircum{}\{p+1\})),

which completes the proof of 1.12.

Since c is finite and flat and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ is regular, ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ is Cohen--Macaulay (EGA IV.15 4.2), purely of relative dimension one over Spec(ℤ). At infinity, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is even smooth (1.10); consequently the whole of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is Cohen--Macaulay and, again by 1.10, smooth over Spec(ℤ) outside a finite set (of codimension 2). Assertion (D) therefore follows from Serre's criterion for normality (EGA IV.5.8.6).

1.14. Variants: Let n be an integer prime to p, let H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n), and let K be the inverse image of H in GL(2, ℤ̂). Theorem 1.6 generalizes to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[1/n]:

(1.14.1) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[1/n] classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S whose geometric fibers are smooth, n-gons, or np-gons, equipped with an isomorphism α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 and a locally free rank-p subgroup H of C such that C\textunderscore{}n·H meets every geometric component of every geometric fiber.

(.14.2) For square-free n, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(n)\} classifies generalized elliptic curves C/S equipped with a locally free rank-n subgroup H of C that meets every geometric component of every geometric fiber.

1.15. Let S be a scheme of characteristic p and E an elliptic curve over S. To E one associates the following two objects of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ(S):

Φ\textunderscore{}1(E) : (E, Ker(F : E → E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\}))

Φ\textunderscore{}2(E) = wΦ\textunderscore{}1(E) : (E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\}, Ker(V : E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\} → E)).

In these formulas, F denotes the Frobenius morphism and V its transpose, the Verschiebung: FV = VF = p. These constructions define a diagram

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p                         \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p ╲ Φ\textunderscore{}1                   wΦ\textunderscore{}1 ╱ ↘                         ↙ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p ↙                         ↘ c ╱                           ╲ cw \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p                         \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p

The composites cΦ\textunderscore{}1 : E ↦ (E, Ker F) ↦ E and cwwΦ\textunderscore{}1 are the identity. The composites cwΦ\textunderscore{}1 : E ↦ (E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\}, Ker V) ↦ E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\} are the Frobenius endomorphism of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p.

Theorem 1.16. (i) The maps Φ\textunderscore{}1 and Φ\textunderscore{}2 = wΦ\textunderscore{}1 are closed immersions. Their images are the two irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p.

(ii) These irreducible components meet transversely at the supersingular points. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p, which is reduced, therefore has no singularities other than ordinary double points.

(iii) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is regular.

Use a superscript h to denote the stack obtained by removing the supersingular points of characteristic p. One checks that Φ\textunderscore{}1 and wΦ\textunderscore{}1 define an isomorphism

Φ\textunderscore{}1 \ensuremath{\amalg} wΦ\textunderscore{}1 : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}\{oh\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p \ensuremath{\amalg} \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}\{oh\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p →̃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}\textasciicircum{}\{oh\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p.

The inverse isomorphism is defined by c and cw, restricted to the images of Φ\textunderscore{}1 and wΦ\textunderscore{}1.

That Φ\textunderscore{}1 (respectively wΦ\textunderscore{}1) is a closed immersion follows from the existence of a retraction (since these are representable morphisms). It is known (IV.5.6) that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p is geometrically irreducible, and this proves (i).

The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is reduced because it is Cohen--Macaulay and generically reduced (I.7.3). The supersingular points lie in the images of both Φ\textunderscore{}1 and Φ\textunderscore{}2 because, for a supersingular elliptic curve E over an algebraically closed field k, the only subgroup of rank p is Ker(F) = Ker(V : E → E\textasciicircum{}\{(1/p)\}). Thus the two irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ modulo p meet at these points. The intersection is transverse because the tangent vectors to the two branches are linearly independent: one is killed by dc and not by d(wc), while the other is killed by d(wc) and not by dc. This proves (ii). We shall prove (iii) in 1.19.

Variant 1.17. Let n ≥ 3 and consider \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ. This stack, as well as \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\}ᵒ, is a scheme: the classified objects have no automorphisms (cf. IV.2.7). By 1.14 these schemes represent the functors of isomorphism classes of objects of the following type

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ : (E,α\textunderscore{}n,H) : α\textunderscore{}n : E\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/nℤ)\textasciicircum{}2; H ⊂ E of rank p,

or, more symmetrically (cf. IV.4.5), set F = E/H.

(E →\textasciicircum{}h F, α\textunderscore{}n) : h a p-isogeny and α\textunderscore{}n : E\textunderscore{}n →̃\textunderscore{}\{h̄\} F\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/nℤ)\textasciicircum{}2.

After reduction modulo p, there is a diagram analogous to (1.15.1). Its arrows (expressed as morphisms of functors) are

Φ\textunderscore{}1 : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p : (E,α\textunderscore{}n) ↦ (E,α\textunderscore{}n, Ker F)

c : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\}ᵒ : (E,α\textunderscore{}n,H) ↦ (E,α\textunderscore{}n).

Take care that the isomorphism w²(E,H) ≃ (E,H) defined in IV.4.4 induces an isomorphism w²(E,α\textunderscore{}n,H) ≃ (E,pα\textunderscore{}n,H). The composite cwΦ\textunderscore{}1 : (E,α\textunderscore{}n) ↦ (E\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\},α\textunderscore{}n) is Frobenius.

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p                         \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p ╲ Φ\textunderscore{}1                   wΦ\textunderscore{}1 ╱ │ ↘                         ↙    │ (E,α\textunderscore{}n) \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p            ↓ ↙                         ↘    │ (E,pα\textunderscore{}n) c ╱                           ╲ cw \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p                         \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}nᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p

(composite diagonals: Frobenius).

Over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p, the condition

Φ\textunderscore{}1((E,α\textunderscore{}r)) = wΦ\textunderscore{}1(F,β\textunderscore{}n)

means that F is supersingular, that E = F\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\}, and that the diagram

is commutative, i.e. (E,α\textunderscore{}n) = (F,β\textunderscore{}n)\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\}. Parts (i) and (ii) of 1.16 therefore admit the following variant.

Variant 1.18. The scheme \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}ᵒ ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p can be obtained by gluing two copies of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\}ᵒ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p along the supersingular points: the supersingular point x of the second copy is identified with the point x\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\} of the first copy.

1.19. Let us prove that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} is regular. Away from the supersingular points, this follows from 1.10. Since at a supersingular point \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} → Spec(ℤ) has an ordinary quadratic singularity, (I.5.2) shows that there exists k ∈ ℕ such that the completion of the strict local ring of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} at such a point is isomorphic to

W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[X,Y]]/(X.Y - p\textasciicircum{}k)    (W = Witt vectors).

If k = 1, this complete local ring is regular. Otherwise, its spectrum has the section X = Y = 0 over W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)/(p\textasciicircum{}k) (k > 1), and over W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)/(p\textasciicircum{}k) there exists an elliptic curve E equipped with a locally free subgroup H of rank p, with E supersingular after reduction modulo p (hence H ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p ≃ α\textunderscore{}p). This is absurd, because by [22] α\textunderscore{}p admits no lifting over W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)/(p\textasciicircum{}k) when k > 1.

1.20. Variants. Let H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n), let K be its inverse image in GL(2, ℤ̂), and suppose (n,p) = 1. Then \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[1/n] is regular. For example, if n ≥ 3, the scheme \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[1/n] is regular; for square-free n, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(n)\} is regular.

2. Study of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}.

2.1. In IV.4 we gave a modular interpretation of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}[1/p]: denoting by (C,A) the universal generalized elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}, and also its inverse image over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p], one has

By definition, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} is the normalization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[ζ\textunderscore{}p] in this stack Isom\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p]\}(ℤ/p,A). One readily deduces the description below of the stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}\textasciicircum{}h obtained from \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} by removing the supersingular points of characteristic p.

2.2. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′\textunderscore{}p ⊂ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} be the open substack classifying the pairs (C,A) for which A is locally isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}p (for the étale topology), and let ℬ′\textunderscore{}p ⊂ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} be the open substack classifying the pairs (C,A) for which A is locally isomorphic to ℤ/p. Set

\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} = Isom\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′\textunderscore{}p\}(μ\textunderscore{}p,A)

\ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} = Isom\textunderscore{}\{ℬ′\textunderscore{}p\}(ℤ/p,A);

the schemes \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} are finite étale over \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′\textunderscore{}p and ℬ′\textunderscore{}p, respectively. Over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p], ℤ/p and μ\textunderscore{}p are canonically isomorphic, hence there is an isomorphism

\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}}[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p].

Proposition 2.3. The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}\textasciicircum{}h is obtained by gluing \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}[ζ\textunderscore{}p] and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}}[ζ\textunderscore{}p] along the common open substack \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p]. In particular, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}\textasciicircum{}h is smooth over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p].

To study \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} near the supersingular points, we shall use the results of [22] recalled below.

2.4. The functor F that assigns to a scheme S the set of isomorphisms

χ : \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p\textasciicircum{}* →̃ μ\textunderscore{}\{p-1\}

satisfying (2.4.1) below is represented by a scheme Spec(Λ\textunderscore{}p).

(2.4.1) At every point s of S of characteristic p, the induced isomorphism

χ : \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p\textasciicircum{}* →̃ μ\textunderscore{}\{p-1\}(k(s)) ≃ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p\textasciicircum{}*

is the identity.

The scheme Spec(Λ\textunderscore{}p) is isomorphic to the open subscheme of Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}\{p-1\}]) obtained by removing the places dividing p-1 and all but one of the places above p. Spec(Λ\textunderscore{}p)

is étale over Spec(ℤ), and \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p →̃ Λ\textunderscore{}p ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p.

Let S be equipped with χ satisfying (2.4.1) (so S is a Λ\textunderscore{}p-scheme), and let A be a locally free group scheme of rank p over S. Let L be the submodule of the affine algebra \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(A) of A (a sheaf of \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S-algebras) formed by those f such that

f(ix) = χ(i) f(x)        (i ∈ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p\textasciicircum{}*).

By [22], L is an invertible module over S, and there exists a unique section a of L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(1-p)\} such that, for f as above,

f\textasciicircum{}p = ⟨a,f\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\}⟩.f        in L ⊂ \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(A).

Let A* be the Cartier dual of A, and let L* be defined by A*. The duality e between \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(A) and \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(A*) puts L and L* in duality. Let b ∈ (L*)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗ 1-p\} be the analogue for A* of the section a above. Then

a.b = w(χ)

for a certain number w(χ) ∈ Λ\textunderscore{}p, with (w(χ)) = (p) in Λ\textunderscore{}p.

The functor (on isomorphisms)

(A : a locally free group scheme of rank p) ↦ ((L,a,b) : L invertible, a ∈ L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(1-p)\}, b ∈ L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(p-1)\}, a.b = w(χ))

is an equivalence of categories.

The set of sections of A over S is identified with the set of sections s of L* over S such that s\textasciicircum{}\{⊗p\} = as. For s to define an isomorphism between A and ℤ/p, it is necessary and sufficient that s be an invertible section of L*.

Examples: One has

(2.4.2)        ℤ/p ↦ (\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S,1,w(χ))

(2.4.3)        μ\textunderscore{}p ↦ (\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S,w(χ),1)

(2.4.4)        α\textunderscore{}p ↦ (\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S,0,0).

2.5. Let an isomorphism ζ : ℤ/p →̃ μ\textunderscore{}p be given over S (where S is a Λ\textunderscore{}p[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p]-scheme). The isomorphism ζ defines ζ* : \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(μ\textunderscore{}p) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(ℤ/p) and, through (2.4.2) and (2.4.3), induces

ζ* : \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S = L(μ\textunderscore{}p) → L(ℤ/p) = \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S,

namely multiplication by a number w(χ,ζ) ∈ Λ\textunderscore{}p[ζ\textunderscore{}p]. One has

w(χ,ζ)\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = w(χ).

An isomorphism s : ℤ/p →̃ A defines by duality

s* : A* →̃ μ\textunderscore{}p ←̃ ℤ/p,

whose inverse is t. The sections s(1) and t(1) are identified with sections x and y of L* and L satisfying

x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = a        y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = b

xy = w(χ,ζ).

2.6. Let ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p be the following algebraic Λ\textunderscore{}p[ζ\textunderscore{}p]-stack over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p[ζ\textunderscore{}p]: an object (C,A,x,y) of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p over a Λ\textunderscore{}p[ζ\textunderscore{}p]-scheme S consists of

a) an object (C,A) of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}. Let (L,a,b) be deduced from A.

b) sections x of L* and y of L such that x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = a, y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = b, and xy = w(χ,ζ).

The constructions of 2.5 and IV.4.8 define an isomorphism

(2.6.1)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p[1/p] ≃ ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p[1/p].

Theorem 2.7. The isomorphism 2.6.1 extends to an isomorphism

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p ≃ ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p.

Let us first show that the isomorphism (2.6.1) extends away from the supersingular points (the left-hand side is interpreted using 2.3). Over the open substack ℬ′\textunderscore{}p of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} (where A is étale), a is invertible; giving x such that x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = a amounts to giving an isomorphism from ℤ/p to A, and (x,ζ) uniquely determines y. Dually, over \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′\textunderscore{}p (where A* is étale), b is invertible. Giving y such that y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = b amounts to giving an isomorphism from μ\textunderscore{}p to A, and (y,ζ)

uniquely determines x. Where p is invertible, these two constructions are compatible through the isomorphism ζ : ℤ/p →̃ μ\textunderscore{}p.

The stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p is finite and representable over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p. It therefore suffices to prove that ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p is normal at the supersingular points. The following lemma shows that ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p is in fact regular at these points.

Lemma 2.8. Let s̄ be a supersingular geometric point of characteristic p of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}. Let Ŝ be the completion of the strict henselization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} at s̄, equipped with its unique Λ\textunderscore{}p-algebra structure. Let (C,A) over Ŝ be defined by Ŝ → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}, and let (L,a,b) be defined by A.

(i)        Ŝ ≃ W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[u,v]]/(uv-w(χ))    with (L,a,b) ≃ (\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}},u,v).

(ii) The normalization ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}\} Ŝ of Ŝ in Isom\textunderscore{}\{Ŝ[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p]\}(ℤ/p,A) is Ŝ-isomorphic to

W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[ζ\textunderscore{}p,x,y]]/(xy-w(χ,ζ)) ≃ W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[x,y]]/(x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} - w(χ)),

with x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = u and y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = v.

It is known that Ŝ ≃ W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[u,v]]/(uv - p) (1.16), that ab = w(χ) = p times a unit (cf. 2.5), and that a and b vanish at the supersingular points (2.4.4). Assertions (i) and (ii) follow.

Remark 2.9. It follows from 2.4, 2.4.4, and 2.8(ii) that Ŝ, equipped with A, is a versal formal moduli scheme for deformations of α\textunderscore{}p.

Variant 2.10. Here is how to modify 2.6 to obtain a modular description of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}: it is the algebraic stack over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p] whose sections over a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]-scheme S classify the objects (C,H) of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} over S, equipped with

a) a morphism ℤ/p →̃ H over S[1/p];

b) over S ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p, where H defines (L,a,b), sections x of L* and y of L such that x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = a, y\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = b, and xy = w(χ,ζ).

2.11. With the notation of 2.2 and of 1.1 and 1.3, \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p are étale covers of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}p ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p and ℬ\textunderscore{}p ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p, respectively. The morphisms Φ\textunderscore{}1 and wΦ\textunderscore{}1 (cf. 1.15) identify \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}p ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p and ℬ\textunderscore{}p ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p with open substacks of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p; through this identification, the local structure theorem 2.8 shows that the covers \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}h ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p are totally ramified at the supersingular points of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p. In particular, \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p are geometrically irreducible, and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p has two irreducible components, the inverse images of the irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p. The proof of 2.7 gives the following result.

Theorem 2.12. (i) The two irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p are smooth and meet transversely at the supersingular points.

(ii) The scheme \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} is regular; the morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]) has only quadratic singularities.

(iii) The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} induces a bijection (an equivalence of categories) on the supersingular geometric points of characteristic p.

2.13. Let n be an integer ≥ 3 and prime to p. As in 1.17, one can make 2.12 more concrete by passing to the scheme \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h be the étale cover Isom(μ\textunderscore{}p,(C\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}o) of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\}\textasciicircum{}h ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p. It extends to a cover \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}n of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p, totally ramified at the supersingular points, and

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p

is obtained by gluing two copies of \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}\{n,p\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p along the supersingular points, identifying x in the second copy with x\textasciicircum{}\{(p)\} in the first (as in 1.17). In particular, the morphism

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p

induces a bijection on the sets of irreducible components, and

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p

induces a bijection on the set of supersingular points.

Since \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}ᵒ[1/n] is finite étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}ᵒ[1/n], the

2.14. Let H be a subgroup of (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*, and let d be its index. Write Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H) and Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) for the subgroups of GL(2, ℤ̂) that are the inverse images of the following subgroups of GL(2, ℤ/p):

Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H) : ( H    ℤ/p )        Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) : ( H    ℤ/p ) ( 0   (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}* )                  ( 0      H  ).

For H = \{e\} (respectively, H = (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*), one has Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H) = Γ\textunderscore{}∞(p) and Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) = Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p) (respectively, Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H) = Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) = Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)).

We denote by ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H the ring of invariants for H acting on ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p] by i ∈ H ⊂ (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}* ↦ (ζ\textunderscore{}p ↦ ζ\textunderscore{}p\textasciicircum{}i). One verifies, as in IV.4, that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/p] and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/p] admit the following descriptions. If (C,A) is the universal curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\},

(2.14.1)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/p] ≃ Isom\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}[1/p]\}(ℤ/p,A)/H        (cf. IV.4.14)

(2.14.2)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/p] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/p] ⊗ ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H        (cf. IV.4.15)

Formula (2.14.1) means that if S is a scheme on which p is invertible, an object of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} over S is identified with an object (C,A) of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} over S, equipped with a global section of the quotient sheaf Isom\textunderscore{}S(ℤ/p,A)/H.

2.15. Let A be a locally free group scheme of rank p over a Λ\textunderscore{}p-scheme S, and let (L,a,b) be defined by A. As seen in 2.4, giving an isomorphism between ℤ/p and A amounts to giving an invertible x such that x\textasciicircum{}\{p-1\} = a. Multiplying the isomorphism by i ∈ (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}* amounts to multiplying x by χ(i). The map x ↦ x\textasciicircum{}\{(p-1)/d\} therefore induces an isomorphism from Isom(ℤ/p,A)/H to the sheaf of invertible sections u of L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(1-p)/d\} such that u\textasciicircum{}d = a.

2.17. As in 2.10, this definition can be modified to obtain a stack ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H: an object of ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} over a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H-scheme S is an object (C,A) of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(H)\} over S, equipped with

a) a global section u\textunderscore{}1 of Isom\textunderscore{}\{S[1/p]\}(ℤ/p,A)/H;

b) over S ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p, where A defines (L,a,b), sections u and v of L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(1-p)/d\} and L\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(p-1)/d\}, with u\textasciicircum{}d = a, v\textasciicircum{}d = b, and uv = w(χ,ζ)\textasciicircum{}\{(p-1)/d\}.

These data are required to be compatible, through (2.16.1), over S ⊗ Λ\textunderscore{}p[1/p].

The proof of 2.7 and 2.12 gives the following results.

Theorem 2.18. (i) One has \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ≃ ′\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}.

(ii) The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p has two irreducible components. They are smooth and meet transversely at the supersingular points.

(iii) The stack \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} is a regular scheme; the morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H) has only quadratic singularities.

(iv) The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} induces a bijection (an equivalence of categories) on the supersingular geometric points of characteristic p.

2.19. Let n be an integer prime to p, with n ≥ 3. Theorem 2.18 generalizes to the schemes \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/n].

(i)        \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/n] is regular.

(ii)        The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}[1/n] → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p,1/p]\textasciicircum{}H) is smooth away from the supersingular points of characteristic p, where it has ordinary quadratic singularities.

(iii)        The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p induces a bijection on supersingular points.

(iv)        The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p induces a bijection between the sets of irreducible components.

(v)        The scheme \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is obtained by gluing two copies of \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}n/H along the supersingular points (cf. 2.13).

3. A good-reduction theorem.

3.1. Let p be a prime, let H ⊂ (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*, let Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) be the group of 2.14, let ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H be as in 2.14, and let n be an integer prime to p. Assume n ≥ 3, so that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ = M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ is a scheme. It is a scheme over ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H.

In this section, J(n,H) denotes the abelian variety over ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H given by the identity component of the Picard scheme of M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ:

J(n,H) = Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ / ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H).

For H = (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*, one has Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H) = Γ\textunderscore{}0(p), and the map

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}

induces a morphism

(3.1.1)        J(n,(ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H → J(n,H)

with finite kernel.

Theorem 3.2. The abelian variety I(n,H) over ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H, defined as the cokernel of (3.1.1), has good reduction at the places dividing p.

Let v be a characteristic-p point of ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H, and let \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\} be an algebraic closure of its residue field. Let ν be the number of supersingular points of M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ]\} \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\}. The scheme M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ]\} \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\} is known to be irreducible. It follows that the connected scheme M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\} has two irreducible components meeting transversely at ν points (cf. 1.18), and that the Picard group variety over \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\},

Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ(H)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H\} \textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\}/\textbackslash{}overline\{k(v)\}),

is an extension of an abelian scheme by a torus of dimension ν - 1 (cf. I. 3.7). In particular:

Proposition 3.3. The abelian variety J(n,H) over ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p]\textasciicircum{}H has semistable reduction at the places dividing p.

Suppose 0 → A → B → C → 0 is an exact sequence up to isogeny of abelian varieties over the fraction field of a discrete valuation ring. Let α\textunderscore{}ab, α\textunderscore{}m, and α\textunderscore{}add denote the abelian, multiplicative, and unipotent ranks of the special fiber of the Néron model. Then

α\textunderscore{}ab(A) - α\textunderscore{}ab(B) + α\textunderscore{}ab(C) = 0,

and likewise for α\textunderscore{}m and α\textunderscore{}add. Moreover, if α\textunderscore{}add = 0, then α\textunderscore{}ab and α\textunderscore{}m are invariant under scalar extension (the semistable case). The condition α\textunderscore{}add = α\textunderscore{}m = 0 is equivalent to good reduction. Here α\textunderscore{}add(J(n,H)) = 0 and α\textunderscore{}m(J(n,H)) = ν-1, independently of H; the theorem follows.

Variant 3.4. It follows from 2.14.2 that

J(n,H) = Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ / ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n)) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n)\} ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H.

Let I\textunderscore{}1(n,H) be the abelian variety over ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n) that is the cokernel of the morphism from

Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} ⊗ ℚ / ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n)) → Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} ⊗ ℚ / ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n)) → I\textunderscore{}1(n,H) → 0.

One has I(n,H) = I\textunderscore{}1(n,H) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n)\} ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H, so 3.2 can still be formulated by saying that, after extension of scalars from ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n) to ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H, I\textunderscore{}1(n,H) acquires good reduction at the places dividing p.

Variant 3.5. The abelian variety over ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H

RJ(n,H) =\textunderscore{}\{dfn\} Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(H)\} / ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H)

is obtained from J(n,H) by Weil restriction of scalars from ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n,ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H to ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H. The cokernel RI(n,H) of

Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\} / ℚ) ⊗ ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H → Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ(n)∩Γ′\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} / ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H)

is obtained similarly from I(n,H) by Weil restriction of scalars. Since ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}n) is unramified at p, RI(n,H) still has good reduction over ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H at the places dividing p.

Variant 3.6. Let K be a subgroup of GL(2,ℤ̂) containing Γ(n), and let RI\textunderscore{}1(K,H) be the abelian variety over ℚ that is the cokernel of

Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}) → Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}).

One has RI\textunderscore{}1(K,H) ⊗\textunderscore{}ℚ ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H ↔ RI(n,H) (up to isogeny); hence the abelian variety RI\textunderscore{}1(K,H) acquires good reduction over ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p)\textasciicircum{}H at the places dividing p.

Examples 3.7. (i) The abelian variety over ℚ

coker(Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)\}) → Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(p)\}))

has good reduction over ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p). Let H be the subgroup \{±1\} of (ℤ/p)\textasciicircum{}*. One has M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(p)\} = M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H)\}. The abelian variety in the preceding variant therefore acquires good reduction already over the maximal totally real subfield of ℚ(ζ\textunderscore{}p).

By 3.6,

B = coker(Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}0(p)∩Γ\textunderscore{}∞(m)\}) → Pic\textasciicircum{}o(M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}∞(H\textunderscore{}p)\}))

4. Study of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n

4.1. Let C be a generalized elliptic curve over a scheme S, and let n be an integer.

Assume that

a) the geometric fibers of C are smooth or m-gons, with n|m;

b) they are never supersingular of characteristic p|n.

Conditions a) and b) ensure that, locally for the étale topology, C\textunderscore{}n is an extension of ℤ/n by μ\textunderscore{}n. The e\textunderscore{}n pairing defines an isomorphism of fppf sheaves

e\textunderscore{}n : ∧\textasciicircum{}2 C\textunderscore{}n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n.

Let R be a group scheme over S, locally isomorphic to an extension of ℤ/n by μ\textunderscore{}n, and equipped with an isomorphism of fppf sheaves i : ∧\textasciicircum{}2 R ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n.

An isomorphism α : C\textunderscore{}n ≃ R is said to have determinant one if the diagram

∧\textasciicircum{}2 C\textunderscore{}n  --∧\textasciicircum{}2 α-->  ∧\textasciicircum{}2 R | e\textunderscore{}n                | i v                    v μ\textunderscore{}n         =        μ\textunderscore{}n

commutes.

4.2. We shall use 4.1 in the following three cases:


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0115-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0115-A05.}
\end{flushright}

a) S is a scheme over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n, 1/n], and R = (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2.

In this case one has over S the map ζ : ℤ/n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n, and i is the composite

∧\textasciicircum{}2(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 = ℤ/n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n.

b) R = μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n and i is the evident isomorphism: if a ∈ μ\textunderscore{}n and b ∈ ℤ/n, then i(a ∧ b) = b·a (= a\textasciicircum{}b when the group law of μ\textunderscore{}n is written multiplicatively).

c) S is a scheme over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] and A ⊂ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 is a cyclic subgroup of order n.

In this case, write R(A) for the S-group scheme obtained by the pushout

A  →  (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2  →  (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2/A ↓ζ      ↓              ∥ A ⊗ μ\textunderscore{}n  →   R(A)    →  (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2/A.

One also has

∧\textasciicircum{}2 R(A) ≃ (A ⊗ μ\textunderscore{}n) ⊗ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2/A ≃ (A ⊗ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2/A) ⊗ μ\textunderscore{}n ≃ ∧\textasciicircum{}2(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 ⊗ μ\textunderscore{}n ≃ ℤ/n ⊗ μ\textunderscore{}n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n.

4.3. In IV.3.10.1 we defined

d : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n → Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]).

As an algebraic stack over Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]), \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] classifies generalized elliptic curves C over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][1/n]-schemes S whose geometric fibers are smooth or n-gons, equipped with a determinant-one isomorphism α : C\textunderscore{}n ≃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2.

4.4. Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{a}} be the stack classifying generalized elliptic curves C over schemes S whose geometric fibers are smooth or n-gons, equipped with a determinant-one isomorphism α : C\textunderscore{}n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n (4.2(b)). Over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n, 1/n], the isomorphism ζ : ℤ/n ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n identifies (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 with μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n, hence gives an isomorphism

(4.4.1)        ζ : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{a}}[1/n] ⊗ ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n].

We denote by \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ the open substack of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} classifying the (C,α) satisfying

α((C\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{\textbackslash{}overline\{s\}\})\textunderscore{}n) = μ\textunderscore{}n.

If (C,α) is an object of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ over S, then (C,α\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(e × ℤ/n)) is an object of the stack ℬ\textunderscore{}n (1.3), hence a morphism

(4.4.3)        γ : \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ → ℬ\textunderscore{}n.

Proposition 4.5. The morphism 4.3.3 is étale and separated. In particular, \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ is a smooth algebraic stack over Spec(ℤ).

Let (C,β) be an object of ℬ\textunderscore{}n over S. Then \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ ×\textunderscore{}\{ℬ\textunderscore{}n\} S represents the following functor F : (Sch/S) → (Ens): F(T) is the set of pairs (A,u) consisting of a subgroup scheme A of C\textunderscore{}n, locally isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}n, such that

A × B →̃ C\textunderscore{}n, together with u : B →̃ ℤ/n.

For such a pair (A,u), there is indeed a unique isomorphism

v : A →̃ μ\textunderscore{}n such that

α : C\textunderscore{}n ←̃ A × B ─(v,u)→̃ μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/nℤ

has determinant one. We leave it to the reader to verify that F is representable by an étale and separated S-scheme.

4.6. Let p be a prime divisor of n, and put n = p\textasciicircum{}k·m with (m,p) = 1. We propose to study \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/m], especially in characteristic p. Gluing together the results obtained for the various p gives results on \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n itself.

We will exhibit the "p-aspect" of a level-n structure as follows.

a) The reduction morphisms define

(4.6.1)        ℤ/n →̃ ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k × ℤ/m.

With this convention, a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-scheme S is also a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}]-scheme and a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m]-scheme; the morphisms ζ : ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k → μ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} and ζ : ℤ/m → μ\textunderscore{}m are the components, via (4.6.1) and (4.6.2), of ζ : ℤ/n → μ\textunderscore{}n.

A morphism α : C\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2 has determinant one if and only if its components, via (4.6.2) (4.6.4), namely α′ : C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}2 and α″ : C\textunderscore{}m →̃ (ℤ/m)\textasciicircum{}2, do.

e) We identify \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] with the stack classifying curves C over a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n,1/n]-scheme S as in 4.3, equipped with α′ : C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}2 and α″ : C\textunderscore{}m →̃ (ℤ/m)\textasciicircum{}2 of determinant one.

f) Likewise, \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} classifies the (C,α′,α″) such that α′ : C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ μ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} × ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k, α″ : C\textunderscore{}m →̃ μ\textunderscore{}m × ℤ/m, and det(α′) = det(α″) = 1.

Lemma 4.7. \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} is a smooth algebraic stack over ℤ.

It is enough to show that \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m,1/m] is smooth over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m,1/m]. This stack is a union of open substacks isomorphic to \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}′ ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m,1/m], and 4.5 applies.

The verification is left to the reader.

4.11. Let C[1/m] be the stack obtained by gluing the stacks C\textunderscore{}A (A ∈ P) along their common open C\textunderscore{}A[1/p] (4.8.1). (4.11.1)        C[1/n] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n]

and the morphisms 4.9 glue to a morphism

Lemma 4.11.3. The representable morphism (4.11.2) is finite and separated.

Proof. It suffices to show that, for every p|n, c induces a proper morphism (in particular, a separated one)

c : C[1/m] → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}h[1/m].

We use the valuative criterion ([8]). Thus let (S,η,s) be a complete trait with

c v\textunderscore{}η = u\textunderscore{}η.

We must show that, after replacing S by its normalization in a finite extension of k(η), u admits a lift v : S → C extending v\textunderscore{}η, and that v is unique up to unique isomorphism. Since \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is dense in C, we may suppose that v\textunderscore{}η sends η into \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o[1/n]. Since C[1/p] ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] is finite over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n] (cf. IV.2.5.), it is enough to consider the case in which s has characteristic p.

Let (C\textunderscore{}η,α′\textunderscore{}η,α′′\textunderscore{}η) be the elliptic curve with level-n structure over η that defines v\textunderscore{}η. A preliminary finite extension of k(η) allows us to suppose that C\textunderscore{}η extends to a generalized elliptic curve C over S, with special fiber either smooth or an n-gon. This curve is unique (IV 1.6). The group scheme C\textunderscore{}m is finite étale over S, so α′′\textunderscore{}η extends uniquely to

α′′ : C\textunderscore{}m →̃ (ℤ/m)\textasciicircum{}2.

0 → A → C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} → B → 0

with A ≃ μ\textunderscore{}n and B ≃ ℤ/n. Let A = α′(A\textunderscore{}η) ∈ P. The isomorphism α′\textunderscore{}η extends to

α′ : C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ R(A),

v\textunderscore{}η extends to v : S → C\textunderscore{}A, defined by (C,α′,α′′), and does not extend to any v : S → C\textunderscore{}\{A′\} for A ≠ A′. This proves 4.11.3.

Let \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h denote the normalization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}h in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n], i.e. the complement in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n of the points whose image in \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is supersingular of characteristic dividing n. From the smoothness of the C\textunderscore{}A (4.8) and from 4.11 we obtain the following results.

Theorem 4.12. One has C ≃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h.

Corollary 4.13. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h is smooth over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n].

This amounts to giving

a) a trivialization τ of the extension in the first row of 4.14.1;

b) isomorphisms α′\textunderscore{}1 : C\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ μ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} and α′\textunderscore{}2 : C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}/C\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k.

Giving τ amounts to giving the étale subgroup α′\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k) of C\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}, so that (C,α′) ↦ (C,τ) corresponds to the morphism \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} → ℬ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} already considered. 4.15. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}k\textasciicircum{}h ⊗ ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}N be the algebraic stack over ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}N classifying generalized elliptic curves with irreducible fibers C/S, with p\textasciicircum{}N = 0 on S, equipped with an isomorphism α′\textunderscore{}1 : C\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} →̃ μ\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}.
