Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}k ⊗ ℤ/p be the normalization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ ℤ/p in \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}k\textasciicircum{}h. We shall use the following theorem of Igusa [35] (see Katz [13] 4.3 (bis)).

Theorem 4.17 (Igusa). The covering \ensuremath{\mathcal{J}}\textunderscore{}k ⊗ ℤ/p → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ ℤ/p is totally ramified at the supersingular points.

4.18. For general n, \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o = \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o(n) is a fiber product

(4.18.1)        \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o = \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o(m) ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\} \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o(p\textasciicircum{}k).

(This moreover remains true at infinity.) From the irreducibility of the geometric fibers of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}m[1/m]/ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m,1/m] and from 4.4.1, 4.18.1, 4.15.1, 4.16(ii), and 4.17, it follows that \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textasciicircum{}o ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}m]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is irreducible, and the following corollary.

Theorem 4.19. Via 4.12, the irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p identify with C\textunderscore{}A ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p, A ∈ P.

4.20. By 4.17 and 4.19, the irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p are indexed by P = ℙ\textasciicircum{}1(ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k), meet only at the supersingular points, and are unibranch. The following theorem asserts that all the irreducible components meet at every supersingular point.

Theorem 4.20. The morphism \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}m is totally ramified at the supersingular points of characteristic p.

Proof. By 4.6, the validity of 4.20 depends only on p\textasciicircum{}k, not on m; we may therefore suppose that m ≥ 3, so that we have to deal only with schemes.

Let E₀ be a supersingular elliptic curve over F̄\textunderscore{}p, and let E/Ŝ be the formal moduli scheme of E₀ over W(F̄\textunderscore{}p). We have Ŝ ≃ W(F̄\textunderscore{}p)[[t]]; it is the completion of the strict henselization of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}₁ at the given geometric point.

Let T̂ be the normalization of Ŝ in the Ŝ[1/p]-scheme Isom\textunderscore{}Ŝ(E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\},(ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}2). Since completions and normalizations commute,

T̂ = \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}₁\} Ŝ.

By 4.17, the irreducible components of T̂ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p are naturally indexed by P = ℙ¹(ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k). The connected components of T̂ define a partition \ensuremath{\mathcal{P}} of P. Assertion 4.20 means that T̂ is always connected, i.e. that \ensuremath{\mathcal{P}} is trivial.

Lemma 4.21. The partition \ensuremath{\mathcal{P}} is independent of E₀.

Proof. Let E₀ and E′₀ be two supersingular elliptic curves over F̄\textunderscore{}p, let Ŝ and Ŝ′ be their formal moduli schemes over W(F̄\textunderscore{}p), let E and E′ be their universal deformations over Ŝ and Ŝ′, and let T̂ and T̂′ be as in 4.20.

It is known that there exists an isogeny φ₀ : E₀ → E′₀ whose kernel K₀ is prime to p. Let N be an integer prime to p such that K₀ ⊂ E\textunderscore{}\{oN\}. Since E\textunderscore{}\{oN\} is étale, the infinitesimal deformations of E₀ identify with the infinitesimal deformations of the pair (E₀,K₀ ⊂ E\textunderscore{}\{oN\}), and there exists a unique isomorphism φ\textunderscore{}S : Ŝ → Ŝ′ giving rise to a commutative diagram

E ──φ──→ E′ │         │ ↓         ↓ Ŝ ──φ\textunderscore{}S─→ Ŝ′

in which φ extends φ₀. The isogeny φ induces an isomorphism from E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\} to E′\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\}, hence an isomorphism φ\textunderscore{}T : T̂ → T̂′ above φ\textunderscore{}S.

The bijection between the set I(T̂ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p) of irreducible components of T̂ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p and P can be described as follows: on T̂ there is a morphism

u : (ℤ/p\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}2 → E\textunderscore{}\{p\textasciicircum{}k\},

which is an isomorphism on T̂[1/p]. The element of P associated with a component of T̂ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p is the kernel of u at the generic point of that component. This description shows that the diagram

I(T̂ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p) ──φ\textunderscore{}T──→ I(T̂′ ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p) ║                         ║ P ═══════════════════════ P

Let us prove 4.20. If \ensuremath{\mathcal{P}} is nontrivial and X ∈ \ensuremath{\mathcal{P}}, the union of the closures (\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}A ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p)⁻ for A ∈ X is a connected component of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗ F̄\textunderscore{}p, which is therefore disconnected. This contradicts (IV 5.5).

VI. Coarse moduli schemes.

In this chapter we study coarse moduli schemes, in particular the relation between \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H and M\textunderscore{}H. Some classical results are proved by "pure thought". The result of § 5 is made more precise in the following chapter.

1. The modular invariant.

Theorem 1.1. The coarse space M\textunderscore{}1 and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is isomorphic to ℙ\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}ℤ.

Characteristics 2 and 3 make the direct verification of 1.1 somewhat laborious. We shall deduce 1.1 from its corollary

(1.1.1)        M\textunderscore{}\{1ℂ\} ≃ ℙ\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}ℂ.

a) M\textunderscore{}1 is proper and flat over Spec(ℤ), normal and hence Cohen--Macaulay (I.7.2), and the geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}1 over Spec(ℤ) are irreducible.

We already know that M\textunderscore{}1 is proper, flat, normal, with geometrically unibranch fibers (IV. 3.10). By 1.1.1 (or IV 5.4), the geometric generic fiber of M\textunderscore{}1 is connected. All geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}1 are therefore connected (Zariski) and, being unibranch, are irreducible.

b) The geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}1 over Spec(ℤ) are reduced.

Let G be the open substack of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 corresponding to generalized curves having no automorphisms other than Id and x ↦ -x. It is open because, for C/S a curve classified by \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1, Aut\textunderscore{}S(C) is finite and unramified over S (a consequence of the fact that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is separated).

The map G → M\textunderscore{}1 is étale, so its image A is smooth over ℤ. One verifies that G ⊃ \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞. The fibers of A are therefore nonempty; by a), they are dense in the fibers of M\textunderscore{}1. The (M\textunderscore{}1)\textunderscore{}\{s̄\} are thus generically reduced; being Cohen--Macaulay, they are in fact reduced.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0125-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0125-A05.}
\end{flushright}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 126; collected-paper page 619; original folio 268.}\par\smallskip
c) End of the proof.

By invariance of the arithmetic genus under specialization, and by (1.1.1), one finds that the geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}1 are integral and have arithmetic genus 0. They are therefore isomorphic to the projective line. In particular, M\textunderscore{}1 is proper and smooth over ℤ.

The Néron polygon with one side over Spec(ℤ) defines a section of M\textunderscore{}1, with image M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞. Fiber by geometric fiber one verifies that H\textasciicircum{}1(M\textunderscore{}1,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞)) = 0, and then that H\textasciicircum{}0(M\textunderscore{}1,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞)) is locally free of rank 2 over ℤ and its formation is compatible with passage to geometric fibers. Since ℤ is principal, H\textasciicircum{}0(M\textunderscore{}1,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞)) is free and has a basis \{1,j\}. Fiber by geometric fiber one verifies that \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}(M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞) is very ample and that j defines an isomorphism

M\textunderscore{}1 →̃ ℙ\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}ℤ.

Proof of (1.1.1) Here are two classical proofs of (1.1.1).

(1) One verifies that M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o(ℂ) is the quotient of the Poincaré half-plane by SL(2,ℤ) (IV 5.4). Drawing a fundamental domain, one finds that M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o(ℂ) is homeomorphic to ℝ\textasciicircum{}2. The space M\textunderscore{}1(ℂ), obtained from it by adjoining one point, is therefore a sphere.

(2) Over ℂ, the moduli scheme classifying integral generalized elliptic curves equipped with an invariant differential is

Spec(ℂ[g\textunderscore{}2,g\textunderscore{}3]) - (the point g\textunderscore{}2 = g\textunderscore{}3 = 0),

with universal curve, in nonhomogeneous coordinates,

⎧ Y\textasciicircum{}2 = 4X\textasciicircum{}3 - g\textunderscore{}2 X - g\textunderscore{}3 ⎩ ω = dX/Y                                      ([33] 2.5)

M\textunderscore{}1 is the quotient of this scheme by \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m acting by

λ.(g\textunderscore{}2,g\textunderscore{}3) = (λ\textasciicircum{}4 g\textunderscore{}2,λ\textasciicircum{}6 g\textunderscore{}3),

and is easily identified with ℙ\textasciicircum{}1.

The irreducibility of the fibers of M\textunderscore{}1 over ℤ has the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2. Let k be an algebraically closed field of characteristic p. There are only finitely many isomorphism classes of supersingular elliptic curves over k.

The set of points of (M\textunderscore{}1)\textunderscore{}k corresponding to generalized elliptic curves E for which the Frobenius kernel is of multiplicative type is open and contains the point at infinity. Its complement, which corresponds to supersingular curves, is therefore finite.

1.3. The function j introduced in 1.1 c) is not uniquely determined; the ambiguity is j ↦ ± j + n (n ∈ ℤ). A choice of j, recalled below, is classical.

Let C/S be a generalized elliptic curve with irreducible fibers over S. Tate's formulary [33] explains how to associate with C/S a section j of ℙ\textasciicircum{}1 over S, with j = ∞ wherever C is singular. By the universal property of M\textunderscore{}1, this morphism j : \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 → ℙ\textasciicircum{}1 factors through j : M\textunderscore{}1 → ℙ\textasciicircum{}1. One readily checks that j : M\textunderscore{}1(ℂ) → ℙ\textasciicircum{}1(ℂ) is bijective [33]. The morphism j is dominant, flat (I 7.1.), and of degree one, hence an isomorphism. In particular, over every algebraically closed field k, j(C\textunderscore{}1) = j(C\textunderscore{}2) if and only if C\textunderscore{}1 is isomorphic to C\textunderscore{}2. This is proved by brute force in [33].

The function j is called the modular invariant.

1.4. Let C be an irreducible generalized elliptic curve over an algebraically closed field k. It is proved in [33] that Aut(C) = \{±1\} if and only if j(C) ≠ 0,2\textasciicircum{}6.3\textasciicircum{}3. Over the open subset of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 where j ≠ 0, 2\textasciicircum{}6.3\textasciicircum{}3, Aut(C) (with C the universal curve) is therefore locally constant. Hence:

Lemma 1.5. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 → M\textunderscore{}1 is étale away from the sections j = 0,2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3 of M\textunderscore{}1.

By contrast, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 is ramified over M\textunderscore{}1 along these sections. Because of this ramification, there can be no section M\textunderscore{}1 → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1, i.e. there is no

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0127-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0127-A05.}

\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0128}{}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 128; collected-paper page 621; original folio 270.}\par\smallskip
generalized elliptic curve over ℙ\textasciicircum{}1 with modular invariant j. Tate has nevertheless shown that over ℙ\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}ℤ minus the two sections 0 and 2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3, i.e. over

S = Spec ℤ[1/j] (2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3 1/j - 1)\textasciicircum{}\{-1\},

there are two curves with invariant j. Put k = j-2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3, so that 1/j and 1/k are sections of \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S and j/k is an invertible section.

Proposition 1.6 (Tate). The curves

(1.6.1)        y\textasciicircum{}2 + xy = x\textasciicircum{}3 - (2\textasciicircum{}2 3\textasciicircum{}2/k) x - 1/k (1.6.2)        y\textasciicircum{}2 + xy = x\textasciicircum{}3 + (2\textasciicircum{}2 3\textasciicircum{}2/j) x - (j-2\textasciicircum{}7 3\textasciicircum{}2)/j\textasciicircum{}2

have modular invariant j.

For (1.6.1), c\textunderscore{}4 = j/k, c\textunderscore{}6 = - j/k, and Δ = j\textasciicircum{}2/k\textasciicircum{}3. For (1.6.2), c\textunderscore{}4 = k/j, c\textunderscore{}6 = - k\textasciicircum{}2/j\textasciicircum{}2, and Δ = k\textasciicircum{}3/j\textasciicircum{}4.

Write C\textunderscore{}o for the curve (1.6.1). If C is a curve over an open subset U of S with invariant j, Isom(C,C\textunderscore{}o) is a ℤ/2-torsor τ(C), and C is obtained from C\textunderscore{}o by "twisting" by τ(C). The curves C are therefore classified by H\textasciicircum{}1(U,ℤ/2) (étale H\textasciicircum{}1). For example, (1.6.2) corresponds to the double cover of S with normalization S[(j/k)\textasciicircum{}\{1/2\}]. By calculating this H\textasciicircum{}1, Tate showed that (1.6.1) and (1.6.2) are the only elliptic curves with invariant j over ℙ\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}ℤ minus the three sections 0,2\textasciicircum{}6.3\textasciicircum{}3 and ∞.

Proposition 1.7. Let k be a field and let j ∈ k. There exists an elliptic curve over k with modular invariant j.

The case j ≠ 0,2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3 follows from 1.6. If k has characteristic p and j = 0 or 2\textasciicircum{}6 3\textasciicircum{}3, one may use the following affine equation.

p ≠ 3,    j = 0             y\textasciicircum{}2 + y = x\textasciicircum{}3 - 1 p ≠ 2,    j = 2\textasciicircum{}6.3\textasciicircum{}3       y\textasciicircum{}2 = x\textasciicircum{}3 - x.

1.8. Let C be the universal elliptic curve over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o, and let γ be the morphism

Aut(C) → Aut(Lie(C)) ≃ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m. We extract from [33] the following result about Aut(C).

Lemma 1.9. Over the substack of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/6] defined by c\textunderscore{}4 = 0 (resp. c\textunderscore{}6 = 0) (i.e. ³√j = 0, resp. ²√(j-1728) = 0), γ is an isomorphism Aut(C) →̃ μ\textunderscore{}6 (resp. Aut(C) →̃ μ\textunderscore{}4).

2. Automorphisms of elliptic curves. Criterion for \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o = M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o.

2.1. For an algebraic stack to be an algebraic space, it is necessary and sufficient that its geometric points have no nontrivial automorphisms. Thus, if H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n), in order that \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] = M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] (resp. \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H[1/n] = M\textunderscore{}H[1/n]), it is necessary and sufficient that an elliptic curve (resp. generalized elliptic curve) equipped with an H-level structure, over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p ∤ n, have no nontrivial automorphism.

For example, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n[1/n] = M\textunderscore{}n[1/n] for n ≥ 3 (IV 2.7).

In [32], the automorphisms of elliptic curves are described explicitly, so the preceding criterion is effective. The calculations of [33] show that characteristics 2 and 3 are exceptional, while the others are "like" characteristic 0. We propose to verify this point a priori. So as not to seem too ridiculous, we shall also treat the case of abelian varieties.

Lemma 2.2. Let (A, θ) be a polarized abelian variety over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p > 0, and let φ be an automorphism of (A, θ). It is known that φ has finite order. If the degree of θ and the order of φ are prime to p, the triple (A, θ, φ) lifts to the ring of Witt vectors W(k).

There is no obstruction to deforming (A, θ): the formal moduli scheme of (A, θ) over W(k) is a scheme

M = Spec(W/k)[[t\textunderscore{}1...t\textunderscore{}r]]        (k = ½ dim(A) (dim(A) + 1)).

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0129-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0129-A05.}

Continuing the proof of Lemma 2.2, interpret φ as the action on (A, θ) of a cyclic group G whose order is prime to p. By transport of structure, G acts on the formal moduli scheme M: the automorphism φ of (A, θ) extends to an automorphism of M and of the universal deformation (A\textunderscore{}M, θ\textunderscore{}M) of (A, θ) over M. The formal moduli scheme of (A, θ, φ) is the G-fixed subscheme M\textasciicircum{}G of M. Since the order of G is prime to p, this subscheme is smooth over W(k), hence admits sections over W(k), and this proves 2.2.

Proposition 2.3. Let A be an abelian variety of dimension g over an algebraically closed field. Let φ be an automorphism of prime order q, and write

A\textasciicircum{}φ =\textunderscore{}dfn Ker(φ - id\textunderscore{}A).

There is an integer k such that

2.g = dim(A\textasciicircum{}φ) + k.(q-1).

Proof: Passing from A to A/A\textasciicircum{}φ, we reduce to the case in which A\textasciicircum{}φ is finite.

The characteristic polynomial of φ acting on A has integer coefficients, does not have 1 as a root, and has only qth roots of unity as roots. It is therefore a power of the cyclotomic polynomial, which has degree q-1. Comparing degrees proves the lemma.

Corollary 2.4. Let A be an abelian variety of dimension g over an algebraically closed field. Let φ be an automorphism of A of finite order. Every prime factor of the order of φ is smaller than 2g + 1.

Proof: This follows from 2.4.

Corollary 2.5. Let (A, θ) be a polarized abelian variety of dimension g over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p, with polarization degree prime to p. Suppose that p > 2g + 1. If φ is an automorphism of (A, θ), then (A, θ, φ) lifts to characteristic 0 (over W(k)).

Proof: This follows from 2.2 and 2.4.

2.6. An elliptic curve has a canonical principal polarization. By 2.6, if φ is an automorphism of an elliptic curve E over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p > 3, (E,φ) lifts to characteristic 0 (over W(k)).

Theorem 2.7. For \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/6n] to be an algebraic space, it is necessary and sufficient that the inverse image Γ of H in SL(2,ℤ) have no finite-order element other than 1.

Let (E,ᾱ) be an elliptic curve equipped with a level-H structure over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic p = 0 or p > 3, with p ∤ n. We must show that every automorphism φ of (E,ᾱ) is trivial. By 2.6, (E,φ), and hence (E,ᾱ,φ), lifts to characteristic 0, so it is enough to treat the case p = 0.

The structure ᾱ defines an inclusion (IV.3.20) ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\textasciicircum{}\{det H\} ⊂ k. We may reduce to the case in which k has finite absolute transcendence degree; then there is an embedding k ↪ ℂ inducing the identity inclusion of ℚ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] in ℂ. This reduces us to k = ℂ, with ᾱ represented by a determinant-one isomorphism α : E\textunderscore{}n →̃ (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2.

If β : ℤ\textasciicircum{}2 → H\textunderscore{}1(E(ℂ),ℤ) lifts the inverse of α (via IV 5.2.1), one verifies that Aut(E,ᾱ) is the stabilizer of z(β) (IV 5.1) in Γ. If Aut(E,ᾱ) ≠ \{e\}, Γ therefore has elements of finite order.

Conversely, every finite-order element φ of Γ has a fixed point in X, corresponding to (E,ᾱ,φ) over ℂ.

3. Rational points of coarse schemes.

3.1. Let H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n). An elliptic curve E equipped with a level-H structure α over a field k defines a point of M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o(k). Conversely, if x ∈ M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o[1/n](k), all one knows is that over the separable closure k̄ of k there exists a pair (E,α) defining x. The isomorphism class of (E,α) is invariant under Gal(k̄/k).

\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0131-A05.png}}

\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 132; collected-paper page 625; original folio 274.}\par\smallskip
If (E,α) comes from (E\textunderscore{}0,α\textunderscore{}0) over k, the set of k-isomorphism classes of curves (E\textunderscore{}1,α\textunderscore{}1) defining x is canonically H\textasciicircum{}1(Gal(k̄/k),A̲u̲t̲(E\textunderscore{}0,α\textunderscore{}0)(k̄)). The obstruction to the existence of an (E\textunderscore{}0,α\textunderscore{}0) lies in an H\textasciicircum{}2 (in Giraud's sense). In the special case where Aut(E,α) is commutative, Gal(k̄/k) acts on Aut(E,α), and the obstruction lies in H\textasciicircum{}2(Gal(k̄/k),Aut(E,α)).

Proposition 3.2. Let k be a field of characteristic p and let H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n). Assume that p ∤ n. Every point x ∈ M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o(k) is defined by an elliptic curve E over k equipped with a level-H structure.

Proof (after J.P. Serre and J.S. Milne). Choose a separable closure k̄ of k and an object (E,α) over k̄ that defines the image of x in M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o(k̄). If H = GL(2, ℤ/n), which amounts to assuming n = 1, Proposition 3.2 reduces to 1.7. In the language of 3.1, a certain obstruction ε(E) is therefore zero. If Aut(E,α) is trivial, Proposition 3.2 is clear. If Aut(E) is reduced to ± 1 and Aut(E,α) is nontrivial, then Aut(E,α) →̃ Aut(E); the obstructions ε(E,α) and ε(E) to defining (E,α) or E over k are therefore equal and zero. To complete the proof, it is enough to treat the following two cases.

Case 1: p ≠ 2,3. By 1.9, Aut(E) is then μ\textunderscore{}2, μ\textunderscore{}4, or μ\textunderscore{}6. The automorphism group Aut(E,α) of (E,α) is a subgroup of Aut(E). Proposition 3.2 says that the obstruction class ε(E,α) ∈ H\textasciicircum{}2(Gal(k̄/k), Aut(E,α)) is zero. By 1.7 its image ε(E) in H\textasciicircum{}2(Gal(k̄/k), Aut(E)) is zero, and we apply the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3. Let n be prime to p.

(i) The subgroups of μ\textunderscore{}n are the μ\textunderscore{}m, for m|n.

(ii) The map H\textasciicircum{}2(Gal(k̄/k), μ\textunderscore{}m) → Gal(k̄/k, μ\textunderscore{}n) is injective.

Proof. We leave the proof of (i) to the reader. Consider the long exact cohomology sequence associated with the short exact sequence

0 → μ\textunderscore{}m → μ\textunderscore{}n ──(x ↦ x\textasciicircum{}m)→ μ\textunderscore{}\{n/m\} → 0 : H\textasciicircum{}1(μ\textunderscore{}n) ──①→ H\textasciicircum{}1(μ\textunderscore{}\{n/m\}) ──j→ H\textasciicircum{}2(μ\textunderscore{}m) ──②→ H\textasciicircum{}2(μ\textunderscore{}n).

To prove that ② is injective, it is enough to prove that ① is surjective. By Hilbert's Theorem 90, H\textasciicircum{}1(μ\textunderscore{}n) = k\textasciicircum{}*/k\textasciicircum{}\{*n\}; ① is passage to the quotient

k\textasciicircum{}*/k\textasciicircum{}\{*m/n\} → k\textasciicircum{}*/k\textasciicircum{}\{*m/n\},

and 3.3 follows.

Case 2: p ≠ 0 and Aut(E) is not reduced to ±1.

In this case, x is obtained by scalar extension from a point of M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o with values in a finite field (one has j = 0 or 2\textasciicircum{}6.3\textasciicircum{}3, and the field of definition of x is algebraic over the field generated by j(x)). We may therefore suppose that k is finite. Since Gal(k̄/k) = ℤ̂ and Aut(E,α) is finite, the Giraud H\textasciicircum{}2 in which the obstruction lies vanishes, and 3.2 follows. In other words, let x ∈ M\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}q); there is a finite extension \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\} of \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}q and a pair (E,α) over \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\} that defines x. The curve (E,α) is geometrically isomorphic to its conjugates under Gal(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\}/\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}q). After replacing \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\} by an extension, we may therefore suppose that there is an isomorphism

φ′ : (E,α)\textasciicircum{}(q) ⟶̃ (E,α).

This isomorphism defines descent data from \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\} to \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}q if and only if the nth power of φ : E —Fr→ E\textasciicircum{}(q) —φ′→ E is the Frobenius endomorphism F of E/\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}n\}. In general, φ\textasciicircum{}n = Fβ, where β has finite order m. Thus φ\textasciicircum{}\{nm\} = F\textasciicircum{}m, and after extending scalars to \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{nm\}\}, φ′ defines descent data from \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{nm\}\} to \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}q for (E,α). The descended curve solves the problem.

4. Numerical remarks

We propose to translate into the language of algebraic stacks the classical formulas giving the genera of modular curves. To do so, we must first define the Euler--Poincaré characteristic χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) of an algebraic stack of finite presentation over ℂ. This notion is related to Wall's virtual Euler--Poincaré characteristic of a discrete group.

4.1. a) Let (\ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}}\textunderscore{}i) be a finite partition of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} into separated locally closed substacks such that, over \ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}}\textunderscore{}i, the group scheme of automorphisms of the objects classified by \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} is locally constant of rank u\textunderscore{}i. In other words, for every geometric point x → \ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}}\textunderscore{}i, we require |Aut(x)| = u\textunderscore{}i. Such a decomposition exists.

b) Let F\textunderscore{}i be the coarse moduli scheme of \ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}}\textunderscore{}i, and let χ(F\textunderscore{}i) be the Euler--Poincaré characteristic of the topological space F\textunderscore{}i(ℂ). Set

χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) =₍dfn₎ Σ (1/u\textunderscore{}i) χ(F\textunderscore{}i(ℂ)).

One checks that this definition is independent of the decomposition (\ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}}\textunderscore{}i). It agrees with the usual definition when \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} "is" a scheme.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0133-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0133-A05.}
\end{flushright}

\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 134}{D018-P0134-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 134; collected-paper page 627; original folio 276.}\par\smallskip
The Euler--Poincaré characteristic has the following properties:

α) additivity: if \ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}} is a closed substack of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} with open complement \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}},

χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) = χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}) + χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{Z}});

if \ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} and \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}} are two open substacks,

χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} ∪ \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}}) + χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}} ∩ \ensuremath{\mathcal{V}}) = χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{U}}) + χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{V}}).

β) multiplicativity: if f : \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} → \ensuremath{\mathcal{D}} is a morphism and the geometric fibers \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s of f have constant Euler--Poincaré characteristic, then

χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) = χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{D}}).χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s).

γ) algebraic character: the definition extends readily and makes it possible to define χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) for an algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} of finite presentation over an algebraically closed field k of characteristic zero.

δ) Let f : \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} → \ensuremath{\mathcal{D}} be a finite flat morphism (i.e. flat, proper, with finite fibers), and let s be a geometric point of \ensuremath{\mathcal{D}}. For a geometric point x of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s, let Ã\textunderscore{}x be the local ring of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s at x. It is an Artinian ring. Set

(4.1.1)        deg\textunderscore{}s(f) = Σ\textunderscore{}x (1/|Aut(x)|) dim\textunderscore{}\{k(s)\}(A\textunderscore{}x).

This number is locally constant in s. If it is constant, denote it by deg(f). If f is finite étale, then χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s) = deg\textunderscore{}s(f), and β) gives

(4.1.2)        χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}) = deg(f) χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{D}}).

More concretely, a closed geometric point s of \ensuremath{\mathcal{D}} is simply an object of \ensuremath{\mathcal{D}} over ℂ, and a geometric point of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s is an object x of \ensuremath{\mathcal{C}} equipped with an isomorphism α : f(x) ↔ s. One has

(4.1.3)        χ(\ensuremath{\mathcal{C}}\textunderscore{}s) = Σ 1/Aut(x,α).

4.2. Apply Definition 4.1 to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o ⊗ ℂ.

We separate the closed substacks "c\textunderscore{}4 = 0" (6 automorphisms), "c\textunderscore{}6 = 0" (4 auto-

Continuing the preceding sentence, the complementary open (2 automorphisms) has as coarse scheme the affine line with the points j = 0 and j = 1728 removed. We find

(4.2.1)        χ(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o ⊗ ℂ) = 1/6 + 1/4 + -1/2 = -1/12,

or, more pleasantly,

(4.2.2)        χ(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o ⊗ ℂ) = ζ(-1).

For H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n), it follows from 4.1.5 that

(4.2.3)        χ(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}o ⊗ ℂ) = [GL(2, ℤ/n) : H] . -1/12.

To pass from this to the classical formula for χ(M\textunderscore{}H ⊗ ℂ), the cusps and the automorphisms must be taken into account. Consider M\textunderscore{}n ⊗ ℂ for n ≥ 3. Let ±U be the group of matrices ±(1  u; 0  1), with u ∈ ℤ/nℤ. There are [GL(2, ℤ/n) : ±U] cusps (5.1), and the objects of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗ ℂ have no nontrivial automorphisms (IV 2.7).

Therefore

χ(M\textunderscore{}n(ℂ)) = |GL(2, ℤ/n)| . -1/12 + [GL(2, ℤ/n) : ±U]

= -1/12 n\textasciicircum{}4 . ∏\textunderscore{}\{p|n\} (1 - 1/p\textasciicircum{}2)(1 - 1/p) + n\textasciicircum{}3/2 ∏\textunderscore{}\{p|n\} (1 - 1/p\textasciicircum{}2)(1 - 1/p).

The curve M\textunderscore{}n(ℂ) has φ(n) connected components, all isomorphic. Their genus g is given by

2g - 2 = ∏\textunderscore{}\{p|n\} (1 - 1/p\textasciicircum{}2) . (n\textasciicircum{}3/12 - n\textasciicircum{}2/2).

4.3. Let C be a proper Cohen-Macaulay algebraic stack, pure of dimension one, over an algebraically closed field k (for example, \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ ℂ). Let ω be an invertible sheaf on C. Its degree is defined as follows.

a) Choose a rational section f of ω.

b) Let x be a closed geometric point of C, and let Õ\textunderscore{}x be the henselian local ring of C at x. If f is regular at x, set deg\textunderscore{}x(f) = dim\textunderscore{}k Õ\textunderscore{}x/(f). If f\textasciicircum{}\{-1\} is regular there, set deg\textunderscore{}x(f) = - dim\textunderscore{}k Õ\textunderscore{}x/(f\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}).

The definition continues on physical page 136.

Continuing §4.3, set

deg(ω) = deg(C,ω) = Σ\textunderscore{}x 1/|Aut(x)| deg\textunderscore{}x(f).

When k is not algebraically closed, degree is defined after first extending scalars to k̄. This degree is independent of the choice of f and agrees with the usual notion when C "is" a scheme.

It satisfies:

α) additivity in ω: deg(ω ⊗ ω') = deg(ω) + deg(ω').

β) coverings: Let f : C' → C be a finite flat morphism of constant degree. Then

deg(f*ω) = deg(f). deg(ω).

γ) specialization: Let S be a trait and C a proper flat Cohen-Macaulay algebraic stack, pure of relative dimension one over S. Let ω be an invertible sheaf on C. Then

deg(C\textunderscore{}η,ω) = deg(C\textunderscore{}s,ω).

4.4. Compute on \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 the degree of ω, the dual of the Lie algebra of the universal elliptic curve. The discriminant Δ is a section of ω\textasciicircum{}(⊗12) vanishing simply at infinity. Since the point at infinity has two automorphisms, one finds

(4.4.1)        deg(ω) = 1/24.

Starting from (4.4.1) and using (4.2.2) and 4.3(β), one obtains, for every H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n),

4.5. Here is a direct proof of (4.4.2). At every point s of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o over ℂ corresponding to an elliptic curve E, deformation theory gives an isomorphism between the tangent space to \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o at s and H\textasciicircum{}1(E,T\textunderscore{}E\textasciicircum{}1) ≃ H\textasciicircum{}1(E,O) ⊗ ω\textasciicircum{}(⊗-1) ≃ ω\textunderscore{}s\textasciicircum{}(⊗-2). At infinity, the identification is

(4.5.1)        Ω\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\}(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}∞) ≃ ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗2\}

Since the pullback of a differential form with logarithmic poles is again of this type, (4.5.1) gives us an isomorphism

(4.5.2)        Ω\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\}(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞) ≃ ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗2\} ;

if c is the number of points at infinity of \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n, we therefore have

χ(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}o ⊗ ℂ) = χ(\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗ ℂ) - c = - deg(Ω\textasciicircum{}1) - c = - deg(Ω\textasciicircum{}1(∞)) = - deg(ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗2\}) = -2 deg(ω).

In the remainder of this section, we define the Hasse invariant and give a proof, based on theorems of Grothendieck, of Igusa's theorem that its zeros are simple. One of us learned the elegant formula 4.9.1 in a lecture by Serre.

4.6. Definition of the Hasse invariant.

Let f : E → S be a generalized elliptic curve over a scheme S of characteristic p, let ω = f\textunderscore{}* ω\textunderscore{}\{E/S\}, and let F be the Frobenius morphism

E ──F→ E\textasciicircum{}(p) f↘     ↙f\textasciicircum{}(p) S

We have f\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}(p) ω\textunderscore{}\{E\textasciicircum{}(p)/S\} = ω\textasciicircum{}(p) = ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗p\}. The trace map (also called the "Cartier operation")

(4.6.1)        Tr\textunderscore{}F : ω → ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗p\},

i.e. a section

(4.6.2)        h ∈ Γ(S,ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(p-1)\}).

This is the Hasse invariant. Here is a dual description: coherent-sheaf duality pairs ω with R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}* \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}, and likewise pairs ω\textasciicircum{}(p) with R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}(p) \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}. The transpose of (4.6.1) is the pullback

(4.6.3)        F\textasciicircum{}* : R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}(p) \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} → R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}* \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}.

If R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}* \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} and R\textasciicircum{}1 f\textunderscore{}*\textasciicircum{}(p) \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}} are identified with ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(-1)\} and ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗(-p)\}, then (4.6.3) becomes multiplication by h from (4.6.2).

On \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p, the Hasse invariant is known not to vanish at infinity, and its zeros correspond to supersingular elliptic curves (see Katz [13]).

The zeros of h on \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p are known to be simple. In 4.8 we give a direct proof based on Grothendieck's theory of deformations of abelian schemes; a special case of that theory is recalled below.

4.7. Let A\textunderscore{}o be an abelian variety over an algebraically closed field k. For every deformation A of A\textunderscore{}o over the dual numbers k[ε], where ε\textasciicircum{}2 = 0, define

H\textunderscore{}DR(A) = H\textasciicircum{}1(A, Ω\textasciicircum{}*\textunderscore{}\{A/Spec(k[ε])\}).

(4.7.1)        0 → H\textasciicircum{}0(A, Ω\textasciicircum{}1\textunderscore{}A) → H\textunderscore{}DR(A) → H\textasciicircum{}1(A, \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}) → 0.

5. The Galois action on the cusps.

5.1. The set \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞(ℂ) is the set of isomorphism classes, over ℂ, of singular generalized elliptic curves with n sides, equipped with a level-n structure.

Let (C,+) be the standard n-gon over ℂ, equipped with its generalized elliptic-curve structure II.1.9. Canonically,

C\textunderscore{}n = μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n.

The automorphism group of (C,+) was computed in II 1.10. Its image in GL(μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n) is the group ± U of matrices

± ⎛1  u⎞        (u ∈ Hom(ℤ/n, μ\textunderscore{}n)). ⎝0  1⎠

The set of level-n structures on (C,+) is Isom(C\textunderscore{}n,(ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2). Thus, canonically,

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞(ℂ) = Isom(μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n, (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2) / ± U.

This bijection is compatible with the action of Gal(ℂ/ℚ) on both sides.

5.2. For H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/n), we have \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H = \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}n/H. Consequently,

\ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞(ℂ) = H∖Isom(μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n, (ℤ/n)\textasciicircum{}2)/± U.

Since \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∞[1/n] is finite étale over ℤ[1/n], this isomorphism of Galois sets extends to an isomorphism of schemes:

6. Study of M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}.

6.1. In IV.4.5 we described \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] as the stack over ℤ[1/n] classifying isogenies E → F with cyclic kernel of degree n. This exhibits two maps \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}[1/n] → \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/n], defined respectively by E and F and interchanged by an involution w (IV.4.4). By IV.3.19, the induced maps on the coarse schemes extend to

(6.1.1)        c and cw : M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\} → M\textunderscore{}1.

6.2. The finite map (c,cw) : M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\} → M\textunderscore{}1 × M\textunderscore{}1 has as its image a divisor invariant under permutation of the coordinates. Since M\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\} → M\textunderscore{}1 has degree equal to the number P(n) of points of the projective line over ℤ/n and is generically injective, this divisor has bidegree (P(n), P(n)). The maps c and cw carry points at infinity to points at infinity. Since M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o = Spec(ℤ[j]) and ℤ[j,j′] is factorial, the image divisor of

(6.2.1)        (c,cw) : M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\} → M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o × M\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o

is defined by an equation Φ\textunderscore{}n ∈ ℤ[j,j′], the modular equation. We have

Φ\textunderscore{}n(X,Y) = Σ\textunderscore{}\{a,b≤P(n)\} c\textunderscore{}ab X\textasciicircum{}a Y\textasciicircum{}b,

and the only nonzero c\textunderscore{}ab for which a = P(n) or b = P(n) occur at (a,b) = (P(n),0) or (0,P(n)); they are ±1.

6.3. Symmetry shows that Φ\textunderscore{}n(X,Y) = ±Φ\textunderscore{}n(Y,X). It is classical, and follows from a study at infinity, that for n ≠ 1 the correct sign is +.

(6.3.1)        Φ\textunderscore{}n(X,Y) = Φ\textunderscore{}n(Y,X)        (n > 1).

We may therefore normalize the sign of Φ\textunderscore{}n so as to have

6.4. One should note that the map (6.2.1) is not necessarily injective. Over an algebraically closed field k in which n is invertible, there may indeed exist two cyclic-kernel n-isogenies

(6.4.1)        φ\textunderscore{}1 : E\textunderscore{}1 → F\textunderscore{}1 φ\textunderscore{}2 : E\textunderscore{}2 → F\textunderscore{}2

that are not isomorphic, even though there are isomorphisms e : E\textunderscore{}1 ≃→ E\textunderscore{}2 and f : F\textunderscore{}1 → F\textunderscore{}2. The endomorphism g = e\textasciicircum{}\{-1\} φ\textunderscore{}2\textasciicircum{}* f φ\textunderscore{}1 of E\textunderscore{}1 then has degree n\textasciicircum{}2, but its kernel is not the kernel of multiplication by n. Thus E\textunderscore{}1 has complex multiplication.

Conversely, let g be an endomorphism of degree n\textasciicircum{}2 of an elliptic curve E, suppose Ker(g) ≠ E\textunderscore{}n, and let A ⊂ Ker(g) be cyclic of order n. A pair (6.4.1) is obtained by taking

E → E/A, and the transpose of E/Ker(g) ← E/A.

The preceding discussion nevertheless shows that (6.2.1) is generically radicial; one easily shows that there is even an open subset U of M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}, dense in the fibers of characteristic p ∤ n, such that the restriction of (6.2.1) to U is an immersion.

The following result follows.

Proposition 6.5. M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\} is the spectrum of the normalization of ℤ[j,j′]/(Φ\textunderscore{}n(j,j′)).

If k is a field of characteristic p and p ∤ n, it follows from 3.2 that every point x ∈ M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{Γ\textunderscore{}o(n)\}(k) is defined by a cyclic-kernel n-isogeny

Theorem 6.6. (H. Weber-Lehrbuch der Algebra III, p.244 eq. (32)).

Φ\textunderscore{}p(j,j') ≡ (j-j'\textasciicircum{}p)(j\textasciicircum{}p-j') mod p.

Proposition 6.7. Let H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/n), and let K be the inverse image of H in GL(2,ℤ̂). The scheme M\textunderscore{}K[1/n] is smooth over ℤ[1/n].

Over the complement of the sections 0, 2\textasciicircum{}6·3\textasciicircum{}3, and ∞ of M\textunderscore{}1, the map M\textunderscore{}K[1/n] → M\textunderscore{}1[1/n] is finite étale. Indeed, over this open set \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K[1/n] (respectively \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1[1/n]) is étale over M\textunderscore{}K[1/n] (respectively M\textunderscore{}1[1/n]), and \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}K\textasciicircum{}o[1/n] is étale over \ensuremath{\mathfrak{m}}\textunderscore{}1\textasciicircum{}o[1/n].

It therefore follows from Abhyankar's lemma that M\textunderscore{}K[1/n] is smooth over ℤ[1/n] away from the points of characteristic 2 or 3 with modular invariant 0.

The group A = Aut(E) acts on W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}2)[[t]], and, in view of I.8, one must prove that for every subgroup B of A the algebra W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}2)[[t]]\textasciicircum{}B is formally smooth over W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}2). The group A embeds in GL(2,\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}3) (IV.2.7). In fact,

A = SL(2,\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}3).

It follows that if one of the algebras W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}2)[[t]]\textasciicircum{}B were not formally smooth, then already one of the schemes M\textunderscore{}H (H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/3)) would not be smooth over ℤ.

The curve M\textunderscore{}3/ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}3] is known to have genus 0. Hence, for H ⊂ GL(2,ℤ/3), M\textunderscore{}H = M\textunderscore{}3/H over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}3]\textasciicircum{}H has genus 0. The geometric fibers of M\textunderscore{}H[1/3] are also known to be generically smooth, irreducible, and reduced (being Cohen--Macaulay, with M\textunderscore{}H normal). Since they have arithmetic genus 0, they are smooth.

Characteristic 3: The same argument applies, using the embedding Aut(E) ⊂ GL(2,\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}4) and the fact that M\textunderscore{}4/ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}4] has genus 0.

(6.9.1)        W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[X,Y]]/(X.Y-p\textasciicircum{}k).

Proof: In a neighborhood of infinity, the map \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} → M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} is étale, and M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} is smooth over ℤ. By Abhyankar's lemma, M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} is tamely ramified over M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} along infinity, and hence is smooth in a neighborhood of infinity. Let us now study the points at finite distance.

Passing to coarse moduli schemes, (V.1.15) and 6.1.1 yield the displayed diagram, with the central upper label “coarse moduli scheme of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p” and the caption “composed diagonals = Frobenius.”

We know that \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is reduced (V.1.16). Its coarse moduli scheme therefore is reduced as well. Since M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} is normal, M\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is Cohen-Macaulay. Being generically reduced, this scheme is reduced. We may now argue as in V.1.16 to prove (i),(ii). The same arguments apply to the coarse moduli scheme of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textasciicircum{}o\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p, and we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 6.10. M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p is the coarse moduli scheme of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p.

We prove 6.9 (iii). Let (E,α) and x be as indicated. Let A' be the automorphism group of (E,α), and set

A=A' if -1∉A',             A=A'/\{±1\} if -1∈A'.

The group A has order k. By I.8, the completion of the strict henselization of M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} at x is Spec(W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[X,Y]]/(XY-p))/A, where A acts faithfully (I.8 and I.4). After reduction modulo p, A preserves each branch and acts faithfully on each one. We also know, by I.5.2 and (ii), that the quotient has the form Spec(W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[X',Y']] /(X'Y'-p\textasciicircum{}\{k'\})), and we must prove k=k'.

By 6.10, Spec(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p[[X,Y]]/(X.Y))/A = Spec(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p[[X',Y']] /(X'.Y')), and hence, upon restriction to one branch, Spec(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p[[X,Y]]/(Y))/A = Spec(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p[[X'Y']] /(Y')).

Since A has order k, it follows that X\textasciicircum{}k=unit·X' (mod(p,Y)). In the spectrum of the regular ring W(\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p)[[X,Y]]/(XY-p), the divisors of X and X' have the same support. The preceding formula therefore forces div(X')=k div(X), i.e. X\textasciicircum{}k=unit·X'. The same argument applies to Y'; hence X'Y'=unit·p\textasciicircum{}k, and it follows that k=k'.

Specialization invariance of the Euler-Poincaré characteristic with ℚ-coefficients, together with 6.9(ii), gives the following topological Euler-Poincaré formula.

Corollary 6.11. Let s be the number of supersingular points on M\textunderscore{}K ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p. Then

(6.11.1)        χ(M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\}) = 2χ(M\textunderscore{}K)-2s.

If M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} is regarded as a geometrically irreducible curve over R=ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\textasciicircum{}H, and if s₀=s/[R:ℤ] denotes the number of supersingular points of M\textunderscore{}K ⊗\textunderscore{}R \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p, then 6.11 can be rewritten in terms of the genus as

(6.11.2)        g(M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}R ℂ) = 2g(M\textunderscore{}K)+(s₀-1).

Corollary 6.12. M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}R ℂ is an elliptic curve if and only if M\textunderscore{}K ⊗\textunderscore{}R ℂ has genus 0 and s₀=2.

Reminder 6.13. Resolving the singularity (6.9.1) by blow-ups produces a chain of (k-1) projective lines of self-intersection -2. The locus p=0 in the resolution is reduced. This makes it possible to calculate the minimal resolution of M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\}.

The following lemma, taken from [20], will be proved in VII.2.7.

Lemma 6.14. Let E be an elliptic curve over the fraction field of a discrete valuation ring V. If the special fiber of the minimal Néron model of E is a Néron k-gon, then v(j(E))=-k.

When M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} is elliptic, Corollary 6.11 gives the following result.

Corollary 6.15. Suppose M\textunderscore{}\{K∩Γ₀(p)\} ⊗\textunderscore{}R ℂ is elliptic, with modular invariant j. The order of the pole of j∈R at a prime ideal π of R dividing p is

-v\textunderscore{}π(j) = 2 + Σ\textunderscore{}\{(E,α)\} (|Aut(E,α)/\{±1\}|-1),

where the sum ranges over the two-element set of isomorphism classes of

6.16. Example. Consider M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\}, where p is prime and p≠2,3. Its special fiber M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p is obtained by gluing two copies of M\textunderscore{}1 ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p = ℙ¹\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}̄\textunderscore{}p\} at the supersingular points.

To obtain the special fiber of the minimal resolution, a supersingular point j=0 must be replaced by a chain of two projective lines, while a supersingular point j=1728 must be replaced by a chain of one projective line. Recall that

j=0 is supersingular      ⇔ p≡-1 (mod 6), j=1728 is supersingular   ⇔ p≡-1 (mod 4).

Thus, over an algebraic closure of \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}\textunderscore{}p, the special fiber of the minimal resolution of M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} is represented by the displayed diagram of copies of ℙ¹ meeting transversely. Its left-hand labels are j=0, other, and j=1728.

Formula 6.15 says that the modular invariant of the genus-one curve M\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\}, for p=11,17,19, has denominator 11\textasciicircum{}5,17\textasciicircum{}4,19\textasciicircum{}3, respectively.

VII. The Tate curve.

In §1 of this chapter, we use a method due to Raynaud to construct the Tate curve \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ over ℤ[[q]]. This enables us to give an explicit description of the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H along infinity (§2). In §§3 and 4, we deduce integrality theorems for the coefficients of the Fourier-series expansions of modular forms.

1. Construction of the Tate curve over ℤ[[q]].

1.1. Let S be a scheme and t∈Γ(S,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S). We propose to construct over S a scheme Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t, isomorphic to \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m over S[t\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}], non-quasi-compact when t is not invertible, and from which generalized elliptic curves will be obtained by taking a quotient when t is nilpotent.

a) Let (x\textunderscore{}i)\textunderscore{}\{i∈ℤ\} be indeterminates. Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t is the union of the local charts (U\textunderscore{}j)\textunderscore{}\{j∈1/2+ℤ\}:

(1.1.1)        U\textunderscore{}j = S[x\textunderscore{}\{j-1/2\},y\textunderscore{}\{j+1/2\}] /(x\textunderscore{}\{j-1/2\}y\textunderscore{}\{j+1/2\}-t).

b) Glue U\textunderscore{}\{i-1/2\} and U\textunderscore{}\{i+1/2\} so that their overlap is U\textunderscore{}\{i+1/2\}[1/x\textunderscore{}i]≃S[x\textunderscore{}i,x\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}], with y\textunderscore{}\{i+1\}=t x\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}, and also U\textunderscore{}\{i-1/2\}[1/y\textunderscore{}i]≃S[y\textunderscore{}i,y\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}], with x\textunderscore{}\{i-1\}=t y\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}; these opens are identified by x\textunderscore{}i y\textunderscore{}i=1.

c) No gluings other than those imposed by b) are made. If |j-j'|≥2, U\textunderscore{}j∩U\textunderscore{}\{j'\} lies over S[1/t]; over S[1/t], all the U\textunderscore{}j are identified by

y\textunderscore{}i=t\textasciicircum{}\{-i\}y\textunderscore{}0.

1.2 Examples: a) if t is invertible, Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t=U\textunderscore{}0=S[x\textunderscore{}0,x\textunderscore{}0\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]≃\ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m.

b) if t=0, Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t is obtained by gluing the disjoint union of a family of copies of ℙ¹ indexed by ℤ: the ith copy is glued to the (i+1)st by identifying the zero section of the ith copy with the infinity section of the (i+1)st.

For each j, U\textunderscore{}j is the union of the (j-1/2)th copy minus its infinity section and the (j+1/2)th copy minus its zero section, with x\textunderscore{}\{j-1/2\}=(coordinate x)∪0 and y\textunderscore{}\{j+1/2\}=0∪(coordinate x)\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}.

The schematic shows an infinite vertical chain with successive junctions x\textunderscore{}1=0, y\textunderscore{}1=0, x\textunderscore{}0=0, y\textunderscore{}0=0, x\textunderscore{}\{-1\}=0, y\textunderscore{}\{-1\}=0. Overlapping braces label U\textunderscore{}\{1/2\}, U\textunderscore{}\{-1/2\}, and U\textunderscore{}\{1/2\}∩U\textunderscore{}\{-1/2\}=S[x\textunderscore{}0,x\textunderscore{}0\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]. The chain meets the base S at the special fiber t=0, and the generic part is labeled “away from t=0: \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m.”

c) Let T\textunderscore{}i=U\textunderscore{}\{i-1/2\}∩U\textunderscore{}\{i+1/2\}=S[x\textunderscore{}i,x\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}], and let \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t be the union of the T\textunderscore{}i. It is the smooth locus of Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t. If S=Spec(V) is a trait and t is a uniformizer of V, then \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t is the Néron model of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m constructed by Raynaud.

1.3. We define on Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t the structure of a commutative group scheme. Multiplication sends T\textunderscore{}i×T\textunderscore{}j to T\textunderscore{}\{i+j\} and is given by x\textunderscore{}\{i+j\}(a·b)=x\textunderscore{}i(a)x\textunderscore{}j(b). The intersection of distinct charts is the inverse image of S[1/t]; over S[1/t] the barred model equals \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m and this law is ordinary multiplication.

Multiplication extends to an action \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t×Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t→Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t, carrying T\textunderscore{}i×U\textunderscore{}j to U\textunderscore{}\{i+j\}, with

x\textunderscore{}\{i+j-1/2\}(a·b)=x\textunderscore{}i(a)x\textunderscore{}\{j-1/2\}(b), y\textunderscore{}\{i+j+1/2\}(a·b)=x\textunderscore{}i(a)\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}y\textunderscore{}\{j+1/2\}(b).

Inversion extends to Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t and carries U\textunderscore{}j to U\textunderscore{}\{-j\}, with

x\textunderscore{}\{-j-1/2\}(a\textasciicircum{}\{-1\})=y\textunderscore{}\{j+1/2\}(a), y\textunderscore{}\{-j+1/2\}(a\textasciicircum{}\{-1\})=x\textunderscore{}\{j-1/2\}(a).

The identity component T\textunderscore{}0 of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t is canonically iso-

1.4. If u∈Γ(S,\ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}\textunderscore{}S)\textasciicircum{}* and m≥1, define [u]:Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t→Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{ut\textasciicircum{}m\}. On the indicated charts its coordinates are

x\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{(ut\textasciicircum{}m)\}∘[u]=x\textunderscore{}\{mi+k\}\textasciicircum{}\{(t)\}t\textasciicircum{}k u\textasciicircum{}\{-i\}, y\textunderscore{}i\textasciicircum{}\{(ut\textasciicircum{}m)\}∘[u]=y\textunderscore{}\{mi-k\}\textasciicircum{}\{(t)\}t\textasciicircum{}k u\textasciicircum{}i,     0≤k<m.

For t=0 this contracts each T\textunderscore{}i with i not congruent to 0 modulo m to a point. If m=1 it is an isomorphism; in particular, the Néron model is independent of the chosen uniformizer.

1.5. Suppose t is nilpotent. Let g be a section of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t lying in T\textunderscore{}k, k≠0. Let ℤ act on Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t by (i,x)↦g\textasciicircum{}i·x.

Proposition-definition 1.6. With the notation of 1.5, the quotient Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/ℤ exists and is denoted Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ.

For |k|≥2 it is covered by the local charts U\textunderscore{}j with the additional gluing that g identifies U\textunderscore{}j with U\textunderscore{}\{j+k\}. For |k|=1 the same description holds étale-locally rather than by Zariski opens. Equivalently,

Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ=(Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}\{2ℤ\})/(ℤ/2ℤ).

Example 1.7. For t=0 and g given by x\textunderscore{}k=1 on T\textunderscore{}k, the quotient is the standard Néron k-gon.

Proposition 1.9. For nilpotent t and g∈T\textunderscore{}k(S), (Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ,+) is a generalized elliptic curve whose geometric fibers are k-gons.

1.10. Let A be Noetherian, t∈A, and g a section of T\textunderscore{}k over S=Spec(A). For every N define C\textunderscore{}N=Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ over A/(t\textasciicircum{}N). The C\textunderscore{}N define the direct-limit formal scheme C over the completion of S along Spec(A/(t)). If A is t-adically complete, the formal generalized elliptic curve is algebraizable: the formal projective curve Ĉ is algebraizable by Grothendieck existence; for k=1 the identity section determines the law; in general fppf descent reduces to g=g\textunderscore{}0\textasciicircum{}k and II 1.17 applies to the resulting covering.

The algebraization over Spec(A) is called a Tate curve and is again denoted Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ.

Proposition 1.11. The image of the nonsmoothness subscheme of the Tate curve Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ over Spec(A) is defined by t=0. It is the same image for the quotient, the formal scheme Ĉ, and the barred model.

1.12. For nilpotent t, the identity components of Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t and Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ are canonically isomorphic to \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m. Hence the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m along its identity is isomorphic to the completion of the quotient; their Lie algebras are identified with \ensuremath{\mathcal{O}}; and μ\textunderscore{}n injects into the n-torsion.

For Noetherian t-complete A, formulas (1.12.1)--(1.12.3) give the corresponding formal-group isomorphism, Lie isomorphism, and inclusion μ\textunderscore{}n↪(Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ)\textunderscore{}n. Tate curves therefore carry the canonical invariant differential dx/x.

For Noetherian t-complete A, passage to the limit yields the exact sequence

0→μ\textunderscore{}k→(Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/g\textasciicircum{}ℤ)\textunderscore{}k→\textasciicircum{}vℤ/k→0.       (1.13.3)

If x\textunderscore{}k(g)=u, the torsor v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(1) is canonically the torsor of kth roots of u:

v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(1)≃\{√[k]\{x\textunderscore{}k(g)\}\}.            (1.13.4)

Construction 1.14. If t=τ\textasciicircum{}m and g lies in T\textunderscore{}k of Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t, regard it as a section of T\textunderscore{}\{km\} of Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}τ satisfying x\textunderscore{}\{km\}\textasciicircum{}\{(τ)\}(g)=x\textunderscore{}k\textasciicircum{}\{(t)\}(g). The t-curve is the contraction of the τ-curve, and the two coincide over A[t\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]. The quotient map defines the contraction morphism. The exact sequence and root-torsor description are independent of τ and hence descend without a root hypothesis.

Construction 1.15. Over Spec(A[t\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]) there is the same exact sequence with projection v.

The torsor v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(1) is canonically the torsor of kth roots of g∈\ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t(A[t\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}])≃A[t\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]\textasciicircum{}*.

Let ℤ[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}]] be the power-series ring in q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}. Denote by q the section of T\textunderscore{}k in Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}\} for which x\textunderscore{}k(g)=1.

Definition 1.16. The k-sided Tate curve over ℤ[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}]] is Ḡ\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}\}/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ. For k=1 it is the Tate curve over ℤ[[q]]. It carries: an isomorphism Ĉ≃Ĝ\textunderscore{}m; the invariant differential dx/x; injections μ\textunderscore{}n↪C\textunderscore{}n; and an isomorphism C\textunderscore{}k≃μ\textunderscore{}k×ℤ/k.

The last isomorphism is defined by μ\textunderscore{}k and the section q\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}. For one natural pairing convention, despite the printed e\textunderscore{}k/e\textunderscore{}n mismatch,

e\textunderscore{}n((a,b),(c,d))=a·d-b·c.

2. Application: structure at infinity of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H

Theorem 2.1. The Tate-curve morphism τ:Spec(ℤ[[q]])→\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}1 identifies ℤ[[q]] with the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}1 along the section at infinity f\textunderscore{}1. Smoothness gives a coordinate t with f\textunderscore{}1 at t=0. The morphism has the form

q↦t=f(q),

and the proof continues on physical page 157.

for a formal series f (that is, τ*(t) = f(q)). We know that the image, in \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁, of the nonsmoothness subscheme of the universal curve is f₁ (IV 2.2). By 1.11, the image, in Spec(ℤ[[q]]), of the nonsmoothness subscheme of the Tate curve has equation q = 0. Since formation of this image commutes with base change, we have

(q) = (f(q))  in ℤ[[q]],

and this implies that τ is an isomorphism.

Let H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n).

Corollary 2.2. The fiber product

Spec(ℤ[[q]]) ×\textunderscore{}\{\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁\} \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H

is the spectrum of the normalization of ℤ[[q]] in the affine algebra of the finite étale scheme over ℤ[[q]][1/n, 1/q]

Isom((\ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}q/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ)\textunderscore{}n, (ℤ/n)²)/H.

Proof. This follows from the definitions and from the fact that normalization and completion commute.

Since the group scheme (\ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}q/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ)\textunderscore{}n over ℤ[[q]][1/q] was computed in 1.15, 2.2 makes it possible to describe explicitly the structure of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H at infinity.

2.3. We shall treat the case of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n (n ≥ 3), considered as an algebraic stack over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]. If the supersingular points of characteristic p | n are removed from \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, the resulting stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h is a scheme, described in modular terms in V 4.

Let C be the standard Néron n-gon over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n], endowed with its structure of generalized elliptic curve (1.9) and with the natural isomorphism

C\textunderscore{}n = μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n.

This curve defines a section at infinity

(2.3.1)  f\textunderscore{}n : Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n.

Over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]], consider the generalized elliptic curve

C′ = \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}\}/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ.

The map (1.16.3), μ\textunderscore{}n → C′\textunderscore{}n, and the section of order n “q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}” (x₁ = 1) define an isomorphism

C′\textunderscore{}n = μ\textunderscore{}n × ℤ/n.

The corresponding morphism

(2.3.2)  Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]]) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n

extends (2.3.1), and, by 1.14, the diagram

Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]])  →  \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n ↓                 ↓ Spec(ℤ[[q]])          →  \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁

is commutative.

Corollary 2.4. The morphism (2.3.2) identifies ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]] with the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n along the section at infinity f\textunderscore{}n.

Proof. This can be deduced from 2.2. It is simpler to repeat the proof of 2.1, using the facts that

(a) \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n is smooth over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n] in a neighborhood of infinity;

(b) the image on Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]]) of the nonsmooth locus of \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}\} is defined by the equation q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\} = 0.

Suppose that n ≥ 3. In this case, \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h is a scheme. The group SL(2, ℤ/n) acts on the ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-scheme \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h = M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h, and the stabilizer of the section at infinity f\textunderscore{}n is the subgroup

±U = \{ ±( 1  * ; 0  1 ) \}.

Corollary 2.5. If n ≥ 3, the completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}h along infinity is a sum of copies of Spec(ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n][[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]]), indexed by SL(2, ℤ/n)/±U.

Corollary 2.6. Let C be a generalized elliptic curve over a complete local ring A. Suppose that C is not smooth; let (t) be the ideal of A that defines the nonsmoothness subscheme. Suppose that the special fiber C\textunderscore{}s of C has k sides and that Gal(s̄/s) acts trivially on the diagram Γ(C\textunderscore{}s̄) of the irreducible components of C\textunderscore{}s̄. Then there exists u ∈ A* such that

C ≅ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/(ut\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}ℤ.

Proof. Let X\textunderscore{}v be the group scheme over ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}], an extension of ℤ/k by μ\textunderscore{}k,

0 → μ\textunderscore{}k → X\textunderscore{}v →\textasciicircum{}\{φ\} ℤ/k → 0,

such that φ\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}(1) is the μ\textunderscore{}k-torsor of kth roots of v. Let \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}v be the algebraic stack over ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}] classifying generalized elliptic curves E over a ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}]-scheme, smooth or k-sided, endowed with an isomorphism E\textunderscore{}k ≅ X\textunderscore{}v. After scalar extension to ℤ[v\textasciicircum{}\{1/k\}], X\textunderscore{}v becomes isomorphic to μ\textunderscore{}k × ℤ/k, and \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}v to \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}} of V 4.4. The map

Spec(ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}][[τ]]) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}v

defined by the curve \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}τ/(vτ\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}ℤ therefore identifies Spec(ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}][[τ]]) with the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{G}}\textunderscore{}v along a section at infinity.

Under the hypotheses of 2.6, there exists v ∈ A* such that C\textunderscore{}k is isomorphic to X\textunderscore{}v and C\textunderscore{}s to \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}τ/(vτ\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}ℤ over k(s). The curve C is therefore the pullback of the curve \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}τ/(vτ\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}ℤ over ℤ[v, v\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}][[τ]], and 2.6 follows.

2.7. Let us prove VI 6.14. With the notation of loc. cit., we reduce to the case in which V is complete with separably closed residue field. Let C be the Néron model of the elliptic curve E over the fraction field, and apply 2.6 to C. We find

C ≅ \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}t/(ut\textasciicircum{}k)\textasciicircum{}ℤ
