3. Application: expansion of a modular form as a Fourier series.

We shall show in § 4 that, after scalar extension to ℂ, the notions introduced in this section coincide with the classical notions.

Definition 3.1. The algebra of integral modular forms is the graded algebra

⊕\textunderscore{}k H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁, ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}).

In other words, an integral modular form of weight k is a rule, compatible with base change, which to C/S, an irreducible generalized elliptic curve over a scheme S, associates a section of ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\} over S.

Definition 3.2. The algebra of integral modular forms of level H ⊂ GL(2, ℤ/n) is the graded algebra

⊕\textunderscore{}k H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H, ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}).

The following lemma makes it possible, in certain cases, to consider only the \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n.

Lemma 3.3. We have

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H, ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}) ≃ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\})\textasciicircum{}H.

Proof. Since \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n] ≃ [\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n]/H] (notation as in [8], § 4), we have

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n], ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}) ≃ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∘[1/n], ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\})\textasciicircum{}H.

We conclude by the normality of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H: for a rational section of ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\} on \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}H to be defined everywhere, it is enough that its pullback to the finite covering


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0160-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0160-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0161}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 161}{D018-P0161-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 161; collected-paper page 654; original folio 303.}\par\smallskip
be so on \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n.

Theorem 3.4. The algebra of integral modular forms of level H is a finitely generated ℤ-algebra.

Proof. By 3.3, it is enough to treat the case of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, for n large. In this case \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n = M\textunderscore{}n is a projective scheme over ℤ, and ω is an ample invertible sheaf on M\textunderscore{}n. Indeed, the divisor of the section Δ of ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗12\} is a multiple of M\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞ (on \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, div(Δ) = \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}∞); it meets every irreducible component of every fiber of M\textunderscore{}n over ℤ, and is therefore ample. The theorem consequently follows from the general theorem EGA III, 2.3.4.1.

3.5. One may also consider modular forms with coefficients in algebras over ℤ. Since we are not sure of the correct definition in the general case, we shall for the most part restrict ourselves to the case of the \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, viewed as ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-stacks.

Definition 3.6. Let A be an algebra over ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]. A modular form of level n and weight k over the ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-algebra A is an element of

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} A, ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}).

3.7. Let φ be a modular form (cf. 3.6). Evaluate φ on the n-sided Tate curve \ensuremath{\mathbb{G}}\textunderscore{}m\textasciicircum{}\{q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}\}/q\textasciicircum{}ℤ over A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]]: the Tate curve defines a morphism (2.1)

τ : Spec(A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]]) → \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} A;

the pullback of ω by τ is trivialized (by the section dx/x (1.12)); hence

τ*φ = f\textunderscore{}φ(q)(dx/x)\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\},  with f\textunderscore{}φ(q) ∈ A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]].

We call f\textunderscore{}φ the Fourier-series expansion of φ. Since the curve


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0161-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0161-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0162}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 162}{D018-P0162-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 162; collected-paper page 655; original folio 304.}\par\smallskip
Tate n-gon is already defined over ℤ[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]] ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ A (the subring of A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]] formed by the formal series whose coefficients generate a finitely generated ℤ-module), we have

(3.7.1)  f\textunderscore{}φ(q) ∈ ℤ[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]] ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ A ⊂ A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]].

3.8. Let φ be a modular form (cf. 3.6). For g ∈ SL(2, ℤ/n), we denote by gφ the modular form which to a curve with level-n structure (C, α) over S associates the section

(gφ)(C, α) = φ(C, g\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}α)

of ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\} over S.

The f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\} are the expansions of φ at the cusps. If

u = ±( 1  a ; 0  1 )

and f\textunderscore{}φ = ∑ c\textunderscore{}m q\textasciicircum{}m (m ∈ (1/n)ℤ), then

(3.8.1)  f\textunderscore{}\{uφ\} = (−1)\textasciicircum{}k ∑ c\textunderscore{}m ζ\textunderscore{}n\textasciicircum{}\{amn\} q\textasciicircum{}m.

Theorem 3.9. Let φ be a modular form of level n and weight k with coefficients in the ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-algebra A.

(i) If the expansions at the cusps f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\} vanish, then φ vanishes.

(ii) If B is a subalgebra of A and the f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\} lie in B[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]], then φ has coefficients in B (it is obtained by scalar extension from a unique form over B).

Proof. If A is a ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]-module, a modular form of level n and weight k with coefficients in A is defined to be an element of

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}n, A ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζ\textunderscore{}n]\} ω\textasciicircum{}\{⊗k\}).

For A an algebra, this definition agrees with 3.6. The Fourier-series expansion f\textunderscore{}φ of such a form φ is defined as in 3.7. We have

f\textunderscore{}φ ∈ A ⊗\textunderscore{}ℤ ℤ[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]] ⊂ A[[q\textasciicircum{}\{1/n\}]].

We shall prove (i) and (ii) under the sole assumption that A and B are modules.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0162-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0162-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0163}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 163}{D018-P0163-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 163; collected-paper page 656; original folio 305.}\par\smallskip
Let us prove (i). If A is a direct limit lim→ Aᵢ, then

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, A ⊗ ωᵏ) = lim→ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, Aᵢ ⊗ ωᵏ).

It therefore suffices to treat the case in which A is a finitely generated ℤ[ζₙ]-module. If the f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\} vanish, (2.4) shows that φ vanishes on the formal completion of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ along infinity. The support of φ, disjoint from at least one point of every irreducible component of every geometric fibre of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, is therefore finite over ℤ[ζₙ]. Since \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ is Cohen–Macaulay over ℤ[ζₙ], and since A ⊗ ωᵏ is locally the inverse image of a module over ℤ[ζₙ], this is absurd if φ ≠ 0.

Let us prove (ii). Let Spec(D̂) be the sum of the formal completions of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ along the sections at infinity gfₙ (g ∈ SL(2, ℤ/n)). The expansions at the cusps define a map

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, A ⊗ ωᵏ) → A ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} D̂.

We prove (ii) by considering the following diagram, whose second row is exact because D̂ is flat over ℤ[ζₙ]:

0 → H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, B ⊗ ωᵏ) → H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, A ⊗ ωᵏ) → H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, A/B ⊗ ωᵏ) ↓                         ↓                         ↓ 0 → B ⊗ D̂ → A ⊗ D̂ → A/B ⊗ D̂.

Theorem 3.10. (i) One has

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, ωᵏ) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℚ(ζₙ) ≃ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗ ℚ, ωᵏ).

(ii) Let φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗ ℚ, ωᵏ), let fφ(q) = ∑ aᵢqⁱ (i ∈ (1/n)ℤ, aᵢ ∈ ℚ(ζₙ)) be the Fourier-series expansion of φ at fₙ, and let π be a place of ℚ(ζₙ). Then

νπ(fφ) = deg(fₙ) inf(νπ(aᵢ))

is the order of the zero (or the negative of the order of the pole) of φ at the generic point of the irreducible component of the fibre of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ at π that meets the section at infinity fₙ.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0163-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0163-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0164}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 164}{D018-P0164-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 164; collected-paper page 657; original folio 306.}\par\smallskip
From the point of view adopted here, this theorem is trivial; it follows from

Corollary 3.11. Let ∑ aᵢqⁱ be the Fourier-series expansion of φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗ ℚ, ωᵏ). The denominators of the aᵢ are bounded.

Corollary 3.12. Let π be a place of ℚ(ζₙ) of characteristic p, let pᵐ be the largest power of p dividing n, and let g be an element of SL(2, ℤ/n) whose image in SL(2, ℤ/pᵐ) lies in the upper-triangular group. Then

νπ(fφ) = νπ(f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\}).

Indeed, the cusps fₙ and gfₙ meet the same irreducible component in characteristic p.

Corollary 3.13. If the expansion at one cusp of φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗ ℚ, ωᵏ) has coefficients in ℤ[ζₙ][1/n], then the expansions at the other cusps have the same property.

3.14. Let φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, ωᵏ). If π is a place of ℚ(ζₙ) of characteristic p and the first coefficients of the Fourier-series expansion of φ are divisible by π, the reduction of φ modulo π vanishes to some order at the point at infinity fₙ. Write ν(π,g,φ) for the order of the zero of gφ:

if f\textunderscore{}\{gφ\}(q) = ∑ aᵢqⁱ (i ∈ (1/n)ℤ), then aᵢ ≡ 0 (π) for ni < ν(π,g,φ).

The degree of ωᵏ on \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ can be calculated using φ. The result is already known (cf. VI 4.4). Carrying out the calculation gives:

Corollary 3.14. Let π be a place of ℚ(ζₙ) of characteristic p, let pᵐ be the largest power of p dividing n, and let φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, ωᵏ). As g runs through the set BSL of elements of SL(2, ℤ/n) whose image in SL(2, ℤ/pᵐ) lies in the upper-triangular group, one has


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0164-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0164-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0165}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 165}{D018-P0165-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 165; collected-paper page 658; original folio 307.}\par\smallskip
|SL(2, ℤ/n)|⁻¹ ∑\textunderscore{}\{g∈BSL\} ν(π,g,φ) ≤ |ℙ¹(ℤ/pᵐ)|⁻¹ k/12.

For n = 1, this result may be restated as follows.

Corollary 3.15. Let p be a prime number and let φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁, ωᵏ) be a modular form with series expansion fφ(q) = ∑ aₙqⁿ. Then

νp(fφ) = inf\{νp(aᵢ) | i ≤ k/12\}.

In other words, to verify that the coefficients of a modular form of weight k are divisible by a power of p, it suffices to verify this for those whose index is ≤ k/12.

3.16. One can also relate what happens on the various irreducible components in characteristic p. As an example, we shall treat the case of modular forms of level Γ₀(p). The same arguments apply to the study, at p, of modular forms of level Γ₀(p) ∩ Γ(n), with n prime to p. Passing to such a level would spare us the use of fractional intersection numbers, introduced on the model of VI 4.

3.17. Let φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ ℚ, ωᵏ). Such a form has two “expansions at the cusps.”

a) Evaluate φ on the Tate curve \ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q/qℤ over ℤ[[q]] ⊗ ℚ, equipped with its subgroup μp (1.16.3); one obtains

₁fφ(q)·(dx/x)ᵏ, with ₁fφ(q) ∈ ℤ[[q]] ⊗ ℚ.

b) Evaluate φ on the Tate curve

\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}\{q¹ᐟᵖ\}/qℤ over ℤ[[q¹ᐟᵖ]] ⊗ ℚ,


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0165-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0165-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0166}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 166}{D018-P0166-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 166; collected-paper page 659; original folio 308.}\par\smallskip
equipped with its subgroup isomorphic to ℤ/p generated by q. One obtains

₂fφ(q)·(dx/x)ᵏ, with ₂fφ(q) ∈ ℤ[[q¹ᐟᵖ]] ⊗ ℚ.

For ℓ ≠ p, the ℓ-adic valuations of ₁fφ and ₂fφ (the lower bound of the ℓ-adic valuations of the coefficients) coincide. We shall write ν₁(φ) and ν₂(φ), or simply ν₁ and ν₂, for the p-adic valuations of ₁fφ and ₂fφ.

3.18. We extend the involution w (V.1.3) to modular forms as follows. To an elliptic curve C/S equipped with a subgroup A ⊂ C[p], the form wφ assigns the inverse image in ωᵏ of the section φ(C/A, C[p]/A) of (ω\textunderscore{}\{(C/A)/S\})ᵏ. One has

wwφ = pᵏφ.

One checks that

₁f\textunderscore{}\{wφ\}(q) = pᵏ ₂fφ(qᵖ),

₂f\textunderscore{}\{wφ\}(q) = ₁fφ(q¹ᐟᵖ).

Consequently

(ν₁(wφ), ν₂(wφ)) = (k + ν₂(φ), ν₁(φ)),

and ν₂ − ν₁ + k/2 changes sign when one passes from φ to wφ.

3.19. We propose to estimate ν₁ − ν₂. This number does not change when φ is multiplied by an integer; we may therefore suppose that

φ ∈ H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\}, ωᵏ).

Assume that φ is not identically zero. If N₁ and N₂ are the two irreducible components of \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}p, corresponding respectively to curves (C,A) with A infinitesimal and A discrete, the divisor of φ may be written

div(φ) = D + ν₁N₁ + ν₂N₂,


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0166-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0166-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0167}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 167}{D018-P0167-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 167; collected-paper page 660; original folio 309.}\par\smallskip
where the divisor D meets N₁ and N₂ at only finitely many points. On the regular stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\}, div(p) = N₁ + N₂, whence the vanishing of the intersection numbers

(N₁ + N₂, N₁) = (N₁ + N₂, N₂) = 0.

On N₁ ≃ \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁ ⊗ \ensuremath{\mathbb{F}}p, ω has degree 1/24; therefore

(div(φ), N₁) = k/24.

Similarly, since on \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\} the degree of ω is (1/24)[\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}\textunderscore{}\{Γ₀(p)\}:\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}₁] = (p+1)/24, one has

deg(D) = (D,N₁) + (D,N₂) = (p+1)k/24.

By VI.4.9, (N₂,N₁) = (p−1)/24.

One has

(D + ν₁N₁ + ν₂N₂, N₁) = (D,N₁) + ν₁(N₁+N₂,N₁) + (ν₂−ν₁)(N₂,N₁)

= ½((D,N₁)+(D,N₂)) + ½((D,N₁)−(D,N₂)) + (ν₂−ν₁+k/2)(N₂,N₁) − (k/2)(N₂,N₁).

Substituting the expressions above gives

½·k·(p+1)/24 + ½((D,N₁)−(D,N₂)) + (ν₂−ν₁+k/2)(p−1)/24 − (k/2)·(p−1)/24 = k/24.

Thus

(ν₂−ν₁+k/2)(p−1)/24 = ½((D,N₂)−(D,N₁)).

Proposition 3.20. One has

ν₂−ν₁+k/2 = ½·24/(p−1) ((D,N₂)−(D,N₁)),

(D,N₁) + (D,N₂) = k·(p+1)/24,

and hence

|ν₂−ν₁+k/2| ≤ ½·k·(p+1)/(p−1).


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0167-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0167-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0168}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 168}{D018-P0168-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 168; collected-paper page 661; original folio 310.}\par\smallskip
In particular, if ₁fφ(q) is p-integral, then the p-adic valuation of ₁f\textunderscore{}\{wφ\}(q) is ≥ −k/(p−1).

4. Comparison with transcendental theory.

4.1. We denote by D the open unit disc contained in ℂ, by q the usual coordinate on D (the inclusion D ↪ ℂ), and by D* the punctured disc D − \{0\}.

We denote by D[q¹ᐟᵏ] the Riemann surface of q¹ᐟᵏ: it is a copy of the unit disc, whose usual coordinate is called q¹ᐟᵏ, equipped with the map u : D[q¹ᐟᵏ] → D such that

q ∘ u = (q¹ᐟᵏ)ᵏ.

4.2. A construction identical to Construction 1.1 gives an analytic space \ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q over D; translation by the section q (x₁ = 1) defines an action of ℤ on \ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q, and this action is free (because |q| < 1). Passing to the quotient gives an analytic space \ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q/qℤ over D, which is a family of generalized elliptic curves parametrized by D (the Tate curve).

The fibre of the morphism \ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q/qℤ → D* at the point q ∈ D* is the elliptic curve ℂ*/qℤ ≃ ℂ/(ℤ + (log q)/(2πi)·ℤ).

4.3. Similarly, the k-gon Tate curve over D[q¹ᐟᵏ] is the quotient

\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}\{q¹ᐟᵏ\}/qℤ.

4.4. Constructions 4.2 and 1.15 are compatible in the following sense. There is a coherent system of isomorphisms of analytic spaces

(\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q/qℤ, analytic, restricted to Spec(ℂ[[q]]/(qᴺ)) ⊂ D) ≃ ((\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}q/qℤ, algebraic) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[[q]]\} ℂ[[q]]/(qᴺ))\textasciicircum{}\{an\}.


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0168-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0168-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0169}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 169}{D018-P0169-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 169; collected-paper page 662; original folio 311.}\par\smallskip
Similarly, there are isomorphisms of analytic spaces over

ℂ[[q¹ᐟᵏ]]/(qᴺ):

(\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}\{q¹ᐟᵏ\}/qℤ, analytic, restricted to Spec(ℂ[[q¹ᐟᵏ]]/(qᴺ)) ⊂ D[q¹ᐟᵏ]) ≃ ((\ensuremath{\overline{\mathbb{G}}}ₘ\textasciicircum{}\{q¹ᐟᵏ\}/qℤ, algebraic) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[[q¹ᐟᵏ]]\} ℂ[[q¹ᐟᵏ]]/(qᴺ))ᵃⁿ;

these isomorphisms respect the structures 1.16 on the two sides.

4.5. Classically, a modular form of weight k and level n is a holomorphic function f on the Poincaré half-plane

X = \{z | Im(z) > 0\}

satisfying the following conditions.

(A) If (a b; c d) ∈ SL(2, ℤ) is congruent to (1 0; 0 1) mod n,

f(z) = (cz+d)⁻ᵏ f((az+b)/(cz+d)).

This condition implies that, for γ ∈ SL(2, ℤ/n), the image of γ₀ = (a b; c d) ∈ SL(2, ℤ), the function

f|γ₀ : z ↦ (cz+d)⁻ᵏ f((az+b)/(cz+d))

depends only on γ, not on the choice of γ₀. It is denoted f|γ.

(B) For every γ ∈ SL(2, ℤ/n),

(f|γ)(z) remains bounded as Im(z) → ∞

(it is equivalent, and more honest, to require f|γ to have polynomial growth in vertical strips as Im(z) → ∞).

Such a function is periodic with period n and therefore has a Fourier-series expansion

f(z) = ∑ aₘ e\textasciicircum{}(2πimz)    (m ∈ (1/n)ℤ, m ≥ 0).


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0169-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0169-A05.}
\end{flushright}
\par\medskip
\hypertarget{D018-P0170}{}
\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 170}{D018-P0170-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 170; collected-paper page 663; original folio 312.}\par\smallskip
Construction 4.6. Modular forms of weight k and level n are identified with the sections

H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ, ω⊗ᵏ) = H⁰(\ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ, ω⊗ᵏ) ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ.

Proof. Suppose first that n ≥ 3. In that case, \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ = Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ is a scheme. The analytic space underlying Mₙ° ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ is

X/Ker(SL(2, ℤ) → SL(2, ℤ/n)),

and the holomorphic sections of ω⊗ᵏ on this space are identified with the holomorphic functions on X satisfying 4.5 (A). The analytic space underlying Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ is obtained from it by adding finitely many points. A holomorphic section of ω⊗ᵏ on Mₙ° ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ extends to Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ if and only if it satisfies (B). By GAGA, these sections are the algebraic sections of ω⊗ᵏ on Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ.

The case of arbitrary n is reduced to the preceding one by passing to the invariants under

Ker(SL(2, ℤ/3n) → SL(2, ℤ/n))

in the space of modular forms of weight 3n. One could also invoke GAGA for the algebraic stack \ensuremath{\mathcal{M}}ₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ.

4.7. For n ≥ 3, (Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ)ᵃⁿ is also the analytic space classifying generalized elliptic curves C/S over analytic spaces S, equipped with a level-n structure α such that det(α) = 1. From this point of view, a modular form f is a rule that assigns to every such curve a section of ω⊗ᵏ over S. The Fourier-series expansion is then obtained by evaluating the form on the Tate curve (4.3) (and multiplying the result by a power of 2πi depending on the normalizations chosen). For φ ∈ H⁰(Mₙ ⊗\textunderscore{}\{ℤ[ζₙ]\} ℂ, ω⊗ᵏ), compatibility 4.4 shows that this expansion coincides with the expansion defined purely algebraically in § 3.

4.8. From this point of view, the results of § 3 give arithmetic properties of the coefficients of the Fourier-series expansions at the various cusps of the functions on X satisfying 2.5 (A) and (B). For example:

- The graded algebra of modular forms of level n (2.5) is the tensor product with ℂ over ℤ[ζₙ] of the finite-type ℤ[ζₙ]-subalgebra consisting of those


\begin{flushright}
\includegraphics[width=.34\linewidth,height=.24\textheight,keepaspectratio]{\detokenize{assets/presentation_derivatives/P0170-A05.png}}
\\{\color{LocatorGray}\scriptsize Authority visual fallback P0170-A05.}
\end{flushright}

\pdfbookmark[2]{Authority page 171}{D018-P0171-bookmark}
\noindent{\color{LocatorGray}\footnotesize Authority page 171; collected-paper page 664; original folio 313.}\par\smallskip
for which the Fourier-series expansions of the f|γ have coefficients in ℤ[ζₙ].

- If the expansion of f has coefficients in ℤ[ζₙ][1/n], that of f|γ has the same property.
